above, for any linear subspace
S\subseteq U
,

1\leq\dim(S)\leq\dim(U)
, we have the useful identities

\displaystyle\sigma_{\max}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})=\max_{x\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert Tx\right\rVert}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert},

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sigma_{\dim(S)}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})=\min_{x\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert Tx\right\rVert}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert},

(17)

where
{\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}}
is the operator
T
restricted to
S
. If
\dim(S)=0
,
recall that
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})=0
by convention.

Remark 2.23
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(T)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}v_{i}u_{i}^{\top}
,
r=\mathrm{rank}(T)
, be the
SVD of
T
and let
\mathbf{U}=[u_{1},\dots,u_{r}]
. For
i\in[\dim(U)]
, a
canonical choice for the subspace attaining the minimum
in (
15
) is
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i})+\ker(T)
for
i\in[\mathrm{rank}(T)]
, and
\ker(T)
for
i>\mathrm{rank}(T)
. For
i\in[\mathrm{rank}(T)]
, using
that
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i})\subseteq\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T))
and
\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T))\perp\ker(T)
, this can be
verified as follows:

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i})+\ker(T)}})

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i}),z\in\ker(T)\\
w+z\neq\mathbf{0}_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\lVert T(w+z)\right\rVert}{\left\lVert w+z\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i}),z\in\ker(T)\\
w+z\neq\mathbf{0}_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\lVert Tw\right\rVert}{\sqrt{\left\lVert w\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert z\right\rVert^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i})\\
w\neq\mathbf{0}_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\lVert Tw\right\rVert}{\left\lVert w\right\rVert}=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{R}^{r-i+1}\\
x\neq\mathbf{0}_{r-i+1}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\lVert T(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i}x)\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{U}_{\geq i}x\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{R}^{r-i+1}\\
x\neq\mathbf{0}_{r-i+1}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{j=r-i+1}^{r}\sigma_{j}^{2}x_{j}^{2}}}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert}=\sigma_{r-i+1},

where the second to last equality uses the orthonormality of
v_{1},\dots,v_{r}\in V
and
u_{1},\dots,u_{r}\in U
, and the last equality uses the
non-increasing order of
\sigma
. Note further that
\dim(\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i})+\ker(T))=\dim(\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\geq i}))+\dim(\ker(T))=(\mathrm{rank}(T)-i+1)+\dim(U)-\mathrm{rank}(T)=\dim(U)-i+1
, and hence the subspace has the correct
dimension. For
i\in[\dim(U)]\setminus[\mathrm{rank}(T)]
, one trivially has

\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\ker(T)}})=0
and
\dim(\ker(T))=\dim(U)-\mathrm{rank}(T)\geq\dim(U)-i+1
, as needed.

Similarly, a canonical choice for the subspace attaining the maximum
in (
16
) is
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{U}_{\leq i})
if
i\in[\mathrm{rank}(T)]
, and

U
if
i>\mathrm{rank}(T)
(recalling that
\sigma_{i}(T)=0
for
i>\mathrm{rank}(T)
).

2.3.1
Approximate Singular Subspaces

In this subsection, we define approximate singular subspaces for general
linear operators and collect their main properties, which will be crucial for
the analysis and implementation of our IPM.

Definition 2.24

(Approximate Singular Subspace)
.

Let
T\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a linear operator. A linear subspace
S\subseteq X
is a
\varrho
-approximate singular subspace for
T
, for
\varrho\geq 1
,
if
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})\leq\varrho\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)+1}(T)
.

Remark 2.25

(Trivial Subspace)
.

The trivial subspace
S\coloneqq\{\mathbf{0}\}\subseteq X
is always

1
-approximate singular subspace for
T
as
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})=0=\sigma_{\dim(X)+1}(T)=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)+1}(T)
by convention.

When computing approximate singular subspaces, we will use the following
lemma to relate approximate singular subspaces of an operator to those of its
associated matrix. The proof is given in
Section

A.1
.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
T:X\rightarrow Y
,
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n},Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a linear operator and let
\bar{T}=\mathcal{M}(T)
be its associated matrix. Then, for a linear subspace
S
satisfying
\ker(\bar{T})\subseteq S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the following holds:

1.

\ker(T)\subseteq S\cap X=\Pi_{X}(S)
and
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\cap X}})=\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\bar{T}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})
.

2.

S
is a
\varrho
-approximate singular subspace for
\bar{T}
of
dimension
d\geq 0
if and only if
S\cap X
is a
\varrho
-approximate
singular subspace for
T
of dimension
d-\dim(X^{\perp})
.

Moreover, for any
\tau\geq 0
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(T,\tau\right)+\dim(X^{\perp})={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{T},\tau\right)
.

Our definition of an approximate singular subspace
S
for
T
of dimension

d
corresponds to
S
be an approximate minimizer
of (
15
), that is

\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})\leq\varrho\min_{C\subseteq X,\dim(C)=d}\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{C}})=\varrho\sigma_{\dim(X)-d+1}(T).

Given this, one may ask whether there is a
relationship between
S
and maximizers for the complementary
program (
16
), that is,

\max_{D\subseteq\dim(D)=\dim(X)-d}\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{D}})=\sigma_{\dim(X)-d}.

The following lemma shows that in fact the orthogonal complement of
S

inside
X
is indeed an approximate maximizer of (
16
), under the condition that
there is a gap between the singular values
\sigma_{\dim(X)-d}(T)
and

\sigma_{\dim(X)-d+1}(T)
. This will be important for the error analysis
of subspace LLS steps defined in
Section

6.1
. The proof is given in
Section

A.1
.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
T\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a linear operator. Let
S\subseteq X

be a
\varrho
-approximate subspace with
0\leq\dim(S)<\dim(X)
for
T
. Then, for
\bar{S}=X\cap S^{\perp}
, we have that

\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\bar{S}}})^{2}\geq\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}-\varrho^{2}\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)+1}(T)^{2}.

2.4
Preliminaries on Interior-Point Methods

In this section, we recall standard properties of the central path and IPM that will be required for
our algorithm.
To ensure that the central path is well-defined, we assume that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{D}
admit strictly feasible solutions, i.e., the sets

\mathcal{P}_{++}
and
\mathcal{D}_{++}
are both nonempty. We recall the notation
z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)=(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu))
to denote the central path point at
\mu
as in definition (
1
).

Given
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{D}
, we recall that the normalized duality gap is defined as
\overline{\mu}(z)\coloneqq\frac{\left\langle x,s\right\rangle}{n}
. The following identity is useful in comparing duality gaps.

Proposition 2.28
.

Given
x,x^{\prime}\in W+d
,
s,s^{\prime}\in W^{\perp}+c
, we have that

\left\langle x,s\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\prime},s^{\prime}\right\rangle=\left\langle x,s^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\prime},s\right\rangle.

In particular, if
\left\langle x^{\prime},s^{\prime}\right\rangle=0
, then

\left\langle x,s\right\rangle=\left\langle x,s^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\prime},s\right\rangle.

Proof.

Since
x-x^{\prime}\in W
and
s-s^{\prime}\in W^{\perp}
, we have that

0=\left\langle x-x^{\prime},s-s^{\prime}\right\rangle\Leftrightarrow\left\langle x,s\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\prime},s^{\prime}\right\rangle=\left\langle x,s^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\prime},s\right\rangle.

∎

The next proposition shows that the normalized duality gap is a linear
function for convex combinations of points.

Proposition 2.29

(Linearity duality gap)
.

For
x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(k)}\in W+d
,
s^{(1)},\dots,

s^{(k)}\in W^{\perp}+c
forming the sequence
z^{(1)}=(x^{(1)},s^{(1)}),\dots,z^{(k)}=(x^{(k)},s^{(k)})
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}=1
, we have that

\overline{\mu}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}z^{(i)}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\overline{\mu}(z^{(i)})\,.

Proof.

Using that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}=1
and the orthogonality of
x^{(i)}-d\in W
and
s^{(i)}-c\in W^{\perp}
for all
i\in[k]
we first get

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}x^{(i)},\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}s^{(i)}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(x^{(i)}-d)+d,\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(s^{(i)}-c)+c\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle d,c\right\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(\left\langle x^{(i)}-d,c\right\rangle+\left\langle d,s^{(i)}-c\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(\left\langle d,c\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{(i)}-d,c\right\rangle+\left\langle d,s^{(i)}-c\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}\left\langle x^{(i)},s^{(i)}\right\rangle.

Division by
n
yields the respective normalized duality gap.
∎

A key property of the central path is
‘near monotonicity’
,
formulated in the following lemma, see
[
66
, Lemma 16]
.

Lemma 2.30
.

For the central path points at
0<\mu^{\prime}\leq\mu
,
we have

\left\lVert\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)}+\frac{s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})}{s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq n\,.

In
Lemma

2.43
, we also present an
\ell_{1}
-variant of this bound.

Central Path Neighborhoods

The neighborhoods
\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
and
\mathcal{N}^{-\infty}(\theta)
introduced in (
2
) and (
3
) comprise the points
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{D}
such that the
centrality error
, i.e., the norm of the vector
\frac{xs}{\overline{\mu}(z)}-\mathbf{1}
, is bounded. They use of the
\ell_{2}
-norm and the
\ell_{\infty}
-seminorm
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{{-\infty}}\coloneqq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\max(0,-u_{i})
, respectively.

We will often use the following proposition which is immediate from the definition of
{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
.

Proposition 2.31
.

Let
z=(x,s)\in{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
for
\beta\in(0,1)
, and
\mu=\overline{\mu}(z)
. Then for each
i\in[n]

(1-\beta)\mu\leq x_{i}s_{i}\leq(1+\beta)\mu\,.

Proof.

By definition of
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
we have for all
i\in[n]
that

|\frac{x_{i}s_{i}}{\mu}-1|\leq\left\lVert\frac{xs}{\mu}-\mathbf{1}\right\rVert\leq\beta
and so
(1-\beta)\mu\leq x_{i}s_{i}\leq(1+\beta)\mu
.
∎

The following proposition gives a bound on the distance between a point
z\in{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
in the
\beta
-neighborhood and the corresponding central path point with the same normalized duality gap
z(\mu)
for
\mu=\overline{\mu}(z)
. See e.g.,
[
28
, Lemma 5.4]
and
[
47
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Proposition 2.32
.

Let
z=(x,s)\in{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
for
\beta\in(0,1/4]
and
\mu=\overline{\mu}(z)
, and
consider the central path point

z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)=(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu))
. For each
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\frac{x_{i}}{1+2\beta}\leq\frac{1-2\beta}{1-\beta}\mathinner{}x_{i}

\displaystyle\leq x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{i}(\mu)\leq\frac{x_{i}}{1-\beta}\,,\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle\frac{s_{i}}{1+2\beta}\leq\frac{1-2\beta}{1-\beta}\mathinner{}s_{i}

\displaystyle\leq s^{\mathrm{cp}}_{i}(\mu)\leq\frac{s_{i}}{1-\beta}\,.

We will need the following lemma regarding the near-optimality of the choice

\overline{\mu}(z)
as
\left\langle x,s\right\rangle/n
for a point
z=(x,s)
with respect to minimizing centrality error.

Lemma 2.33

(
[
47
, Lemma 4.4]
)
.

For
\beta\in(0,1/4]
, let

z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{P}_{++}\times\mathcal{D}_{++}
and
\mu^{\prime}>0
satisfy

\left\lVert xs/\mu^{\prime}-\mathbf{1}\right\rVert\leq\beta
. Then,

(1-\beta/\sqrt{n})\mu^{\prime}\leq\overline{\mu}(z)\leq(1+\beta/\sqrt{n})\mu\,\quad\mbox{and}\quad z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/(1-\beta))\,.

The next lemma relates a point in the wide neighborhood to the corresponding central path point.

Lemma 2.34
.

Let
z=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}^{-\infty}(\theta)
,
\theta\in[0,1)
. Then for
\mu=\overline{\mu}(z)
, and the corresponding central path point
z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)=(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu))
, we have that

\frac{1}{2n}x^{\prime}\leq x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\leq\frac{2n}{1-\theta}x^{\prime}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{1}{2n}s^{\prime}\leq s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\leq\frac{2n}{1-\theta}s^{\prime}\,.

Proof.

We only prove the inequalities on
x^{\prime}
; the proof of the inequalities on
s^{\prime}
is symmetric. Let
(x,s)\coloneqq(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu))
. Using Proposition
2.28
, for
i\in[n]
we have that

\frac{x^{\prime}_{i}}{x_{i}}=\frac{x^{\prime}_{i}s_{i}}{\mu}\leq\frac{1}{\mu}(\left\langle x^{\prime},s\right\rangle+\left\langle x,s^{\prime}\right\rangle)=\frac{1}{\mu}(\left\langle x^{\prime},s^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle x,s\right\rangle)=2n.

This proves the first inequality; note that this part does not use
z\in\mathcal{N}^{-\infty}(\theta)
, but only that
z\in\mathcal{P}_{++}\times\mathcal{D}_{++}
.
For the second inequality,
z\in\mathcal{N}^{-\infty}(\theta)
by definition implies

\frac{x_{i}}{x^{\prime}_{i}}\leq\frac{x_{i}s^{\prime}_{i}}{\mu(1-\theta)}\leq\frac{1}{\mu(1-\theta)}(\left\langle x^{\prime},s^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle x,s\right\rangle)=\frac{2n}{1-\theta},

as needed.
∎

2.5
The Central Path Limit

Assume that the polytope

\mathcal{P}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:x\in W+d,x\geq\mathbf{0}\}
has a strictly positive solution and is bounded. Then, the
analytic center
of
\mathcal{P}
is the point

\tilde{x}\coloneqq\arg\max_{x\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log x_{i}\,.

By Lagrangian duality, there exists a vector
\tilde{v}\in W^{\perp}
such that
\tilde{x}\tilde{v}=\mathbf{1}_{n}
.
The limit point
z^{\star}=(x^{\star},s^{\star})
of the central path corresponds to the analytic centers of the primal and dual optimal faces. Namely, assume
\mathcal{P}_{++},\mathcal{D}_{++}\neq\emptyset
, and let
(B^{\star},N^{\star})
denote the optimal partition, i.e.,
x\in\mathcal{P}
is optimal if and only if
\operatorname{supp}(x)\subseteq B^{\star}
and
s\in\mathcal{D}
is optimal if and only if
\operatorname{supp}(y)\subseteq N^{\star}
. Let

\mathcal{P}^{\star}\coloneqq\mathcal{P}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{B^{\star}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{D}^{\star}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{N^{\star}}

denote the set of primal and dual optimal solutions. Then,
\pi_{B^{\star}}(\mathcal{P}^{\star})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{B^{\star}}
and
\pi_{N^{\star}}(\mathcal{D}^{\star})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N^{\star}}
are the projections of the optimal sets to the respective coordinate sets, and the following holds; see e.g.,
[
53
, Theorem I.30]

Theorem 2.35
.

Assume
\mathcal{P}_{++},\mathcal{D}_{++}\neq\emptyset
. Then, the optimal partition
(B^{\star},N^{\star})
and the limit
(x^{\star},s^{\star})=\lim_{\mu\searrow 0}(x(\mu),s(\mu))
exist, and
x^{\star}_{B^{\star}}
is the analytic center of
\pi_{B^{\star}}(\mathcal{P}^{\star})
, and
s^{\star}_{N^{\star}}
is the analytic center of
\pi_{N^{\star}}(\mathcal{D}^{\star})
.

Consider now the output in
Theorem

1.4
in the case
\mu_{1}=0
. Since
B=\operatorname{supp}(x^{1})
and
N=\operatorname{supp}(s^{1})
form a partition of
[n]
, it follows that the solutions
(x^{1},s^{1})
are primal and dual optimal, and
B=B^{\star}
,
N=N^{\star}
. The additional output
(v^{1},w^{1})
in
Theorem

1.4
satisfying

\left\lVert(x^{1}_{B}v^{1}_{B},s^{1}_{N}w^{1}_{N})-\mathbf{1}\right\rVert\leq\beta

provides Lagrange certificates that the solution
(x^{1},s^{1})
is close to
(x^{\star},s^{\star})
. Namely,
v^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{B}
certifies that
x^{1}_{B}
is multiplicatively near the analytic center of
\pi_{B}(\mathcal{P}^{\star})
, and
w^{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
is analogously a certificate for
s^{1}_{N}
and
\pi_{N}(\mathcal{D}^{\star})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
(see
Proposition

2.32
).

2.6
Predictor-Corrector Methods

Given
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{P}_{++}\times\mathcal{D}_{++}
,
the search directions commonly used in interior-point methods are
obtained as the solution
(\Delta x,\Delta s)
to the
following linear system for some
\nu\in[0,1]
.

\displaystyle\Delta x

\displaystyle\in W

(18)

\displaystyle\Delta s

\displaystyle\in W^{\perp}

(19)

\displaystyle s\Delta x+x\Delta s

\displaystyle=\nu\mu\mathbf{1}-xs

(20)

Predictor-corrector methods, such as the Mizuno–Todd–Ye
Predictor-Corrector algorithm
[
45
]
, alternate
between two types of steps. In
corrector steps
, we use
\nu=1
. This gives the
centrality
direction
, denoted as
\Delta z^{\mathrm{c}}=(\Delta x^{\mathrm{c}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{c}})
. In
predictor steps
, we use

\nu=0
. This direction is also called the
affine scaling
direction
, and will be denoted as
\Delta z^{\mathrm{a}}=(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})

throughout.

Let
z\coloneqq(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
be our current iterate. In our
algorithm, we will first apply a corrector step to get
z^{\mathrm{c}}\coloneqq z+\Delta z^{\mathrm{c}}
, which will reduce our centrality error by a factor
2
, that is,

z^{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)
, without changing the gap
\overline{\mu}(z)
. Following
this, we apply a predictor step to get
z^{+}\coloneqq z^{\mathrm{c}}+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta z^{\mathrm{a}}
, for
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\in(0,1]
, which will make progress along the
central path while maintaining that
z^{+}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
. Here we slightly
abuse notation, by letting
\Delta z^{\mathrm{a}}\coloneqq\Delta(z^{\mathrm{c}})^{\mathrm{a}}
, that is the
predictor direction computed from the recentered iterate
z^{\mathrm{c}}
.
The step-length
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}>0
will be chosen such that

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\leq\sup\{\alpha\in[0,1]:\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in[0,\alpha]:z^{\mathrm{c}}+\alpha^{\prime}\Delta z^{\mathrm{a}}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)\}.

Thus, we conclude
z^{+}=z^{\mathrm{c}}+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta z^{\mathrm{a}}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
. We
remark that the closure allows us to take a step that goes all the way to an
optimal solution. If
z^{+}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
, i.e., if we have not
arrived at an optimal solution, then
z^{+}
remains a valid iterate for the
next step.

Remark 2.36
.

In contrast to predictor-corrector methods such as
[
45
]
, ours is a ‘corrector-predictor’ method, first performing corrector steps followed by predictor steps in each iteration. While the two descriptions are equivalent, it is more convenient for the description of the final iterate, i.e., achieve the termination guarantee in Theorem
1.4
for the same
\beta
neighbourhood.

The next proposition summarizes well-known properties of predictor and corrector steps, see
e.g.
[
67
, Section 4.5.1]
.

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
for
\beta\in(0,1/6]
.

(i)

For
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
, let
\Delta z^{\mathrm{c}}
be the corrector direction at
z
. Then for
z^{\mathrm{c}}=z+\Delta z^{\mathrm{c}}
, we have
\overline{\mu}(z^{\mathrm{c}})=\overline{\mu}(z)
and
z^{\mathrm{c}}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)
.

(ii)

For the affine scaling step, we have
\overline{\mu}(z^{+})=(1-\alpha^{\mathrm{a}})\overline{\mu}(z)
and
z^{+}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
.

(iii)

The affine scaling step-length
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}
can be chosen in the range

0\leq\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\leq\max\left\{\frac{\beta}{2\sqrt{n}},1-\frac{2\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert}{\beta\overline{\mu}(z)}\right\}\,.

(iv)

After a sequence of
\left\lceil\tfrac{3\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(1/\varepsilon)\right\rceil
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, corrector and predictor steps from
z
,
assuming the affine scaling step-lengths are all at least

\tfrac{\beta}{3\sqrt{n}}
, we obtain an iterate
z^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
such that
\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\overline{\mu}(z)
.

Remark 2.38
.

By
(iii)
, an affine scaling step-length of

\tfrac{\beta}{2\sqrt{n}}
is always valid. Therefore, the assumption
in
(iv)
that the step-lengths are at least

\tfrac{\beta}{3\sqrt{n}}
is conservative. We use this conservative estimate
for purely computational reasons as
\tfrac{\beta}{2\sqrt{n}}
may be
irrational. In particular, this choice is designed to combine with the
step-length computation given by
Proposition

2.39
below.

The following proposition explicitly states that the predictor and corrector steps can be computed in strongly polynomial time, and that we can select appropriate step-lengths. The reason is simply that computing the steps amounts to solving linear systems based on the data

x,s,\mu
and
\nu
.

Proposition 2.39

(Step Formulas)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A})=m
,
W=\ker(\mathbf{A})
and
W^{\perp}=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}^{\top})
. Let
z\coloneqq(x,s)
, where
x\in W,x>\mathbf{0}
,
s\in W^{\perp},s>\mathbf{0}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, the solution to
s\Delta x+x\Delta s=t
,
\Delta x\in W
,
\Delta s\in W^{\perp}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\Delta s

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{A}\operatorname{diag}\left(x/s\right)\mathbf{A}^{\top}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}\frac{t}{s}

\displaystyle\Delta x

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{s}-\operatorname{diag}\left(x/s\right)\mathbf{A}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{A}\operatorname{diag}\left(x/s\right)\mathbf{A}^{\top}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}\frac{t}{s}.

Moreover, both the affine
scaling step
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
and corrector step
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{c}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{c}})
, corresponding to
t=\nu\overline{\mu}(z)\mathbf{1}_{n}-xs
for
\nu\in\{0,1\}
respectively, can be
computed in strongly polynomial time. Furthermore, given
\Delta x,\Delta s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\beta\in(0,1/6]
, one can in strongly polynomial time compute an affine scaling step-length
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\in(0,1]
satisfying

\max\left\{\frac{\beta}{3\sqrt{n}},1-\frac{3\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert}{\beta\overline{\mu}(z)}\right\}\leq\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\leq\max\left\{\frac{\beta}{2\sqrt{n}},1-\frac{2\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert}{\beta\overline{\mu}(z)}\right\}\,.

Proof.

It is directly verified by inspection that
s\Delta x+x\Delta s=t
and
that
\mathbf{A}\Delta x=\mathbf{0}
and
\Delta s\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}^{\top})
. Strongly polynomial computability follows since matrix inversion can be done in strongly polynomial time.

For the affine scaling step-length, note that
\lceil a\rceil
can be computed strongly polynomially, using
O(\log n)
comparisons as long as
a=\mathrm{poly}(n)
. Thus, we can compute
a=\frac{\beta}{\lceil 2\sqrt{n}\rceil}
,
r=\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert_{\infty}
and
b=1-\lceil 2\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert/r\rceil r/(\beta\overline{\mu}(z))
.
We then return
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\coloneqq\max\{a,b\}
. Since
\lceil 2\sqrt{n}\rceil\in[2\sqrt{n},3\sqrt{n}]
and
\lceil 2\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert/r\rceil r\in[2\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert,3\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert]
, we have that

\frac{\beta}{3\sqrt{n}}\leq a\leq\frac{\beta}{2\sqrt{n}}
and

1-\frac{3\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert}{\beta\overline{\mu}(z)}\leq b\leq 1-\frac{2\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\right\rVert}{\beta\overline{\mu}(z)}
. Thus,
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}

satisfies the requirement.
∎

Minimum-Norm Viewpoint

We introduce some useful notation for the algorithm, and derive the minimum-norm interpretation of the affine scaling steps.

Definition 2.40

(Normalized Iterates, Gap Vector and Subspaces)
.

For
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{P}_{++}\times\mathcal{D}_{++}
, we let

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}(z)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sqrt{\frac{xs}{\overline{\mu}(z)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,,

(21)

\displaystyle\hat{x}

\displaystyle\coloneqq x\hat{\xi}(z)^{-1}=\sqrt{\frac{x\overline{\mu}(z)}{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{s}

\displaystyle\coloneqq s\hat{\xi}(z)^{-1}=\sqrt{\frac{s\overline{\mu}(z)}{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,.

We call
\hat{\xi}(z)
the
normalized gap vector
and simply use
\hat{\xi}
when clear from the context. We call
\hat{x}
and
\hat{s}
the
normalized primal and dual iterates
, respectively. We also define the
normalized subspaces

\hat{W}\coloneqq\hat{x}^{-1}W\,\quad\mbox{and }\quad\hat{W}^{\perp}\coloneqq\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}\,.

If
z=(x,s)
falls on the central path, that is,
xs=\overline{\mu}(z)\mathbf{1}
, then
\hat{\xi}(z)=\mathbf{1}
,
\hat{x}=x
and
\hat{s}=s
. The variables
\hat{x}
and
\hat{s}
represent natural adjustments for points off the central path. The next statement is immediate from the definitions, using
\hat{x}\hat{s}=\overline{\mu}(z)\mathbf{1}
.

Proposition 2.41
.

The subspaces
\hat{x}^{-1}W
and
\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}
are orthogonal.

The following is a simple corollary of Proposition
2.31
.

Proposition 2.42
.

For
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
for
\beta\in(0,1)
, we have
\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert=\sqrt{n}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\beta}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle\leq\hat{\xi}\leq\sqrt{1+\beta}\mathbf{1}\,,

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\beta}\hat{x}\leq x\leq\sqrt{1+\beta}\hat{x}

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad\sqrt{1-\beta}\hat{s}\leq s\leq\sqrt{1+\beta}\hat{s}\,.

We will frequently use the rescaled subspaces
\hat{x}^{-1}W
and
\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}
that correspond to using the local geometry at the point
z=(x,s)
.
Throughout, we will refer to
\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}w\right\rVert
and
\left\lVert\hat{s}^{-1}w\right\rVert
as the
primal and dual local norms
of the vector
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
at the point
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{P}_{++}\times\mathcal{D}_{++}
.

Equation

20
for the predictor step (
\nu=0
) with update direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
can be written as

x^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}+x^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}=-\mathbf{1}\,,

(22)

or equivalently,

\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}+\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}=-\hat{\xi},

(23)

which serves the purpose that now
\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\in\hat{W}
and
\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}\in{\hat{W}}^{\perp}
are orthogonal vectors (Proposition
2.41
). Thus,
\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}
and
\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}
give an orthogonal decomposition of
-\hat{\xi}
. This leads to the following formulas:

\displaystyle\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=-\hat{x}\Pi_{\hat{W}}(\hat{\xi})\,,

(24)

\displaystyle\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=-\hat{s}\Pi_{{\hat{W}}^{\perp}}(\hat{\xi})\,.

Equivalently, we can see
\Delta z^{\mathrm{a}}=(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
as the
optimal solutions of the following minimum-norm problems:

\displaystyle\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=\hat{x}\argmin_{\delta\in\hat{W}}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}+\delta\right\rVert\,,

(25)

\displaystyle\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=\hat{s}\argmin_{\delta\in{\hat{W}}^{\perp}}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}+\delta\right\rVert\,.

We can rewrite these equivalently as projections in
W
and
W^{\perp}
, noting that
\hat{x}^{-1}x=\hat{s}^{-1}s=\hat{\xi}
.

\displaystyle\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\Delta x\in W}\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}(x+\Delta x)\right\rVert\,,

(26)

\displaystyle\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\Delta s\in W^{\perp}}\left\lVert\hat{s}^{-1}(s+\Delta s)\right\rVert\,.

We will require the following generic monotonicity estimate in terms of local
norms. The next lemma is an
\ell_{1}
-variant of
Lemma

2.30
, and the proof is implicit in the proof of
[
66
, Lemma
16]
.

Lemma 2.43
.

Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1)
, and
z^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{D}
. Then, we have that

\left\lVert(\hat{x}^{-1}x^{\prime},\hat{s}^{-1}s^{\prime})\right\rVert_{1}\leq\frac{n}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\left(1+\frac{\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})}{\overline{\mu}(z)}\right).

(27)

Proof.

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{x}^{-1}x^{\prime},\hat{s}^{-1}s^{\prime})\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\lVert(\hat{s}x^{\prime},\hat{x}s^{\prime})\right\rVert_{1}}{\overline{\mu}(z)}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\frac{\left\lVert(sx^{\prime},xs^{\prime})\right\rVert_{1}}{\overline{\mu}(z)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\frac{\left\langle s,x^{\prime}\right\rangle+\left\langle x,s^{\prime}\right\rangle}{\overline{\mu}(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\frac{\left\langle s,x\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\prime},s^{\prime}\right\rangle}{\overline{\mu}(z)}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\frac{n\overline{\mu}(z)+n\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})}{\overline{\mu}(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\left(1+\frac{\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})}{\overline{\mu}(z)}\right),

where the first inequality follows from
Proposition

2.42
.
∎

Step-Length Estimates

We will also need good estimates on the size on predictor steps beyond affine
scaling. Our main estimate in this regard is given below. We use the latter
part to compute the step-length associated with layered least squares steps.
Note that the algorithm outputs a step-length of
0
in case the requirements
of the step are not satisfied.

Proposition 2.44

(Step-length Estimate for General Directions)
.

Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)
,
\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
. Consider directions
\Delta x\in W
,
\Delta s\in W^{\perp}
that satisfy

\delta\coloneqq\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x\Delta s}{\mu}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\beta}{9},\quad\quad\varepsilon\coloneqq\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)}{\mu}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\beta}{9}.

(28)

Then
(x+\alpha\Delta x,s+\alpha\Delta s)\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
and
\overline{\mu}(x+\alpha\Delta x,s+\alpha\Delta s)\in[1\pm 1/8](1-\alpha)\mu
, for all
0\leq\alpha\leq 1-\frac{8\varepsilon}{\beta}
. Furthermore, given
\Delta x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\Delta s\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mu>0,\beta>0
, one can in strongly polynomial time output a step-length
\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\in[0,1]
satisfying
1-\frac{9\varepsilon}{\beta}\leq\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\leq 1-\frac{8\varepsilon}{\beta}
if
\max\{\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x\Delta s}{\mu}\right\rVert,\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)}{\mu}\right\rVert\}\leq\beta/9
and
\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}=0
otherwise.

Proof.

Let
z_{\alpha}\coloneqq(x+\alpha\Delta x,s+\alpha\Delta s)
for
0\leq\alpha\leq 1-\frac{8\varepsilon}{\beta}
,
\alpha<1
. (Note that
\alpha<1
only becomes relevant in case
\varepsilon=0
; we will discuss this case later.) We first bound
the centrality error using the estimate
(1-\alpha)\mu
for
\mu(z_{\alpha})

as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{(x+\alpha\Delta x)(s+\alpha\Delta s)}{(1-\alpha)\mu}-\mathbf{1}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{(1-\alpha)xs+\alpha(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)-\alpha(1-\alpha)\Delta x\Delta s}{(1-\alpha)\mu}-\mathbf{1}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\frac{xs}{\mu}-\mathbf{1}\right\rVert+\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)}{\mu}\right\rVert+\alpha\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x\Delta s}{\mu}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\beta/2+\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\varepsilon+\alpha\delta\leq\beta/2+\beta/8+\beta/9<\frac{3}{4}\beta\,,

where the last inequality follows since
\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\varepsilon\leq\beta/8
for
0\leq\alpha\leq 1-8\varepsilon/\beta
and
\alpha<1
(needed to
ensure the denominator
(1-\alpha)
is positive).

By
Lemma

2.33
and the above bound, we get that
\overline{\mu}(z_{\alpha})\in[1\pm\frac{3}{4}\beta/\sqrt{n}](1-\alpha)\mu\subseteq[1\pm\frac{1}{8}](1-\alpha)\mu
, and
z_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}\left(\frac{\frac{3}{4}\beta}{1-\frac{3}{4}\beta}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
, for
\beta\in(0,1/6]
. If
\varepsilon=0
, letting
\alpha\rightarrow 1^{-}
, we conclude by
continuity that
z_{1}\coloneqq(x+\Delta x,s+\Delta s)\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
and

\overline{\mu}(z_{1})=0
, as needed.

For the last part, note that the condition
\max\{\frac{\left\lVert\Delta x\Delta s\right\rVert}{\mu},\frac{\left\lVert(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)\right\rVert}{\mu}\}\leq\beta/9
can be checked in strongly polynomial time by squaring both sides. If this check fails, output
\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}=0
. Otherwise, compute
r=\left\lVert(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)\right\rVert_{\infty}
and
compute
\nu=\lceil 8\left\lVert(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)\right\rVert/r\rceil\in[8,\lceil 8\sqrt{n}\rceil]
via binary search in
O(\log n)
time, and return

\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}=1-\frac{\nu r}{\mu\beta}
. For correctness, note that

\left\lVert(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)\right\rVert\leq\nu(r/8)\leq\frac{9}{8}\left\lVert(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)\right\rVert
, and thus the desired inequalities follow recalling that
\varepsilon\coloneqq\left\lVert(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)\right\rVert/\mu
.
∎

3
Polarization of the Central Path

We now introduce the notion of polarized segments of the central path. For
0\leq\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{0}
, the central path segment between these values is denoted by

\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]\coloneqq\{z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu):\mu_{1}\leq\mu\leq\mu_{0}\}\,.

(29)

Definition 3.1

(Polarization)
.

For
\gamma\in(0,1]
and
\mu_{0}>\mu_{1}\geq 0
, we say that the segment

\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
is

\gamma
-polarized
if there exists a partition
B\cup N=[n]
such that for all
\mu\in[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
:

\displaystyle x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\geq\gamma x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})\,,\quad\forall i\in B\,,

\displaystyle s_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\geq\gamma s_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})\,,\quad\forall i\in N\,.

Remark 3.2
.

By continuity of the central path and the condition
\mu_{0}>\mu_{1}\geq 0
, we may restrict the polarization check above to
\mu\in[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
,
\mu>0
. For
i\in N
, since
s_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)=\mu/x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)
for
\mu>0
, the condition
s_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\geq\gamma s_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})
is equivalent to

\mu/x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\geq\gamma\mu_{0}/x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad x_{i}(\mu)\leq\frac{\mu}{\gamma\mu_{0}}x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0}).

(30)

Thus, the polarization condition can be stated only in terms of the primal
central path. Similarly, switching the roles of
B
and
N
, the polarization
condition can also be stated only in terms of the dual central path.

Remark 3.3
.

As stated, the notion of polarization requires an inequality to hold for all

\mu\in[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
. At the cost of losing a factor
n
however, it is in
fact sufficient to check
the polarization condition only at
\mu=\mu_{1}
. This
follows by the near-monotonicity of the central path (
Lemma

2.30
):

\frac{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)}{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})}=\frac{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)}{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})}\cdot\frac{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})}{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})}\geq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})}{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})}\,,\quad\forall i\in[n]\,,

The same is true for
s(\mu)
by a symmetric
argument.

As a direct consequence of the definition together with near-monotonicity, we
deduce the following crucial corollary:

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
,
0\leq\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{0}
, be
\gamma
-polarized
with respect to the partition
B\cup N=[n]
. Then, for all
\mu\in[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
, the following holds:

(1)

\gamma x_{i}(\mu_{0})\leq x_{i}(\mu)\leq nx_{i}(\mu_{0})
,
i\in B
.

(2)

\gamma s_{i}(\mu_{0})\leq s_{i}(\mu)\leq ns_{i}(\mu_{0})
,
i\in N
.

(3)

\frac{\mu}{n\mu_{0}}x_{i}(\mu_{0})\leq x_{i}(\mu)\leq\frac{\mu}{\gamma\mu_{0}}x_{i}(\mu_{0})
,
i\in N
.

(4)

\frac{\mu}{n\mu_{0}}s_{i}(\mu_{0})\leq s_{i}(\mu)\leq\frac{\mu}{\gamma\mu_{0}}s_{i}(\mu_{0})
,
i\in B
.

Proof.

The first inequalities in (1) and (2) are the definition of

\gamma
-polarization and the second inequalities are from
Lemma

2.30
. (3)
and (4) are equivalent to (1) and (2) using the central path relations

x(\mu_{0})s(\mu_{0})=\mu_{0}\mathbf{1}
and
x(\mu)s(\mu)=\mu\mathbf{1}
.
∎

Section
7
introduces the algorithm

2SLLS-IPM
that can traverse

\gamma
-polarized segments in

O(n^{1.5}

\log(n/\gamma))
iterations.
Theorem
1.1
follows by combining this algorithm with the following decomposition result that is the main result of this section; the proof can be found in Section
7.3
.
The stronger variant
Theorem
1.4
is proved in Section
8
using an additional amortization argument.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\Gamma:(\mu_{1},\mu_{0})\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{-\infty}(\theta)
,
\theta\in(0,1)
,
0\leq\mu_{1}<\mu_{0}\leq\infty
, be a piecewise linear curve
satisfying
\overline{\mu}\left(\Gamma(\mu)\right)=\mu
,
\forall\mu\in(\mu_{1},\mu_{0})
consisting of
T
linear segments. Then,
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
can be
decomposed into
T
segments that are

\frac{(1-\theta)^{2}}{16n^{3}}
-polarized.

Theorem

3.5
is a direct consequence of the following key lemma.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
\theta\in(0,1)
, let
[z^{(0)},z^{(1)}]\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{-\infty}(\theta)
,

\overline{\mu}(z^{(0)})>\overline{\mu}(z^{(1)})
. Then,
\mathrm{CP}[\overline{\mu}(z^{(1)}),\overline{\mu}(z^{(0)})]
is

\frac{(1-\theta)^{2}}{16n^{3}}
-polarized.

Proof of
Theorem

3.5
.

By assumption, the curve
\Gamma([\mu_{1},\mu_{0}])=\cup_{i=1}^{T}[z^{(i-1)},z^{(i)}]\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{-\infty}(\theta)
, where
\mu_{0}=\overline{\mu}(z^{(0)})>\overline{\mu}(z^{(1)})>\cdots>\overline{\mu}(z^{(T)})=\mu_{1}
. By
Lemma

3.6
,
each segment
\mathrm{CP}[\overline{\mu}(z^{(i)}),\overline{\mu}(z^{(i-1)})]
,
i\in[T]
, is therefore

\frac{(1-\theta)^{2}}{16n^{3}}
-polarized with respect to some polarization
partition
(B^{(i)},N^{(i)})
. This proves the theorem.
∎

It remains to prove
Lemma

3.6
. The proof requires the following simple technical lemma that
allows us to relate approximate centrality along lines to polarization.

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
u,v>0
,

\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\frac{(1-\alpha+\alpha u)(1-\alpha+\alpha v)}{1-\alpha+\alpha uv}=\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{\sqrt{u}+\sqrt{v}}{1+\sqrt{uv}}\right)^{2}\right\}\leq 2(u+v)\,.

(31)

Proof.

To show the equality, let
\mu\coloneqq uv
.
Note that

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\frac{(1-\alpha+\alpha u)(1-\alpha+\alpha v)}{1-\alpha+\alpha uv}

\displaystyle=\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\frac{(1-\alpha)^{2}+\alpha^{2}\mu+\alpha(1-\alpha)(u+v)}{1-\alpha+\alpha\mu}

(32)

\displaystyle=1+\min_{\alpha\in[0,1]}\left(u+v-(1+\mu)\right)\frac{\alpha(1-\alpha)}{1-\alpha+\alpha\mu}\,.

Case I:
\left(\frac{\sqrt{u}+\sqrt{v}}{1+\sqrt{uv}}\right)^{2}\geq 1
.
In this case, we need to show that the minimum of the expression is 1. It is easy to see that the condition equivalent to
u+v\geq 1+uv=1+\mu
. Thus, the minimum value of (
32
) is clearly
1
,
attained at
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.

Case II:
\left(\frac{\sqrt{u}+\sqrt{v}}{1+\sqrt{uv}}\right)^{2}<1
,
or equivalently,
u+v<1+\mu
. In this case, the minimizer
of (
32
) corresponds to the maximizer of

\frac{\alpha(1-\alpha)}{1-\alpha+\alpha\mu}
. This function takes value

0
at
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
and is strictly positive for
0<\alpha<1
.
Furthermore, the unique critical point in the interval
[0,1]
occurs at

\alpha^{*}=\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{\mu}}
, which is thus the maximizer. The minimum
value of (
32
) is therefore

1+(u+v-(1+\mu))\frac{\alpha^{*}(1-\alpha^{*})}{1-\alpha^{*}+\alpha^{*}\mu}=\frac{u+v+2\sqrt{\mu}}{(1+\sqrt{\mu})^{2}}\,,

as required.
The inequality in the statement follows easily as

\left(\frac{\sqrt{u}+\sqrt{v}}{1+\sqrt{uv}}\right)^{2}\leq\left({\sqrt{u}+\sqrt{v}}\right)^{2}=2(u+v)-(\sqrt{u}-\sqrt{v})^{2}\,.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

For
\alpha\in[0,1]
, let
z^{(\alpha)}\coloneqq(x^{(\alpha)},s^{(\alpha)})\coloneqq(1-\alpha)z^{(0)}+\alpha z^{(1)}
. By
Proposition

2.29
, we first note that the
normalized gap function
\overline{\mu}(z)
is in fact linear on
[z^{(0)},z^{(1)}]
. That is,

\mu_{\alpha}\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z^{(\alpha)})=(1-\alpha)\overline{\mu}(z^{(0)})+\alpha\overline{\mu}(z^{(1)})\,.

For any
i\in[n]
,
z^{(\alpha)}\in\mathcal{N}^{-\infty}(\theta)
implies

\displaystyle\frac{x^{(\alpha)}_{i}s^{(\alpha)}_{i}}{(1-\alpha)x^{(0)}_{i}s^{(0)}_{i}+\alpha x^{(1)}_{i}s^{(1)}_{i}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-\theta)\mu_{\alpha}}{(1-\alpha)x^{(0)}_{i}s^{(0)}_{i}+\alpha x^{(1)}_{i}s^{(1)}_{i}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-\theta)\mu_{\alpha}}{n((1-\alpha)\overline{\mu}(z^{(0)})+\alpha\overline{\mu}(z^{(1)}))}=\frac{1-\theta}{n}\,.

Note that the above expression is the same as in
Lemma

3.7
for
u=x^{(1)}_{i}/x^{(0)}_{i}
,
v=s^{(1)}_{i}/s^{(0)}_{i}
.
Since the bound is true for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
, the Lemma implies

\frac{1-\theta}{n}\leq 2\left(\frac{x^{(1)}_{i}}{x^{(0)}_{i}}+\frac{s^{(1)}_{i}}{s^{(0)}_{i}}\right)\,.

Let

B\coloneqq\left\{i\in[n]:\frac{x^{(1)}_{i}}{x^{(0)}_{i}}\geq\frac{s^{(1)}_{i}}{s^{(0)}_{i}}\right\}\,,\quad N\coloneqq[n]\setminus B\,.

Then,
x^{(1)}_{i}/x^{(0)}_{i}\geq\frac{1-\theta}{4n}
,
for all
i\in B
, and
s^{(1)}_{i}/s^{(0)}_{i}\geq\frac{1-\theta}{4n}
for all
i\in N
.

For any
\alpha\in[0,1]
and
i\in B
,

\frac{x^{(\alpha)}_{i}}{x^{(0)}_{i}}=(1-\alpha)+\alpha\frac{x^{(1)}_{i}}{x^{(0)}_{i}}\geq\min\left\{1,\frac{1-\theta}{4n}\right\}=\frac{1-\theta}{4n}.

Similarly, for
i\in N
,
s^{(\alpha)}_{i}/s^{(0)}_{i}\geq\frac{1-\theta}{4n}
.

For the central path point
z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{\alpha})=(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{\alpha}),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{\alpha}))
at
\mu_{\alpha}
,
the bounds in
Lemma

2.34
relating points in a neighborhood with central path points give

\frac{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{\alpha})}{x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})}\geq\frac{x^{(\alpha)}_{i}/(2n)}{\frac{2n}{1-\theta}x^{(0)}_{i}}\geq\frac{(1-\theta)^{2}}{16n^{3}}\,,\quad\forall i\in B\,.

By a similar argument, we also have
s_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{\alpha})/s_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})\geq\frac{(1-\theta)^{2}}{16n^{3}}
,
\forall i\in N
. Thus,
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
is

\frac{(1-\theta)^{2}}{16n^{3}}
-polarized.
∎

4
The Max Central Path

In this section, we derive key properties of the max central path and how to
use the max central path to decompose the central path into polarized
segments. In particular, we prove
1.2
,

1.5
and
Theorem

1.8
. Given
g\geq 0
, we
denote by

\begin{aligned} \mathcal{P}_{g}&\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\mathbf{A}x=b\,,\;x\geq\mathbf{0}\,,\;\left\langle c,x\right\rangle\leq v^{\star}+g\}\,,\\
\mathcal{D}_{g}&\coloneqq\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,\exists y\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\;\mathbf{A}^{\top}y+s=c\,,\;s\geq\mathbf{0}\,,\;\left\langle b,y\right\rangle\geq v^{\star}-g\}\end{aligned}\,.

We will also use the subspace formulation (
11
) with
d\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such
\mathbf{A}d=b
; in these terms, we can write
\mathcal{P}_{g}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,x\in W+d\,,\;x\geq 0\,,\;\left\langle c,x\right\rangle\leq v^{\star}+g\}
and
\mathcal{D}_{g}=\{s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,:\,s\in W^{\top}+c\,,\;s\geq 0\,,\;\left\langle d,c-s\right\rangle\geq v^{\star}-g\}
.
the feasible sets of the linear programs in (
4
). They correspond to the sets of the primal and dual feasible points
(x,s)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{D}
with objective value within
g
from the optimum
v^{\star}
, respectively. By the assumption that (
LP
) is feasible and bounded, it follows that
\mathcal{P}_{g}
and
\mathcal{D}_{g}
are both non-empty for any
g\geq 0
.

We recall that the duality gap of any pair
(x,(y,s))
of primal-dual feasible points of (
LP
) fulfills
\left\langle c,x\right\rangle-\left\langle b,y\right\rangle=\left\langle x,s\right\rangle
. In particular, we have
\left\langle x,s^{\star}\right\rangle=\left\langle c,x\right\rangle-v^{\star}
and
\left\langle x^{\star},s\right\rangle=v^{\star}-\left\langle b,y\right\rangle
. Thus, the two sets
\mathcal{P}_{g}
and
\mathcal{D}_{g}
are equivalently given by

\mathcal{P}_{g}=\{x\in\mathcal{P}:\left\langle x,s^{\star}\right\rangle\leq g\}\,,\quad\mathcal{D}_{g}=\{s\in\mathcal{D}:\left\langle x^{\star},s\right\rangle\leq g\}\,.

These expressions are in fact independent of the choice of optimal solutions
(x^{\star},s^{\star})
. The following claim is immediate by our assumption that
\mathcal{P}_{++}
and
\mathcal{D}_{++}
are non-empty.

Proposition 4.1
.

For all
g\geq 0
, the sets
\mathcal{P}_{g}
and
\mathcal{D}_{g}
are bounded.

Proof.

We restrict to the proof of the boundedness of
\mathcal{P}_{g}
, since the proof is analogous for
\mathcal{D}_{g}
. Let
s^{\circ}\in\mathcal{D}_{++}
be a strictly feasible point of the dual, and
x\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
. By
Proposition

2.28
, we have

\left\langle x,s^{\star}\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\star},s^{\circ}\right\rangle=\left\langle x,s^{\circ}\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\star},s^{\star}\right\rangle\,.

Since
\left\langle x^{\star},s^{\star}\right\rangle=0
, we deduce that
\left\langle x,s^{\circ}\right\rangle\leq g+\left\langle x^{\star},s^{\circ}\right\rangle
. As
s^{\circ}>0
, this implies that
x_{i}\leq(g+\left\langle x^{\star},s^{\circ}\right\rangle)/s^{\circ}_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
.
∎

We denote by
\mathrm{MCP}=\{z^{\mathfrak{m}}(g):g\geq 0\}
the whole max central path.
The max central path point
z^{\mathfrak{m}}(g)=(x^{\mathfrak{m}}(g),s^{\mathfrak{m}}(g))
is the entry-wise maximum of the set
\mathcal{P}_{g}\times\mathcal{D}_{g}
.

While the points of the max central path are not feasible in general, the following theorem shows that the max central path shares important similarities with the central path:

Theorem 4.2

(Centrality of the max central path)
.

For all
g\geq 0
, we have that

g\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)\leq 2g\,\quad\forall i\in[n]\,.

Proof.

We first prove the upper bound. For
i\in[n]
, let
x^{(i)}\in\argmax\{x_{i}:x\in\mathcal{P}_{g}\}
and
s^{(i)}\in\argmax\{s_{i}:s\in\mathcal{D}_{g}\}
. Note
that
x^{(i)},s^{(i)}
exist by
Proposition

4.1
.
Then,

x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)=x^{(i)}_{i}s^{(i)}_{i}\leq\left\langle x^{(i)},s^{(i)}\right\rangle=\left\langle x^{(i)},s^{\star}\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\star},s^{(i)}\right\rangle\leq 2g\,,

where the last equality follows from
Proposition

2.28
.
We now prove the lower bound. We assume
g>0
, since the statement is
trivial otherwise.

Note that the dual program of
\max\{x_{i}:x\in W+d,x\geq 0,\left\langle x,s^{\star}\right\rangle\leq g\}
can be expressed as

\min\left\{\alpha g+\left\langle u,x^{\star}\right\rangle:\alpha s^{\star}+u\geq e^{i},u\in W^{\perp},\alpha\geq 0\right\}\,,

using that
\left\langle u,x^{\star}\right\rangle=\left\langle u,d\right\rangle
since
d-x^{\star}\in W
,
u\in W^{\perp}
.
Similarly, the dual program of
\max\{s_{i}:s\in W^{\perp}+c,s\geq 0,\left\langle s,x^{\star}\right\rangle\leq g\}
can be expressed as

\min\left\{\beta g+\left\langle v,s^{\star}\right\rangle:\beta x^{\star}+v\geq e^{i},v\in W,\beta\geq 0\right\}\,.

Let us pick optimal
(\alpha,u)
and
(\beta,v)
to these two programs. The product of the objective values is thus equal to
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
; the proof is complete by showing a lower bound
g
.

We first claim that

\left\langle u,x^{\star}\right\rangle\geq 0\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\left\langle v,s^{\star}\right\rangle\geq 0\,.

(33)

By symmetry, it suffices to prove the first claim. Recall
x^{\star}\geq 0
; we show that whenever
x^{\star}_{j}>0
then we must have
u_{j}\geq 0
. This follows since by complementary,
s^{\star}_{j}=0
, and that we have the constraint
\alpha s^{\star}_{j}+u_{j}\geq e^{i}_{j}
.

Next, note that the constraints in the two programs imply

1=\left\langle e^{i},e^{i}\right\rangle\leq\left\langle\alpha s^{\star}+u,\beta x^{\star}+v\right\rangle=\alpha\left\langle v,s^{\star}\right\rangle+\beta\left\langle u,x^{\star}\right\rangle\,.

(34)

Now, the product of the objective values can be written as

\displaystyle x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)

\displaystyle=(\alpha g+\left\langle u,x^{\star}\right\rangle)(\beta g+\left\langle v,s^{\star}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle=\alpha\beta g^{2}+g\left(\alpha\left\langle v,s^{\star}\right\rangle+\beta\left\langle u,x^{\star}\right\rangle\right)+\left\langle u,x^{\star}\right\rangle\cdot\left\langle v,s^{\star}\right\rangle\geq g\,,

using (
33
) and (
34
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Given the above, we are ready to show

See
1.2

Proof.

Recall that
z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)=(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu))
with

\left\langle x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\right\rangle=\left\langle x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\star}\right\rangle+\left\langle x^{\star},s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\right\rangle=n\mu

using
Proposition

2.28
. Therefore,
x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\in\mathcal{P}_{n\mu}
and
s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\in\mathcal{D}_{n\mu}
. By definition of the max central path,
z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)=(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu),s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu))\leq(x^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu),s^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu))=z^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)
. For the second inequality,
note that

\displaystyle x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)}\geq\frac{\mu}{s^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)}=\frac{\mu}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)s^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)}x^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Thm. }\ref{thm:mcp-central}}}{{\geq}}\frac{\mu}{2n\mu}\mathbf{1}x^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)=\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)}{2n}\,.

By a symmetric argument,
s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)\geq s^{\mathfrak{m}}(n\mu)/2n
.
∎

4.1
The Shadow Vertex Simplex Rule

Given a pointed polyhedron
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
(that means that it has at least one vertex) and two objectives
c^{(1)},c^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\max_{x\in P}\left\langle c^{(1)},x\right\rangle,\ \max_{x\in P}\left\langle c^{(2)},x\right\rangle

<\infty
, we recall that the shadow vertex simplex rule consists in pivoting over vertices of
P
maximizing the objectives
(1-\lambda)c^{(1)}+\lambda c^{(2)}
as
\lambda
goes from
0
to
1
. More formally, a sequence of vertices
v^{(1)},\dots,v^{(k)}\in P
is a
(c^{(1)},c^{(2)})
-shadow vertex path on
P
if

•

[v^{(i)},v^{({i+1})}]
is an edge
of
P
,
\forall i\in[k-1]
,

•

\left\langle c^{(2)},v^{(i)}\right\rangle<\left\langle c^{(2)},v^{({i+1})}\right\rangle
,
\forall i\in[k-1]
, and

•

there exists
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{k-1}<\lambda_{k}=1
such that
\forall i\in[k]
,

\left\langle v^{(i)},(1-\alpha)c^{(1)}+\alpha c^{(2)}\right\rangle=\max_{x\in P}\left\langle x,(1-\alpha)c^{(1)}+\alpha c^{(2)}\right\rangle
,
\forall\alpha\in[\lambda_{i-1},\lambda_{i}]
.

To analyze shadow vertex paths further, we define the two-dimensional projection

P[c^{(1)},c^{(2)}]\coloneqq\left\{\left(\left\langle c^{(1)},x\right\rangle,\left\langle c^{(2)},x\right\rangle\right):x\in P\right\}=\left(c^{(1)},c^{(2)}\right)^{\top}\cdot P\,.

The vertices of
P[c^{(1)},c^{(2)}]
maximizing an open interval of objectives in
(1-\lambda)e^{1}+\lambda e^{2}
,
\lambda\in[0,1]
are
precisely the projections of vertices
v^{(i)}
,
i\in[k]
, on the shadow path
such that
\lambda_{i-1}<\lambda_{i}
.

We define
S_{P}(c^{(1)},c^{(2)})
as the
number of vertices of
P[c^{(1)},c^{(2)}]
maximizing an open interval of objectives
in
(1-\lambda)e^{1}+\lambda e^{2}
,
\lambda\in[0,1]
. By the preceding
observations, we have that
S_{P}(c^{(1)},c^{(2)})
is a lower bound on the number of
vertices of any
(c^{(1)},c^{(2)})
-shadow vertex path.

In the above, we restricted both starting and ending objectives
c^{(1)},c^{(2)}
to
have finite objective value on
P
. It will be useful in the sequel to extend
to the case where
P
might be unbounded in direction
c^{(2)}
.
In this case, we define the
shadow vertex path as above, with the only modification being that we let

\lambda_{k}\coloneqq\max\{\lambda\in[0,1]:\max_{x\in P}\left\langle x,(1-\lambda)c^{(1)}+\lambda c^{(2)}\right\rangle<\infty\}
, that is, the simplex path stops just before
reaching an unbounded ray for
c^{(2)}
. In this setting, note that

S_{P}(c^{(1)},c^{(2)})
is still well-defined and continues to be a lower bound on the
number of vertices on any
c^{(1)},c^{(2)}
shadow vertex path.

We are ready to prove Lemma
1.5
.
See
1.5

Proof.

We only prove part
(i)
; part
(ii)
follows analogously.
For
i\in[n]
, let
Q_{i}=P[s^{\star},e^{i}]
. We note that
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)=\max\{v_{2}:(v_{1},v_{2})\in Q_{i},v_{1}\leq g\}
.
In particular, the map
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
is a non-decreasing function of
g
. Moreover, it is easy to verify that
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
is concave.

Again by definition,
S_{\mathcal{P}}(-s^{\star},e^{i})
equals the number of vertices of

Q_{i}
maximizing an open interval of objectives in
{\mathcal{O}}\coloneqq\{-(1-\lambda)e^{1}+\lambda e^{2}:\lambda\in[0,1]\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Define
\bar{u}_{i}(g)\coloneqq\sup\{v_{2}:(g,v_{2})\in Q_{i}\}
, which is defined to
equal
-\infty
if
\{(g,v_{2})\in Q_{i}\}=\emptyset
. By
Proposition

4.1
, note that
\bar{u}_{i}(g)<\infty
for all
g\geq 0
. By
convexity of
Q_{i}
,
\bar{u}_{i}
is concave function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Let
h=\sup\{\bar{u}_{i}(g):g\geq 0\}
.

Assume
h=\infty
. By concavity,
\bar{u}_{i}
must be a
strictly increasing function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. In particular,
\bar{u}_{i}(g)=x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
. Given this, we see that the linear pieces of
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
are in one to
one correspondence with the edges of
Q_{i}
on the upper convex hull whose
projection onto the
e_{1}
-axis have positive length (i.e., excluding the
potential edge
\{(0,v_{2})\in Q_{i}\}
). Since
Q_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
, every
such edge can be uniquely associated with its left endpoint (which is always
a vertex of
Q_{i}
). It is now easy to check geometrically that the set of
such endpoints exactly corresponds to the set of vertices that are maximizers
of the objectives in an open interval of
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Assume
h<\infty
. Let
g_{h}\coloneqq\min\{g\geq 0:x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)=h\}
. It is direct
to see that
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)=\bar{u}_{i}(g)
if
g\leq g_{h}
and that
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)=h
for

g\geq g_{h}
. Furthermore,
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
is strictly increasing on
[0,g_{h}]
.
From this, it is easy to see geometrically that the number of linear pieces
of
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
is one plus the number of edges of
Q_{i}
on the upper convex hull
lying in the band
\{(v_{1},v_{2}):0\leq v_{1}\leq g\}
, where the extra linear
segment corresponds to constant segment between
g_{h}
and
\infty
. As in the
previous case, these linear segments can be uniquely identified with their
left endpoints, which correspond to vertices of
Q_{i}
. Furthermore, it is
easy to check that these correspond to vertices of
Q_{i}
maximizing an open
interval of objectives in
\mathcal{O}
.
∎

4.2
Decomposition of Straight Line Segments into Polarized Segments

In this section we prove
Theorem

1.8
. To begin, we provide some
simple consequences of the concavity of the max central path coordinates.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
g\geq 0
and
\alpha\geq 1
. Then for all
i\in[n]
we have
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(\alpha g)\leq\alpha x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
and
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(\frac{1}{\alpha}g)\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
.

Proof.

We use the concavity of
g\mapsto x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
(
1.5
) and deduce that
(1-\frac{1}{\alpha})x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(0)+\frac{1}{\alpha}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(\alpha g)\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
. The first inequality of the lemma follows from
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(0)\geq 0
. The second inequality follows from the first applied to
\tilde{g}=\frac{g}{\alpha}
.
∎

The next lemma shows that the definition of the primal straight-line complexity (Definition
1.3
) can be restricted to breakpoints
(g_{k},z_{k})=(g_{k},x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k}))
. By symmetry, the analogous statement for the dual straight-line complexity also holds.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
0\leq\underline{g}<\overline{g}
. We have

\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})=\min\begin{multlined}\big\{p\geq 1\colon\exists(g_{k})_{k\in[p+1]}\in\mathbb{R}\,,\kern 5.0pt\overline{g}=g_{1}>g_{2}>\dots>g_{p+1}=\underline{g}\,,\\
\forall k\in[p]\,,\;\forall\lambda\in[0,1]\,,(1-\lambda)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k})+\lambda x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k+1})\\
\geq(1-\theta)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}((1-\lambda)g_{k}+\lambda g_{k+1})\big\}\end{multlined}

(35)

Proof.

Let
\overline{g}=g_{1}>g_{2}>\dots>g_{p+1}=\underline{g}
. By the concavity of
g\mapsto x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
, the condition
(1-\lambda)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k})+\lambda x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k+1})\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}((1-\lambda)g_{k}+\lambda g_{k+1})
is satisfied for every
k\in[p]
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
. We deduce that
\operatorname{SLC}_{\theta,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
is less than or equal to the right-hand side of (
35
).

We now prove the converse inequality. Let
(g_{1},z_{1}),\dots,(g_{p+1},z_{p+1})
as in the right-hand side of (
5
). Up to removing or shortening segments in the sequence
\big([(g_{k},z_{k}),(g_{k+1},z_{k+1})]\big)_{k\in[p]}
, we can assume that
\overline{g}=g_{1}\geq g_{k}>g_{p+1}=\underline{g}
for every
k\in[p]
. As
z_{k}\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k})
for all
k\in[p+1]
, we have
(1-\lambda)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k})+\lambda x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k+1})\geq(1-\lambda)z_{k}+\lambda z_{k+1}\geq(1-\theta)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}((1-\lambda)g_{k}+\lambda g_{k+1})
for all
k\in[p]
. It remains us to show that, up to removing some
g_{k}
, we can ensure
g_{1}>g_{2}>\dots>g_{p+1}
. Suppose that there is
k\in[p]
such that
g_{1}>\dots>g_{k}
and
g_{k+1}\geq g_{k}
. We introduce the smallest integer
l\geq k
such that
g_{l+1}<g_{k}
(
l
is well-defined as
g_{p+1}=\underline{g}<g_{k}
). By concavity of
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
, we have
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k})\geq(1-\bar{\lambda})x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{l})+\bar{\lambda}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{l+1})
for
\bar{\lambda}=\frac{g_{k}-g_{l}}{g_{l+1}-g_{l}}\in[0,1]
. Then, for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k})+\lambda x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{l+1})

\displaystyle\geq(1-\lambda^{\prime})x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{l})+\lambda^{\prime}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{l+1})

\displaystyle\geq(1-\theta)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}((1-\lambda^{\prime})g_{l}+\lambda^{\prime}g_{l+1})

\displaystyle=(1-\theta)x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}((1-\lambda)g_{k}+\lambda g_{l+1})

where
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda+\bar{\lambda}(1-\lambda)\in[0,1]
. By removing the
g_{k+1},\dots,g_{l}
and recursively applying the same argument on the remaining sequence, we end up with a sequence
\overline{g}=g_{1}>\dots>g_{q+1}=\underline{g}
as in the right-hand side of (
35
), with
q\leq p
. This shows that
\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
is greater than or equal to the right-hand side of (
35
).
∎

In the next lemma, we show that the primal and dual straight line complexity
for a single component of the maximum central path are related up to a factor

2
, modulo a slight widening of the neighborhood. This shows that that
primal and dual straight line complexities are essentially equivalent. The
proof proceeds by decomposing straight line segments into polarized segments.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
L\coloneqq\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{1-\alpha,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
,
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
i\in[n]
, and let

\overline{g}=g_{1}>g_{2}>\dots>g_{L+1}=\underline{g}
be the minimizer
of (
35
). Define
\phi_{i}:[\underline{g},\overline{g}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
so that
\phi_{i}(g)=\max\{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1}),\frac{g}{g_{j}}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})\}
for
g\in[g_{j},g_{j+1}]
,
j\in[L]
. Then,
\phi_{i}(g)
and
\frac{g}{\phi_{i}(g)}
are continuous piecewise linear functions on
(\underline{g},\overline{g}]
with at most
2L
pieces, and

\displaystyle x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)

\displaystyle\geq\phi_{i}(g)\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g),

(36)

\displaystyle s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha g}{2\phi_{i}(g)}\geq\frac{\alpha}{4}s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g),\quad\forall g\in(\underline{g},\overline{g}].

(37)

Moreover,
\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1-\alpha/4,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})\leq 2\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{1-\alpha,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
.

Remark 4.6
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
, it can be shown that straight line complexity satisfies

\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1-\alpha,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})\leq O(1)\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1-\alpha/4,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
. That is,
shrinking the neighborhood size by a constant factor can only change the
straight line complexity of a single coordinate of the max central path by a
constant factor. In particular, this implies that

\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1-\alpha,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})\leq O(1)\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{1-\alpha,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
for
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
. As this does not have a significant effect on our iteration bounds,
we do not prove this here.

Proof of
Lemma

4.5
.

Noting that
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})\geq\tfrac{g_{j}}{g_{j-1}}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j-1})
,
j\in\{2,\dots,L+1\}
, by
Lemma

4.3
and
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})\geq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})
,
j\in[L]
, by monotonicity, we have that
\phi(g_{j})=x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})
,
\forall j\in[L+1]
. In particular,
\phi_{i}
is
well-defined. Since each function
\max\{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1}),\frac{g}{g_{j}}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})\}
,
i\in[L]
, is continuous piecewise linear
with at most
2
pieces,
\phi_{i}
is also continuous with at most
2L
pieces.
By our assumption that
\mathcal{P}
is strictly feasible, we have that

x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})>0
for
j\in[L]
since
g_{j}>g_{L+1}\geq 0
. In
particular, for
g\in(g_{j+1},g_{j}]
,
i\in[L]
, we have that

\tfrac{g}{\phi_{i}(g)}=\min\{\tfrac{g}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})},\tfrac{g_{j}}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})}\}
if
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})>0
and

\tfrac{g}{\phi_{i}(g)}=\tfrac{g_{j}}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})}
otherwise. Thus,

\frac{g}{\phi_{i}(g)}
is also continous piecewise linear with at most
2L

pieces on
(\underline{g},\overline{g}]
.

We now prove (
36
), (
37
). For
j\in[L]
,

g\in[\underline{g},\overline{g}]
, let

l^{j}_{i}(g)\coloneqq\left(x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})-g_{j+1}\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})-x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})}{g_{j}-g_{j+1}}\right)+\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})-x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})}{g_{j}-g_{j+1}}g:=a_{j}+b_{j}g,

denote the linear interpolation of
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
through
g_{j}
and
g_{j+1}
. By
concavity, monotonicity of
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
and
0\leq g_{j+1}<g_{j}
, the
intercept
a_{j}
and slope
b_{j}
of
l^{j}_{i}
satisfy

\displaystyle 0\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(0)\leq l^{j}_{i}(0)

\displaystyle=a_{j}=x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})-g_{j+1}\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})-x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})}{g_{j}-g_{j+1}}\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1}),

(38)

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})-x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})}{g_{j}-g_{j+1}}

\displaystyle=b_{j}\leq\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})-x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(0)}{g_{j}-0}\leq\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})}{g_{j}}\,.

(39)

For
g\in(g_{j+1},g_{j}]
, then by (
38
) and (
39
) we
get that

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(g)

\displaystyle=\max\left\{l^{j}_{i}(g_{j+1}),g\frac{l^{j}_{i}(g_{j})}{g_{j}}\right\}

(40)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{l^{j}_{i}(g_{j+1})+b_{j}(g_{j}-g_{j+1}),\left(1-\frac{g}{g_{j}}\right)l^{j}_{i}(0)+\frac{g}{g_{j}}l^{j}_{i}(g_{j})\right\}=l^{j}_{i}(g)

\displaystyle=a_{j}+gb_{j}\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1})+g\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})}{g_{j}}\leq 2\phi_{i}(g).

(41)

Since
[(g_{j},x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j})),(g_{j+1},x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{j+1}))]\subset{{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{mp}}_{i}}(1-\alpha)
by construction, for
g\in(g_{j+1},g_{j}]
we have that

x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)\geq l^{j}_{i}(g)\geq\phi_{i}(g)\geq\frac{1}{2}l^{j}_{i}(g)\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g).

Since the above holds for all
j\in[L]
, this
proves (
36
). To prove (
37
), we
combine (
36
) with
Theorem

4.2
as follows: for

g\in(\underline{g},\overline{g}]
,

s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)=\frac{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)}\geq\frac{g}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)}\geq\frac{\alpha g}{2\phi_{i}(g)}\geq\frac{\alpha g}{2x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)}=\frac{\alpha s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)}{2}\frac{g}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)}\geq\frac{\alpha s^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)}{4}.

From here, the moreover
\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{d}}_{1-\alpha/4,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})\leq 2\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{1-\alpha,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
follows immediately from (
37
) and the fact that

\frac{\alpha g}{2\phi_{i}(g)}
is continuous piecewise linear with a number of pieces bounded by

2\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{1-\alpha,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
on

(\underline{g},\overline{g}]
(note that we can extend
\frac{\alpha g}{2\phi_{g}(g)}
to
[\underline{g},\overline{g}]
by continuity).
∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

1.8
; we restate it here for convenience.
See
1.8

Proof.

We only prove the statement for the primal straight-line complexity bound;
the proof for the dual bound follows analogously.

Let
\underline{g}\coloneqq n\mu_{1}
and
\overline{g}\coloneqq n\mu_{0}
denote
the gaps at
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{0}
. For
i\in[n]
, apply
Lemma

4.5

on
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}
on
[\overline{g},\underline{g}]
to get
\overline{g}=g_{i,1}>\dots>g_{i,L_{i}+1}=\underline{g}
,
L_{i}=\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g})
, and the corresponding piecewise linear
function
\phi_{i}(\cdot)
satisfying
x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)\geq\phi_{i}(g)\geq\tfrac{1-\theta}{2}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
,
\forall g\in[\underline{g},\overline{g}]
.
For
j\in[L_{i}]
, define
g_{i,j}^{+}\coloneqq\tfrac{g_{i,j}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{i,j+1})}{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{i,j})}\in[g_{i,j+1},g_{i,j}]
, which we note satisfies
\phi_{i}(g_{i,j}^{+})=\max\left\{x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{i,j+1}),\frac{g_{i,j}^{+}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{i,j})}{g_{i,j}}\right\}=\frac{g_{i,j}^{+}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{i,j})}{g_{i,j}}=x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{i,j+1})
. From here, let
G_{i}\coloneqq\{g_{i,j}:i\in[L_{i}+1]\}\cup\{g_{i,j}^{+}:i\in[L_{i}]\}

denote the breakpoints of the function
\phi_{i}
. For a closed interval
[a,b]\subseteq[\underline{g},\overline{g}]
with
a<b
, we say that
[a,b]
is type (B) for
\phi_{i}
if
\phi_{i}
is constant on

[a,b]
and is type (N) for
\phi_{i}
if
\phi_{i}(g^{\prime})=\frac{g^{\prime}}{g}\phi_{i}(g)
,
\forall~g^{\prime},g\in[a,b],g^{\prime}\leq g
and
g>0
. By construction of
\phi_{i}

and
G_{i}
, if
G_{i}\cap(a,b)=\emptyset
, then
[a,b]
must be either type
(B) or type (N) for
\phi_{i}
(note that these options are mutually exclusive).

Define
G=\cup_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}
to be the union of the breakpoints of the
functions
\phi_{i},i\in[n]
. Let
\underline{g}=g_{T}<\dots<g_{0}=\overline{g}
satisfy
G=\{g_{i}:i\in\{0,\dots,T\}\}
. Since
\underline{g}\in G_{i}
,
\forall i\in[n]
, we have that

T=|G|-1\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}(|G_{i}|-1)\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\coloneqq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g}).

(42)

Define
(B^{(0)},N^{(0)})\coloneqq([n],\emptyset)
, and for

k\in[T]
, define
(B^{(k)},N^{(k)})
by

\displaystyle B^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\{i\in[n]:[g_{k},g_{k-1}]\text{ is type (B) for }\phi_{i}\},

\displaystyle N^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\{i\in[n]:[g_{k},g_{k-1}]\text{ is type (N) for }\phi_{i}\}.

By construction of
G
, we have
(g_{k},g_{k-1})\cap G_{i}=\emptyset
,
i\in[n]
, and hence
(B^{(k)},N^{(k)})
partitions
[n]
. The sum of partition differences then satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{T}|N^{(k)}\Delta N^{(k-1)}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{T}|1[i\in N^{(k)}]-1[i\in N^{(k-1)}]|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{T}1[g_{k-1}\in G_{i}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(|G_{i}|-1)\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta,i}(\underline{g},\overline{g}).

(43)

Define
h_{i}\coloneqq g_{i}/n
,
i\in\{0,\dots,T\}
, where we have
h_{0}=g_{0}/n=\overline{g}/n=\mu_{0}
and
h_{T}=g_{T}/n=\underline{g}/n=\mu_{1}
.
For
k\in[T]
, we claim that the central path segment

\mathrm{CP}[h_{k},h_{k-1}]
is
\gamma=\frac{1-\theta}{4n}
-polarized with
polarization partition
(B^{(k)},N^{(k)})
. Together with the claim, the
bounds (
42
) and (
43
) directly imply theorem.

We now prove the claim for
k\in[T]
. For
\mu\in[h_{k},h_{k-1}]=[g_{k}/n,g_{k-1}/n]
and
i\in B^{(k)}
, by
1.2
and
the guarantees on
\phi_{i}
, we have that

x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{i}(\mu)\geq\frac{1}{2n}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(n\mu)\geq\frac{1}{2n}\phi_{i}(n\mu)=\frac{1}{2n}\phi_{i}(g_{k-1})\geq\frac{1-\theta}{4n}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g_{k-1})\geq\frac{1-\theta}{4n}x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(h_{k-1}),

(44)

where in the first equality we used that
[g_{k},g_{k-1}]
is type (B) for

\phi_{i}
. Similarly, for
i\in N^{(k)}
, we have that

\displaystyle x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{i}(\mu)

\displaystyle\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(n\mu)\leq\frac{2}{1-\theta}\phi_{i}(n\mu)=\frac{2}{1-\theta}\frac{\mu}{h_{k-1}}\phi_{i}(nh_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{1-\theta}\frac{\mu}{h_{k-1}}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(nh_{k-1})\leq\frac{4n}{1-\theta}\frac{\mu}{h_{k-1}}x_{i}^{\mathrm{cp}}(h_{k-1}),

(45)

where in the first equality we used that
[g_{k},g_{k-1}]
is type (N) for

\phi_{i}
. By
Remark

3.2
, the
inequalities (
44
) and (
45
)
imply that
\mathrm{CP}[h_{k},h_{k-1}]
is
\frac{1-\theta}{4n}
-polarized with
polarization partition
(B^{(k)},N^{(k)})
as desired.
∎

5
The Trust Region Step

In this section, we prove important properties for the trust-region step of
Lan, Monteiro and Tsuchiya
[
38
]
, as defined in the
program (
8
) in the introduction. The estimates proved
here will play a crucial role in the analysis of the IPM, and correspond to
refinements of existing estimates in
[
38
]
and
[
48
]
that
are more adapted to our IPM.

Our first estimate, given in
Section

5.1
is a general relationship
between the parameters of trust-region directions and the achievable
step-length guarantees one can derive from them. The second main result,
given in
Section

5.2
, regards the computation of the optimal
partition
(B,N)
to use when computing trust-region directions. We show that
if a suitably good trust-region direction exists with respect to some
partition
(B,N)
, then this partition can be read off from the coordinates
of the affine scaling direction (which implies that it is unique). We prove
this by showing that the affine scaling direction must be close to any good
enough trust region direction. This is analoguous to a result
of
[
48
]
, who showed that the same statement holds if we replace
the trust-region direction by the layered least squares direction.

We dub the partition induced by the affine scaling direction the

associated partition
(in
[
38
]
this was called the AS
bipartition). With respect to our IPM, the associated partition will be used
to identify the current polarization partition.

5.1
Step-Length Estimates for Trust Region Directions

We now formally link the properties of trust-region program solutions to the
achievable step-lengths in
Proposition

2.44
. A similar estimate is
proved in
[
38
, Lemma 2.7]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
x,s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}
satisfy
\left\lVert xs/\mu-\mathbf{1}_{n}\right\rVert\leq 1/6
, where
\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(x,s)
, and let
B\cup N=[n]
be a partition. For
\Delta x,\Delta s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, assume that

\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}_{B},\Delta\hat{s}_{N})\right\rVert\leq\delta\leq 1/6\,,\quad\quad\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B},\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N})\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon\leq 1/6,

(46)

where
\hat{\xi}\coloneqq\sqrt{xs/\mu}
,
(\Delta\hat{x},\Delta\hat{s})\coloneqq(\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x,\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s)
and
(\hat{x},\hat{s})\coloneqq(x\hat{\xi}^{-1},s\hat{\xi}^{-1})
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x\Delta s}{\mu}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\delta,

(47)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)}{\mu}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\varepsilon.

(48)

Proof.

To derive (
47
) and (
48
), we prove the following more general statements:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x\Delta s}{\mu}\right\rVert^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert\right)^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert\right)^{2},

(49)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)}{\mu}\right\rVert^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert\right)^{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert\right)^{2}.

(50)

To derive (
47
), we combine (
49
) and the assumed bounds (
46
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x\Delta s}{\mu}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert\right)^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}_{B},\Delta\hat{s}_{N})\right\rVert^{2}(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\varepsilon)^{2}\leq\delta^{2}(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\tfrac{1}{6})^{2}\leq\delta^{2}(\tfrac{5}{4})^{2}\leq 2\delta^{2}.

Similarly, (
48
) is derived by combining (
50
) and (
46
) as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)}{\mu}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert)^{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{2}(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\delta)^{2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{2}.

We now focus on the proofs of (
49
)
and (
50
).

Proof of inequality (
49
)

To begin, note that

\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x\Delta s}{\mu}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}\Delta\hat{s}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}.

For the
B
term on the right hand side, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left(\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert\right)^{2},

where the last inequality follows since
\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert
and

\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i\in[n]}\sqrt{\tfrac{x_{i}s_{i}}{\mu}}\leq\sqrt{1+\tfrac{1}{6}}\leq 1+\tfrac{1}{12}.

By a symmetric argument, swapping the roles of (
x
,
s
) and (
B
,
N
), we
also get that

\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}\left(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert\right)^{2}.

Inequality (
49
) now follows by combining for the
B
and

N
bounds above.

Proof of inequality (
50
)

Similar to the above, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x)(s+\Delta s)}{\mu}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}+\Delta\hat{x})(\hat{\xi}+\Delta\hat{s})\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{x}_{B})(\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B})\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N})(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{s}_{N})\right\rVert^{2}.

As before, we bound the
B
and
N
parts separately. For the
B
part, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{x}_{B})(\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B})\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\left(\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert)^{2},

where the last inequality from identical to the first part. Again, by a
symmetric argument,

\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N})(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{s}_{N})\right\rVert^{2}\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}(1+\tfrac{1}{12}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert)^{2}.

Inequality (
50
) now follows by combining for the
B
and

N
bounds above.
∎

5.2
The Associated Partition

The trust-region step is applicable for any non-trivial partition
B\cup N=[n]
and
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
. Following
[
38
]
, we choose a
natural partition derived from the size of normalized coordinates of the
affine scaling direction:

Definition 5.2

(Associated partition)
.

For
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
, let
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
be the affine scaling step as in (
24
). Let us define the associated partition
\widetilde{B}_{z}\cup\widetilde{N}_{z}=[n]
as

\widetilde{B}_{z}\coloneqq\left\{i:\left|\frac{\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}}{x_{i}}\right|<\left|\frac{\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}}{s_{i}}\right|\right\}\,,\quad\widetilde{N}_{z}\coloneqq[n]\setminus\widetilde{B}_{z}\,.

The affine scaling step is the canonical candidate for an improving direction. Namely, for each
i\in\widetilde{B}_{z}
the variable
s_{i}
decreases at a faster rate than
x_{i}
, and vice versa for
i\in\widetilde{N}_{z}
.

As we show below, as long as the trust-region program admits a sufficiently
good solution with respect to the polarization partition
(B,N)
, then the
affine scaling step is close to this trust-region direction and has
associated partition
(B,N)
. This implies that the optimal choice of
partition is in fact unique under the assumption that a long steps exists.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
,
\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z)
,
B\cup N=[n]
be a partition. Assume that there exists
(\Delta x,\Delta s)\in W\times W^{\perp}
satisfying

\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}_{B},\Delta\hat{s}_{N})\right\rVert\leq\delta\leq 1/30,\quad\quad\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B})\right\rVert\leq\epsilon\leq 1/30,

(51)

where
\hat{\xi}\coloneqq\sqrt{xs/\mu}
,
(\Delta\hat{x},\Delta\hat{s})\coloneqq(\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x,\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s)
,
(\hat{x},\hat{s})\coloneqq(x\hat{\xi}^{-1},s\hat{\xi}^{-1})
.
Then, the affine scale direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
at
z
satisfies

\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}}{\mu}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}})(s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})}{\mu}\right\rVert\leq 3.5(\delta+\varepsilon),

(52)

and the associated partition at
z
satisfies
(\widetilde{B}_{z},\widetilde{N}_{z})=(B,N)
.

Proof.

Recall that the affine scaling step
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
is defined by

\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}}=-\hat{\xi},

where
(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}})\coloneqq(\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})\in\hat{x}^{-1}W\times\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}
form an orthogonal
decomposition of
-\hat{\xi}
. By orthogonality, we therefore have that
