\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}-\Delta\hat{x}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}}-\Delta\hat{s}\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\hat{\xi}+\Delta\hat{x}+\Delta\hat{s}\right\rVert^{2}.

By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{x}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}+2\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}+\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}+2\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

Therefore, by (
51
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{\xi}+\Delta\hat{x}+\Delta\hat{s}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{x}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}+\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B}\right\rVert^{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2(\delta^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}).

In particular,

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}_{B},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}}_{N})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2(\delta^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})}+\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}_{B},\Delta\hat{s}_{N})\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{2}(\delta+\varepsilon)+\delta

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{2}+1)(\delta+\varepsilon)\leq\frac{1}{6}\,,

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2(\delta^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})}+\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq(\sqrt{2}+1)(\delta+\varepsilon)\leq\frac{1}{6}\,.

Therefore by
Proposition

5.1
, since affine scaling satisfies
\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}=(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}})(s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
, we get that

\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}}{\mu}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}})(s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})}{\mu}\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{2}(\sqrt{2}+1)(\delta+\varepsilon)\leq 3.5(\delta+\varepsilon).

For the last part, notice that
|\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}/x_{i}|<|\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}/s_{i}|\Leftrightarrow|\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|<|\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|
,
\forall i\in[n]
. By

Proposition

2.42
, since
\beta\in(0,1/6]
, we have that
\hat{\xi}\geq\sqrt{1-\beta}\mathbf{1}_{n}\geq(1-1/6)\mathbf{1}_{n}
. For
i\in B
, using that

(\sqrt{2}+1)(\delta+\varepsilon)\leq 3(\delta+\varepsilon)\leq 1/3
together with (
5.2
) we see that

|\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|\leq 3(\delta+\varepsilon)<(1-1/6)-3(\delta+\varepsilon)\leq\hat{\xi}_{i}-|\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|\leq|\hat{\xi}_{i}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|=|\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|.

By a symmetric argument, we also have
|\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|<|\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|
, for
i\in N
. Therefore,
(\widetilde{B}_{z},\widetilde{N}_{z})=(B,N)
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

Given that the affine scaling step is always close to a trust-region direction, as long as the direction yields a sufficiently long step,
one may wonder why the trust-region
direction, or its approximate version the subspace LLS direction (defined in the next section), is even
needed. At a quantitative level, this comes from the fact that the
trust-region direction assumed to exist in
Lemma

5.3
would in fact
allow us to decrease the gap by a factor
\Theta(\varepsilon)
(see

Proposition

2.44
), whereas the “nearby” affine scaling would only
achieve
\Theta(\delta+\varepsilon)
, which can be arbitrarily worse. Indeed, recall
that in (
8
) we only enforce
\delta=O(\beta)
, that is,
proportional to the neighborhood size, while trying to minimize
\varepsilon
.

6
The Subspace LLS Direction and Cheap Lift Subspaces

In this section, we formally define the subspace layered least squares (SLLS)
steps and show how to compute the cheap lift subspaces as described in the
Introduction. In
Section

6.1
, we formally define the SLLS step and overview
its basic computational aspects. Then, in
Section

6.2
, we introduce the
lifting map and operator and overview their basic properties and give an
algorithm to compute them. In
Section

6.3
, we define cheap lift subspaces,
that will be used for taking subspace LLS steps, as well as the lifting
operators, and show how to construct cheap lift subspaces from approximate
singular subspaces (as defined in
Definition

2.24
). In

Section

6.4
, we show how to compute cheap lift subspaces of
approximately maximum dimension using an approximate singular value
decomposition (see the
\varrho
-
SVD
problem).

6.1
The Subspace Layered Least Squares Direction

We now introduce a new update direction, called the
subspace layered
least squares
(SLLS) update direction, which will allow us to accelerate our
IPM over long straight parts of the central path. As discussed in the
previous sections, such straight segments of the central path are in
fact
polarized
according to a partition
B\cup N=[n]

(
Definition

3.1
). Within this segment, the primal variables indexed
by
N
scale down linearly with respect to the parameter
\mu
, while the primal
variables in
B
will remain roughly constant. For the dual, the situation is
reversed, the variables in
B
scale down while the variables in
N
remain
roughly constant.

As discussed, in the Introduction, the “optimal” step direction that mimics
the polarization pattern is the
trust region step
of Lan, Monteiro and
Tsuchiya (
8
). To be able to suitably approximate this
step in strongly polynomial time, we require the SLLS update direction, which
we formally define below.

Definition 6.1

(Subspace Layered Least Squares direction)
.

Let
z\coloneqq(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
\mu=\overline{\mu}(z)
,
B\cup N=[n]
be a non-trivial partition. Let
V\subseteq W
,
U\subseteq W^{\perp}
be linear subspaces satisfying

\dim(\pi_{N}(V))=\dim(V)
, and
\dim(\pi_{B}(U))=\dim(U)
. The
Subspace LLS (SLLS)
update direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})\in W\times W^{\perp}
at
z
with respect to
(B,N,V,U)
is defined as

\displaystyle\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\delta\in V}\left\lVert\frac{x_{N}+\delta_{N}}{\hat{x}_{N}}\right\rVert^{2},

(53)

\displaystyle\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\delta\in U}\left\lVert\frac{s_{B}+\delta_{B}}{\hat{s}_{B}}\right\rVert^{2},

(54)

where
\hat{x}\coloneqq\sqrt{x\mu/s}
and
\hat{s}\coloneqq\sqrt{s\mu/x}
.

Remark 6.2
.

Note that
\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}
are indeed well-defined, as our
assumption that
\dim(\pi_{N}(V))=\dim(V)
and
\dim(\pi_{B}(U))=\dim(U)

allows to uniquely determine
\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}
from their
coordinates in
N
and
B
respectively. In particular, if
N=\emptyset
,
then
V=\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
and hence
\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}=\mathbf{0}_{n}
. Similarly, if
B=\emptyset
, then
U=\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
and
\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}=\mathbf{0}_{n}
.

As with affine scaling, one can interpret the SLLS step directions in
terms of orthogonal projections. Using that
\hat{\xi}\coloneqq\sqrt{xs/\mu}=x/\hat{x}=s/\hat{s}
, in direct analogy with (
24
) in

Section

2.6
,
(\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x)_{N}
and
(\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s)_{B}
are
respectively the orthogonal projections of
-\hat{\xi}_{N}
and
-\hat{\xi}_{B}
onto

\pi_{N}(\hat{x}^{-1}V)
and
\pi_{B}(\hat{s}^{-1}U)
. In contrast, whereas the affine
scaling direction minimizes the norm of the primal-dual residual vector

(\hat{\xi}+\Delta\hat{x},\hat{\xi}+\Delta\hat{s})
, the subspace LLS direction instead tries to
minimize the norm of
(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}_{B})
.

Similarly to the affine scaling and corrector direction, the SLLS direction can be computed in
strongly polynomial time, given an appropriate representation of the
subspaces. The formulas for the subspace LLS step directions are given in the next
proposition. These are computed by solving the linear system which sets the
gradient of the corresponding quadratic optimization problems to zero.

Proposition 6.3

(Subspace LLS Step Formulas)
.

Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}_{++}
be an iterate,
B\cup N=[n]
be a non-trivial partition, and
\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{p}},\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{d}}
be matrices where
V\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P})
,
U\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})
satisfy
\dim(V)=\dim(\pi_{N}(V))
and
\dim(U)=\dim(\pi_{B}(U))
. Then, the SLLS direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
at
z

with respect to
(B,N,U,V)
can be computed in strongly polynomial time as
follows:

\displaystyle\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}\left((\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet})^{\top}\operatorname{diag}(s_{N}/x_{N})\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{+}\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}s_{N},

\displaystyle\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}\left((\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}_{B,\bullet})^{\top}\operatorname{diag}(x_{B}/s_{B})\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}_{B,\bullet}\right)^{+}\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}_{B,\bullet}\right)^{\top}x_{B}.

In the above, by convention,
\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}=\mathbf{0}_{n}
if
N=\emptyset
and

\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}=\mathbf{0}_{n}
if
B=\emptyset
.

Proof.

The strong polynomiality follows directly from the fact that computing
pseudoinverses is strongly polynomial. We thus focus on correctness. We
prove correctness of the formula for
\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}
as the analysis for

\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}
is symmetric. The formula is clearly correct if
N=\emptyset
by convention, so assume
N\neq\emptyset
. Let

z_{x}\coloneqq-\left((\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet})^{\top}\operatorname{diag}(s_{N}/x_{N})\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{+}\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}s_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{p}}.

Recalling that
V\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P})
, we have that
\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}=\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}z_{x}\in V
. Thus, letting
g_{x}(z)=\left\lVert\frac{x_{N}+\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}z}{\hat{x}_{N}}\right\rVert^{2}
, it suffices to check that

\displaystyle z_{x}\in\argmin_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{p}}}g_{x}(z)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\nabla g_{x}(z_{x})=\mathbf{0}_{m_{p}}\Leftrightarrow 2\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}\left(\frac{x_{N}+\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}z_{x}}{\hat{x}_{N}^{2}}\right)=\mathbf{0}_{m_{p}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}\left(s_{N}+\operatorname{diag}(s_{N}/x_{N})\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}z_{x}\right)=\mathbf{0}_{m_{p}},

(55)

where we have used convexity of
g_{x}
and
\hat{x}\coloneqq\sqrt{x\mu/s}
where

\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(x,s)>0
. Note that while
g_{x}
need not have a unique
minimizer (i.e., the columns of
\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}
may be linearly dependent), the
condition
\dim(V)=\dim(\pi_{N}(V))=\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet})
indeed ensures
that
\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}=\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}z_{x}
is the unique minimizer
to (
53
) assuming
z_{x}
minimizes
g_{x}
. From here, it suffices to check that
z_{x}
satisfies (
55
), and hence minimizes
g_{x}
. For this purpose, letting
\mathbf{P}=\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}\operatorname{diag}(s_{N}/x_{N})\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}
, by
Proposition

2.14
part (
1
) and
x,s>\mathbf{0}_{n}

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}s_{N}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\operatorname{im}\left(\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}\right)}\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}s_{N}=\Pi_{\operatorname{im}\left(\mathbf{P}\right)}\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}s_{N}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{+}\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}s_{N},

where we have used that
\operatorname{im}\left(\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}\right)=\operatorname{im}\left(\left(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)^{\top}\operatorname{diag}(s_{N}/x_{N})\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}_{N,\bullet}\right)
.
∎

6.2
Lifting Maps and Operators

The algorithm in
Section

7
and the analysis in
Section

7.3
will rely crucially
on the properties of
lifting maps and operators
. These will be used to compute cheap
lift subspaces for the purpose of computing subspace layered least squares
directions. These maps have appeared in many prior works on layered least
squares algorithms
[
66
,
47
,
19
]
. In this
section, we give a self-contained overview of all their main properties.
Importantly, we show their duality properties and that they can be computed
in strongly polynomial time.

Definition 6.4
.

Given a non-trivial partition
I\cup J=[n]
and a subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define the lifting map
L_{I}^{W}\colon\mathbb{R}^{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
follows:

L^{W}_{I}(x)\coloneqq\argmin\{\left\lVert w\right\rVert:w\in W,w_{I}=\Pi_{\pi_{I}(W)}(x)\}.

(56)

We further define the lifting operator
\ell^{W}_{I}\colon\pi_{I}(W)\rightarrow\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
by

\ell_{I}^{W}(x)\coloneqq(L^{W}_{I}(x))_{J}=\pi_{J}\left(L^{W}_{I}(x)\right),\forall x\in\pi_{I}(W)\,.

(57)

Note that if
x\in\pi_{I}(W)
, then
w=L_{I}^{W}(x)=(\ell_{I}^{W}(x),x)
is the minimum-norm point in
W
with
w_{I}=x
.

By convention, if
(I,J)
is a trivial partition, we define
L_{I}^{W}:=\Pi_{W}

if
I=[n]
, and define
L_{I}^{W}
to be the linear operator from

\mathbb{R}^{\emptyset}\coloneqq\{0\}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
if
I=\emptyset
. We furthermore
define
\ell_{I}^{W}
to be the linear operator from
W
to

\mathbb{R}^{\emptyset}
if
I=[n]
and from
\mathbb{R}^{\emptyset}
to
W^{\perp}
if

I=\emptyset
.

The computation of lifting operators will be crucial to our IPM. The
following proposition, whose proof is deferred to later in the section,
gives an explicit formula for the associated matrices of the lifting map and
operator and shows how to compute them in strongly polynomial time.

Proposition 6.5

(Computing Lifting Maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A})=m
, and
I\cup J=[n]
be a
partition. Then, for
W=\ker(\mathbf{A})
, one can in strongly
polynomial time compute the associated matrices (as in
Definition

2.1
) for the lifting map
and operator as follows:

\mathcal{M}(L_{I}^{W})=\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+},\quad\quad\mathcal{M}(\ell_{I}^{W})=\mathbf{P}_{J,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+},

where
\mathbf{P}\coloneqq\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{A}
is the orthogonal projection
onto
W
, i.e., the matrix associated with
\Pi_{W}
.

Towards proving the above, we first show that
L_{I}^{W}
and
\ell_{I}^{W}
are
both well-defined linear operators, which is not directly obvious from the
definitions. Linearity of these operators, as well as other key properties,
is proven in
Lemma

6.6
and
Lemma

6.7
below.

Lemma 6.6
.

For a linear subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and a partition
I\cup J=[n]
, then the function
L_{I}^{W}\colon\mathbb{R}^{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as defined in
Definition

6.4
is a
linear operator. In particular, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{I}
,
w=L_{I}^{W}(x)
is the
unique solution to the following linear system:

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})\,,

(58)

\displaystyle\pi_{I}(w)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\pi_{I}(W)}(x).

Furthermore,
\pi_{I}(W)=\pi_{I}(\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}))
.

Proof.

If
(I,J)
is a trivial partition, the characterization follows directly by our convention, so we may assume that
(I,J)
is non-trivial.

By construction, the solution set of
L_{I}^{W}(x)
is non-empty. Furthermore,
the minimum norm solution exists and is unique by strict convexity of the
squared Euclidean norm. Thus,
w=L_{I}^{W}(x)
is well-defined. The definition requires
w_{I}=\pi_{I}(w)=\Pi_{\pi_{I}(W)}(x)
; we show that subject to this,

w=L_{I}^{W}(x)
if and only if

w\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
. By Lagrangian duality, using that the gradient of the squared norm objective is

\nabla(\left\lVert w\right\rVert^{2})=2w
, it follows that
w
is optimal to the program in the definition (
56
) if and only if

\displaystyle w_{I}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\pi_{I}(W)}(x)\,,

(59)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\in W\,,\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\perp(W\cap\mathbb{R}_{J}^{n})\,,

where we have used that the difference of any two feasible solutions to (
56
) lives in
W\cap\mathbb{R}_{J}^{n}
. The last requirement can be written as
w\in(W\cap\mathbb{R}_{J}^{n})^{\perp}=W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}
, where the equality follows by
Proposition

2.3
.
By orthogonal decomposition, we note that
\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})=W\cap(W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
.

Thus, the last two requirements are equivalent to
w\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
, completing the proof. Further, since (
59
) can be equivalently written as a linear system of equations, it follows that
L_{I}^{W}
is a linear map.

We now prove the furthermore:
\pi_{I}(W)=\pi_{I}(\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}))
. Since

\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})\subseteq W
, we have
\pi_{I}(\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}))\subseteq\pi_{I}(W)
. To show the reverse containment
\pi_{I}(W)\subseteq\pi_{I}(\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}))
, take any

x\in\pi_{I}(W)
. We have that
L_{I}^{W}(x)\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})

by the first part, and
\pi_{I}(L_{I}^{W}(x))=x
. Therefore,
\pi_{I}(W)\subseteq\pi_{I}(\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}))
, as needed.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

For a linear subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, a partition
I\cup J=[n]
,
\ell^{W}_{I}\colon\pi_{I}(W)\rightarrow\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
as in

Definition

6.4
is a well-defined linear operator. Moreover,

\operatorname{im}(\ell^{W}_{I})=\pi_{J}(W)\cap\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
,
\ker(\ell^{W}_{I})=\pi_{I}(W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
and
\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(\ell^{W}_{I}))=\pi_{I}(W)\cap\pi_{I}(W^{\perp})
.

Proof.

If
(I,J)
is a trivial partition, the lemma follows trivially by the definition of
L_{I}^{W}
, so we may assume that
(I,J)
is non-trivial. We first prove the moreover statement
\operatorname{im}(\ell^{W}_{I})=\pi_{J}(W)\cap\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
. Assuming this, the well-definedness of
\ell^{W}_{I}\colon\pi_{I}(W)\rightarrow\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
follows immediately from the inclusion
\operatorname{im}(\ell^{W}_{I})\subseteq\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
. Furthermore, linearity of
\ell^{W}_{I}
follows from linearity of
L_{I}^{W}
, proved in
Lemma

6.6
, and the definition
\ell^{W}_{I}(x)=\pi_{J}(L_{I}^{W}(x))
,
\forall x\in\pi_{I}(W)
.

We start by showing the inclusion
\operatorname{im}(\ell^{W}_{I})\subseteq\pi_{J}(W)\cap\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
. For
x\in\pi_{I}(W)
, by
Lemma

6.6

we have that
L_{I}^{W}(x)\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
and
\ell^{W}_{I}(x)=\pi_{J}(L_{I}^{W}(x))
. Since
\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})=W\cap(W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
, we see that

\ell^{W}_{I}(x)\in\pi_{J}(\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}))\subseteq\pi_{J}(W)\cap\pi_{J}(W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})=\pi_{J}(W)\cap\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
, as needed. We now
show the reverse inclusion
\pi_{J}(W)\cap\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})\subseteq\operatorname{im}(\ell^{W}_{I})
. Take
y\in\pi_{J}(W)\cap\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
. By definition,
there exists
w\in W
such that
w_{J}=y
. It now suffices to show that

L^{W}_{I}(w_{I})=w
, since then
\ell^{W}_{I}(w_{I})=y
. Clearly
\pi_{I}(L^{W}_{I}(w_{I}))=w_{I}
since
w\in W
. By
Lemma

6.6
, it suffices to show that
w\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})=W\cap(W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
. From here, we see that
w\in W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}\Leftrightarrow\pi_{J}(w)\subseteq\pi_{J}(W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})=\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
. The inclusion now follows since
w_{J}=y\in\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})

by assumption.

We now characterize the kernel of
\ell^{W}_{I}
by

\ker(\ell^{W}_{I})=\{x\in\pi_{I}(W):\ell^{W}_{I}(x)=\mathbf{0}_{J}\}=\{x\in\pi_{I}(W):(x,\mathbf{0}_{J})\in W\}=\pi_{I}(W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}),

as needed. Finally, we characterize
\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(\ell^{W}_{I}))
. By

Proposition

2.7
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(\ell^{W}_{I}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{I}(W)\cap\ker(\ell^{W}_{I})^{\perp}=\pi_{I}(W)\cap\pi_{I}(W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\pi_{I}(W)\cap\pi_{I}((W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})

\displaystyle=\pi_{I}(W)\cap\pi_{I}((W^{\perp}+\mathbb{R}^{n}_{J})\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})=\pi_{I}(W)\cap\pi_{I}(W^{\perp}),

as needed.
∎

We now have the tools to prove
Proposition

6.5
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.5
.

To begin, recall that
\mathbf{A}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{A}
is the orthogonal
projection onto
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}^{\top})

=\ker(\mathbf{A})^{\perp}=W^{\perp}
, and hence
\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{A}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{A}
is the orthogonal projection onto
W
.
Furthermore, the inverse
(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})^{-1}
is well-defined since
\mathbf{A}
has
full row rank.

To justify the formula for
\mathcal{M}(L_{I}^{W})
, it suffices to show that

L_{I}^{W}(x)=\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+}x
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{I}
since the
input space of
L_{I}^{W}
is
\mathbb{R}^{I}
. By
Lemma

6.6
, we have that

w=L_{I}^{W}(x)
is the unique solution to
w_{I}=\Pi_{\pi_{I}(W)}(x)=x
and
w\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})=\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
. By
Proposition

2.14
part (
1
), we
have
\mathbf{P}_{I,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+}x=\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})}x=\Pi_{\pi_{I}(W)}x
,
where the last equality is
\pi_{I}(W)=\pi_{I}(\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}))=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})
in

Lemma

6.6
. Since by construction
\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+}x\in\mathbf{P}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})
, by uniqueness we have that
w=\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+}x
as needed.

We now justify the formula for
\mathcal{M}(\ell_{I}^{W})
. The identity

\ell^{W}_{I}(x)=\mathbf{P}_{J,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+}x
, for
x\in\pi_{I}(W)
, follows
directly from
\ell^{W}_{I}(x)\coloneqq\pi_{J}(L^{W}_{I}(x))
. To conclude the
proof, it suffices to show that
\operatorname{im}((\mathbf{P}_{J,I}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+})^{\top})\subseteq\operatorname{im}(((\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+})^{\top})\subseteq\pi_{I}(W)
, recalling that
\ell_{I}^{W}:\pi_{I}(W)\rightarrow\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
. Since
\ker((\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+})=\ker((\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{\top})
by definition of the pseudoinverse, we have that
\operatorname{im}(((\mathbf{P}_{I,I})^{+})^{\top})=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{P}_{I,I})=\pi_{I}(W)
, where the first equality is by
Proposition

2.7
part (
2
). This proves the statement.

For the strongly polynomial computability, this follows directly from the
fact that computing matrix products, inverses and pseudoinverses
(
Proposition

2.15
) is strongly polynomial.
∎

To conclude this section, we give the fundamental duality relation between
lifting operators, which will be crucial to the analysis of the IPM.

Lemma 6.8
.

For a linear subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and a partition
I\cup J=[n]
,
\ell^{W}_{I}=-\operatorname{ad}\left(\ell^{W^{\perp}}_{J}\right)
. In particular,

\sigma^{+}(\ell^{W}_{I})=\sigma^{+}(\ell^{W^{\perp}}_{J})
.

Proof.

If
(I,J)
is a trivial partition, the the statement follows by our definition of
\ell_{I}^{W}
, so may assume that
(I,J)
is non-trivial. To prove the statement, it suffices to show that for all
x\in\pi_{I}(W)
that

L_{I}^{W}(x)=\left(x,\ell_{I}^{W}(x)\right)=\left(x,-\operatorname{ad}\left(\ell_{J}^{W^{\perp}}\right)(x)\right).

Letting
z\coloneqq\left(x,-\operatorname{ad}\left(\ell_{J}^{W^{\perp}}\right)(x)\right)
, by
Lemma

6.6
,
it suffices to show that
z\in\Pi_{W}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I})

=W\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}+W^{\perp})
. To show that
z\in W
, we must show that
\left\langle v,z\right\rangle=0
,
\forall v\in W^{\perp}
. For
v\in W^{\perp}
, we see that

\displaystyle\left\langle z,v\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle x,v_{I}\right\rangle-\left\langle\operatorname{ad}\left(\ell_{J}^{W^{\perp}}\right)(x),v_{J}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle x,v_{I}\right\rangle-\left\langle x,\ell_{J}^{W^{\perp}}(v_{J})\right\rangle\quad\left(\text{ since }v_{J}\in\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})~\right)

\displaystyle=\left\langle x,v_{I}-\ell_{J}^{W^{\perp}}(v_{J})\right\rangle=\left\langle L_{I}^{W}(x),(v_{I}-\ell_{J}^{W^{\perp}}(v_{J}),0_{J})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle L_{I}^{W}(x),v-L_{J}^{W^{\perp}}(v_{J})\right\rangle=0\,,

where the last equality follows since
L_{I}^{W}(x)\in W
and

v-L_{J}^{W^{\perp}}(v_{j})\in W^{\perp}
.

The inclusion
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{I}+W^{\perp}
follows directly from
z_{J}=-\operatorname{ad}\left(\ell_{J}^{W^{\perp}}\right)(x)\in\pi_{J}(W^{\perp})
by definition of
the adjoint. Thus,
\ell^{W}_{I}=-\operatorname{ad}\left(\ell^{W^{\perp}}_{J}\right)
as needed.

The equality of the positive singular values now follows from

Proposition

2.18
, noting that
\sigma^{+}(\ell^{W}_{I})=\sigma^{+}(-\operatorname{ad}(\ell^{W}_{I}))=\sigma^{+}(\ell^{W^{\perp}}_{J})
. ∎

6.3
Cheap Lift Subspaces

To argue for the usefulness of the SLLS step direction, and to select
suitable subspaces
V
and
U
for a given partition
(B,N)
, we recall the
discussion of the trust region step from the Introduction (
Section

1
).
As long as the step primal and dual directions
(\Delta x,\Delta s)
are
feasible to the systems (
8
) for a suitably small
threshold, we are guaranteed to make progress as measured by the primal and
dual objective values as in (
9
).

Simply selecting
V=W
and
U=W^{\perp}
would attain the smallest possible
objective values; however, the constraints bounding the local norms of

\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B}
and
\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N}
in (
8
) could
be arbitrarily violated. We will select the subspaces
V
and
U
so that

\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B}\right\rVert
and

\left\lVert\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N}\right\rVert
are guaranteed
to be small.

To ensure that the constraints are satisfied, we will restrict the primal and dual movement directions to
cheap lift subspaces

V
and
U
. These are formally defined below:

Definition 6.9

(Cheap Lift Subspace)
.

Let
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
subspace,
\Xi\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{n}
,
(B,N)
be a partition of
[n]
. Then,
V\subseteq W
is a cheap lift subspace for
(W,\Xi,B,N)
with lifting cost

\tau\geq 0
if

\left\lVert\Xi_{B}x_{B}\right\rVert\leq\tau\left\lVert\Xi_{N}x_{N}\right\rVert,\forall x\in V.

Remark 6.10
.

Note that for the above inequality to hold, we must have that
\dim(\pi_{N}(V))=\dim(V)
, since otherwise there exists a vector
x\in V
with
x_{B}\neq\mathbf{0}_{B}
and
x_{N}=\mathbf{0}_{N}
. In particular, if
N=\emptyset
, we must have
V=\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
, which has lifting cost
0
. Furthermore, if
N=[n]
, then
V=W

is a cheap lift subspace for
(W,\Xi,B,N)
with lifting cost
0
of maximum dimension (recall
that
(\Xi x)_{\emptyset}\coloneqq\pi_{\emptyset}(\Xi x)=0
by
convention).

In the context of solving the primal trust-region program, if
V\subseteq W

is a cheap lift subspace with respect to
(W,\hat{x}^{-1},B,N)
with lifting
cost
\tau=\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{n}}
, the primal subspace LLS direction
with respect to
(W,V,N,B)

\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}\coloneqq\argmin_{\Delta x\in V}\left\lVert\hat{x}_{N}^{-1}(x_{N}+\Delta x_{N})\right\rVert

automatically satisfies the trust-region constraint
\left\lVert\hat{x}_{B}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B}\right\rVert\leq\nu
. This is because
\hat{x}_{N}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N}
is the orthogonal
projection of
-\hat{x}_{N}^{-1}x_{N}=-\hat{\xi}_{N}
onto the subspace
\hat{x}_{N}^{-1}\pi_{N}(V)
, and hence

\left\lVert\hat{x}_{N}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\hat{x}_{N}^{-1}x_{N}\right\rVert=\sqrt{\sum_{i\in N}x_{i}s_{i}/\mu}\leq\sqrt{n}
. By the lifting
cost condition on
V
, we then have

\left\lVert\hat{x}_{B}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B}\right\rVert\leq\tau\left\lVert\hat{x}_{N}^{-1}\Delta x_{N}^{\mathrm{\ell}}\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{n}\tau=\nu,

as needed. Analogously, any cheap lift subspace
U\subseteq W^{\perp}
for

(W^{\perp},\hat{s}^{-1},N,B)
with lifting cost at most
\tau
will also suffice for
the dual subspace LLS direction
\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}
as in

Definition

6.1
to be feasible.

While low lifting cost subspaces are sufficient to get feasible solutions to
the trust-region program, they do not necessarily yield useful
approximations. In particular, one can always choose the subspace
V=\{\mathbf{0}\}
, which is trivially cheap. To make significant progress along a
polarized segment, we will require that the use of cheap lift subspaces of

maximum dimension
subject to a lifting cost bound
\tau
. These
will allow us to either quickly increase the dimension of the cheap lift
subspace, or to get past the end of the of the polarized segment.

We now explain how to find these cheap lift subspaces and what their
achievable dimensions are. For this purpose, we will rely on approximate
singular subspaces of lifting operator, as defined in
Definition

6.4
.
The following lemma gives the precise relation between cheap lift subspaces
and approximate singular subspaces of the lifting operator, and will be the
main tool underlying the computation of cheap lift subspaces.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a linear subspace,
\Xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}
,
B\cup N=[n]
be a partition. Let
L^{\Xi W}_{N}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\ell^{\Xi W}_{N}:\pi_{N}(\Xi W)\rightarrow\pi_{B}(\Xi^{-1}W^{\perp})
be the lifting operator and map as in
Definition

6.4
.

Then, the maximum dimension of a cheap lift subspace
V
for
(W,\Xi,B,N)

with lifting cost
\tau\geq 0
is

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\Xi W}_{N},\tau\right)\coloneqq|\{i\in[\dim(\pi_{N}(\Xi W))]:\sigma_{i}(\ell^{\Xi W}_{N})\leq\tau\}|,

the number of singular values of
\ell^{\Xi W}_{N}
of value at most
\tau

as in
Definition

2.16
. Furthermore, if
S\subseteq\pi_{N}(\Xi W)
is a

\varrho
-approximate singular subspace for
\ell^{\Xi W}_{N}
of dimension

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\Xi W}_{N},\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right)
, then

V\coloneqq\Xi^{-1}L^{\Xi W}_{N}(S)

is a cheap lift subspace for
(W,\Xi,B,N)
of dimension

\dim(S)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\Xi W}_{N},\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right)
with lifting cost

\sigma_{1}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell^{\Xi W}_{N}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}}\right)\leq\varrho\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(\Xi W))-\dim(S)+1}(\ell^{\Xi W}_{N})\leq\tau.

Proof.

We first show the upper bound of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\Xi W}_{N},\tau\right)
on the
dimension of any cheap lift subspace. Let
V\subseteq W
be a cheap lift
subpace as above with
\dim(V)=d
, and let
\widehat{V}\coloneqq\Xi V
and

\widehat{W}\coloneqq\Xi W
. For any
y\in\widehat{V}\subseteq\widehat{W}
, by
definition of the lifting map and operator, we have that

L_{N}^{\widehat{W}}(y)\in\widehat{W},\,(L_{N}^{\widehat{W}}(y))_{N}=y_{N},\,\text{ and }\left\lVert y_{B}\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert(L_{N}^{\widehat{W}}(y_{N}))_{B}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N}(y_{N})\right\rVert,

where we note that this
still holds if
(B,N)
is trivial as
y_{\emptyset}:=\pi_{\emptyset}(y)=0
by
convention. Therefore, without loss of generality, we may assume that

\widehat{V}=L^{\widehat{W}}_{N}(\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})),

since this can only decrease lifting cost while maintaining the dimension,
recalling that
\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{V}))=\dim(\widehat{V})
for any cheap lift subspace.
If
d=0
then trivially
d\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{N}^{\widehat{W}},\tau\right)
, and there
is nothing left to prove. So assume
d\geq 1
. Then, since
\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})\subseteq\pi_{N}(\widehat{W})
and
\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{V}))=d
, the lifting cost
of
V
satisfies

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\geq\max_{x\in V,x_{N}\neq\mathbf{0}_{N}}\frac{\left\lVert\Xi_{B}x_{B}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\Xi_{N}x_{N}\right\rVert}=\max_{y\in\widehat{V},y_{N}\neq\mathbf{0}_{N}}\frac{\left\lVert y_{B}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert y_{N}\right\rVert}=\max_{z\in\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{N}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N}(z)\right\rVert}{\left\lVert z\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})}})\geq\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))-d+1}(\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N}),

(60)

where the last inequality follows from (
15
). Since
\tau\geq\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))-d+1}(\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N})
and the singular values
are in non-increasing order, we have that

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=|\{i\in[\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))]:\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))-d+1\leq i\}|

\displaystyle\leq|\{i\in[\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))]:\sigma_{i}(\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N})\leq\tau\}|={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N},\tau\right),

as needed.

For the second part, if
S\subseteq\pi_{N}(\widehat{W})
, then tracing the
reduction of the first part backwards, we immediately get
from (
60
) that
\Xi^{-1}L^{\widehat{W}}_{N}(S)\subseteq W
is a cheap lift subspace for
(W,\Xi,B,N)
of dimension
\dim(S)
and
lifting cost
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})
. If
S
is a

\varrho
-approximate singular subspace of
\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N}
of dimension

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N},\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right)
, then by definition

\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})\leq\varrho\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))-\dim(S)+1}(\ell^{\widehat{W}}_{N})\leq\varrho(\tau/\varrho)=\tau
, as needed.
∎

We remark that so far we have been treating the tasks of finding primal and
dual cheap lift subspaces separately. Fortunately, it turns out the singular
values of the corresponding primal and dual lifting operators are identical.
Thus, the corresponding problems of finding cheap lift subspace on both sides
are intimately linked. In particular, we derive the following relation as a
direct corollary of
Lemma

6.8
.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
\hat{x},\hat{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}
, satisfy
\hat{x}\hat{s}=\mu\mathbf{1}_{n}
where
\mu=\overline{\mu}(\hat{x},\hat{s})
.
Then, for a subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and non-trivial partition
B\cup N=[n]
,
\sigma^{+}(\ell^{\hat{x}^{-1}W}_{N})=\sigma^{+}(\ell^{\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}}_{B})
.
Furthermore, if
\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}u_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
is the SVD of

\ell^{\hat{x}^{-1}W}_{N}
, then
-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}v_{i}u_{i}^{\top}
is the SVD of

\ell^{\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}}_{B}
.

Given the above, computing cheap lift subspaces for either the primal or dual
side is closely tied to the problem of computing an
approximate
SVD of
the lifting operator.

6.4
Computing Cheap Lift Subspaces

In this section, we explain how to compute cheap lift subspaces.

6.4.1
Approximating the Singular Subspaces

To compute cheap lift subspaces, we will rely on a solver for the

\varrho
-
SVD
problem defined below, which provides the requisite
approximate singular value decomposition.

Definition 6.13

(
\varrho
-
SVD
problem)
.

For
\varrho\geq 1
, the input to the
\varrho
-
SVD
problem is a matrix

\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, and the output is an
orthogonal
basis
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
,
s
sorted in non-increasing
order, such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{M}x\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq s_{i}\left\lVert x\right\rVert,\forall x\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq i}),\forall i\in[n]\,,\quad\mbox{and}

(61)

\displaystyle\varrho^{-1}s

\displaystyle\leq\sigma(\mathbf{M})\leq s\,.

(62)

Remark 6.14
.

The requirement that
s\geq\sigma(\mathbf{M})
in (
62
) is
automatically satisfied given (
61
)
and (
15
). Furthermore, the requirement that
s
be sorted
in non-increasing order is without loss of generality: if
s
satisfies the
requirements (
61
) and (
62
), then
\bar{s}
defined by
\bar{s}_{i}=\min_{j\in[i]}s_{j}
,
\forall i\in[n]
, also satisfies the requirements and is non-increasing.

Remark 6.15
.

The guarantees of
\varrho
-
SVD
imply that for each
i\in[m]
, the
subspace
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq i})
is a
\varrho
-approximate singular subspace of

\mathbf{M}
(i.e., of the operator
\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{M})
) of dimension
n-i+1
. In
particular, given a threshold
\tau\geq 0
, letting
c_{\tau}=|\{i\in[n]:s_{i}\leq\tau\}|
, the subspace
S_{\tau}=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq n-c_{\tau}+1})
is
the subspace of largest dimension in this collection that satisfies

\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathbf{M}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S_{\tau}}})\leq\tau
under the guarantee of (
61
).

For the sake of being able to execute each iteration of our IPM in strongly
polynomial time, we will need a
\varrho
-
SVD
solver that uses a
strongly polynomial number of basic arithmetic operations (i.e., depending
only on the dimension of the underlying matrix), and that is numerically
stable, i.e., that ensures iterates with polynomial bit complexity. In
particular, the algorithm cannot rely on computing square root computations.

In
Section

9
, we show that for any fixed
\varrho>1
,

\varrho
-
SVD
can be solved in deterministic strongly polynomial time.
Our main theorem is as follows.

Theorem 6.16
.

For
\varrho>1
, there is a deterministic algorithm for solving

\varrho
-
SVD
that on an input
m\times n
matrix requires
O(n^{2}\max(m,n)^{3}\log(n+\frac{1}{\varrho-1}))
operations and space polynomial in

n,m,\max\{1,\frac{1}{\varrho-1}\}
and the bit-encoding length of the input matrix.

We defer the discussion of how this theorem is proved and how it relates to
earlier work on approximate SVDs to
Section

9
.

Our IPM can be implemented using any
\varrho
-
SVD
solver (modulo
strongly polynomial considerations). While the iteration complexity of the IPM does
depend on the approximation factor
\varrho
, this dependency is very mild: as
we show in
Section

7.3
, the iteration bound of our IPM will depend
only logarithmically on the approximation factor. The guarantees of our IPM in
Theorem

1.4
will be derived
by using the above for
\varrho=2
.

6.4.2
The Cheap Lift Subspace Algorithm

Input :
Matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A})=m
,
\Xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}
, partition
B\cup N=[n]
and SVD approximation factor
\varrho\geq 1
.

Output :
Matrices
\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{p}},\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m_{d}}
,
m_{p},m_{d}\in[n]
. Letting
W=\ker(\mathbf{A})
and
\ell=\ell^{\Xi W}_{N}
,
\ell^{\perp}=\ell^{\Xi^{-1}W^{\perp}}_{B}
, the subspaces
V=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P})
,
U=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})
satisfy:

[rightmargin=1cm]

1.

W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{N}\subseteq V
and
W^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{B}\subseteq U
.

2.

V
is a cheap lift subspace for
(W,\Xi,B,N)
with lifting cost
\varrho\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-\dim(V)+1}(\ell)
, and

U
is a cheap lift subspace for
(W^{\perp},\Xi^{-1},N,B)
with lifting cost

\varrho\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{B}(W^{\perp}))-\dim(U)+1}(\ell^{\perp})
.

3.

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,\tfrac{1}{\varrho}\right)\leq\dim(V)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,1\right)
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\perp},\tfrac{1}{\varrho}\right)\leq\dim(U)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\perp},1\right)
.

//
Compute Primal and Dual Projection Maps

1

\mathbf{P}^{\perp}\leftarrow\operatorname{diag}(\Xi^{-1})\mathbf{A}^{\top}(\mathbf{A}\operatorname{diag}(\Xi^{-2})\mathbf{A}^{\top})^{-1}\mathbf{A}\operatorname{diag}(\Xi^{-1})
;
\mathbf{P}\leftarrow\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{P}^{\perp}
;

2

3

if

B=\emptyset

then

4

(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P},\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})\leftarrow(\mathbf{P},[\mathbf{0}_{n}])
;

5

else if

N=\emptyset

then

6

(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P},\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})\leftarrow([\mathbf{0}_{n}],\mathbf{P}^{\perp})
;

7

else

8

//
Compute Primal and Dual Lifting Maps and Operators

9

(\bar{L},\bar{L}^{\perp})\leftarrow(\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,N}(\mathbf{P}_{N,N})^{+},\mathbf{P}^{\perp}_{\bullet,B}(\mathbf{P}^{\perp}_{B,B})^{+})
, associated matrices of lifting maps
L^{\Xi W}_{N}
,
L^{\Xi^{-1}W^{\perp}}_{B}
;

10

(\bar{\ell},\bar{\ell}^{\perp})\leftarrow(\bar{L}_{B,\bullet},\bar{L}^{\perp}_{N,\bullet})
, associated matrices of
lifting operators
\ell^{\Xi W}_{N}
,
\ell^{\Xi^{-1}W^{\perp}}_{B}
;

11

12

//
Approximate SVDs of
\bar{\ell}
and
\bar{\ell}^{\perp}

13

(\bar{\mathbf{C}},\bar{s})

\leftarrow

\varrho
-
SVD
on matrix
\bar{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times N}
;
(\bar{\mathbf{C}}^{\perp},\bar{s}^{\perp})

\leftarrow

\varrho
-
SVD
on matrix
\bar{\ell}^{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times B}
;

14

15

//
Number of Small Primal and Dual Singular Values

16

\bar{c}_{p}\leftarrow|\{i\in[|N|]:\bar{s}_{i}\leq 1\}|
;
\bar{c}_{d}\leftarrow|\{i\in[|B|]:\bar{s}_{i}^{\perp}\leq 1\}|
;

17

18

//
Generators of Primal and Dual Cheap Lift Subspaces

19

(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P},\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})\leftarrow(\Xi^{-1}\bar{L}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-\bar{c}_{p}+1}),\Xi\bar{L}^{\perp}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}^{\perp}_{\geq|B|-\bar{c}_{d}+1}))
;

20

21

return
(
\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}
,
\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}
);

22

Algorithm 1

Cheap-Lift-Subspaces

We now overview the
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
algorithm. At a high level, the
algorithm is a direct algorithmic implementation of
Lemma

6.11

applied to both the primal and dual separately. Specifically, it computes the
primal and dual lifting operators, followed by approximate singular subspaces
associated with the singular values smaller than
1
, and then lifts them to
build the cheap lift subspaces. One complication however is that the
approximate
\varrho
-
SVD
solver directly computes approximate
singular subspaces for the associated matrices of the lifting operators and
not the lifting operators themselves. The difference here being that the
computed subspaces do not necessarily reside in the input spaces of the
lifting operators. Fortunately, the lifting maps automatically correct for
this discrepancy as they first project onto the input space. In
the proof of correctness, we justify that these projections yield the desired approximate singular subspaces using
Lemma

2.26
.

Remark 6.17
.

On line
1
of
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
, recall that by
convention, if
\bar{c}_{p}=0
then
\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-c_{p}+1}=\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\emptyset}=[\mathbf{0}_{B}]
. In particular,
\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}=\Xi^{-1}\bar{L}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-\bar{c}_{p}+1})=[\mathbf{0}_{n}]
. Similarly, if
\bar{c}_{d}=0
, then
\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}=\Xi\bar{L}^{\perp}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}^{\perp}_{\geq|B|-\bar{c}_{d}+1})=[\mathbf{0}_{n}]
. In a similar vein, the checks on whether
B
or
N
are empty are not strictly speaking necessary. It can be verified that the output of the algorithm is the same without these checks, however we have decided to include them for the sake of clarity.

Remark 6.18
.

One may wonder why
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
puts the singular value
cutoff at
1
instead of
\nu/\sqrt{n}
,
\nu=O(\beta)
, as described in

Section

6.1
. As the analysis will show, we will only need the
cheap lift subspaces when there is a large multiplicative gap between the
singular values less than
1
and greater than
1
. In particular, all the
singular values less than
1
in this case will be significantly smaller than
the threshold
\nu/\sqrt{n}
.

Remark 6.19
.

Given the duality lifting operators it is natural to wonder whether the cheap
lift subspace
V
for
(W,\Xi,B,N)
outputted by

1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
can be be transformed into a cheap
lift subspace
U
for
(W^{\perp},\Xi^{-1},N,B)
satisfying the output
conditions (and vice versa). Assuming
\Xi=\mathbf{1}_{n}
for simplicity, one can
in fact show that setting
U=L_{B}^{W^{\perp}}((\ell_{B}^{W^{\perp}})^{+}(\pi_{N}(V)))+(W^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{B})
indeed satisfies the desired conditions. For the sake of
keeping the analysis and description of the algorithm as simple as possible however, we have opted to avoid using and proving this relation here.

We now prove correctness of
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
and give its running
time guarantee.

Lemma 6.20
.

Algorithm
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
is correct. Furthermore, assuming a
strongly polynomial solver for
\varrho
-
SVD
,
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces

runs in time strongly polynomial
n
.

Proof.

The strong polynomiality claim is immediate from the assumption on the

\varrho
-
SVD
solver and the strong polynomiality of
computing pseudoinverses (
Proposition

2.15
). We thus focus on
correctness.

For correctness, we must show that the outputted matrices
\mathbf{M}^{\rm P},\mathbf{M}^{\rm D}
and
correspondings subspaces
V=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P})
,
U=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})
satisfy the output
conditions (
1
), (
2
), (
3
) and that
m_{p},m_{d}\in[n]
.

By
Proposition

2.15
, on line
1
the algorithm
computes the orthogonal projection matrices
\mathbf{P}^{\perp},\mathbf{P}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\mathbf{P}^{\perp}
onto
\Xi^{-1}\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}^{\top})=\Xi^{-1}W^{\perp}
and

(\Xi^{-1}W^{\perp})^{\perp}=\Xi W=\Xi\ker(\mathbf{A})
, respectively.

After this line, the algorithm is completely symmetric for the primal and
dual, so we restrict to
proving (
1
), (
2
), (
3
) for the
primal subspace
V
and proving
m_{p}\in[n]
, recalling the shorthand
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{N}^{\Xi W}
.

Let us first assume that
(B,N)
is a trivial partition. If
B=\emptyset
,
then
N=[n]
,
V=\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{P})=W=W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{N}
and
m_{p}=\dim(W)\in[n]
, which
verifies (
1
) and the requirement on
m_{p}
. Furthermore,
since
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{[n]}^{W}:W\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{\emptyset}
, we have

\dim(V)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,0\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,1/\varrho\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,1\right)
, which
verifies (
3
). The lifting cost condition (
2
) also
holds trivially. If
N=\emptyset
, then
V=\operatorname{im}([\mathbf{0}_{n}])=\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}=W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\emptyset}
and
m_{p}=1
, which verifies (
1
) and the
requirement on
m_{p}
. From here, since
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{\emptyset}^{W}:\mathbb{R}^{\emptyset}\rightarrow W^{\perp}
, we have
\dim(V)=0={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,\mathbb{R}^{+}\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,1/\varrho\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,1\right)
, which verifies (
3
).
Again, the lifting cost condition (
2
) is trivial.

Now assume that
(B,N)
is non-trivial. Then by
Proposition

6.5
,

\bar{L}
computed on line
1
equals
\mathcal{M}(L_{N}^{\Xi W})
and
\bar{\ell}
computed on
line
1
equals
\mathcal{M}(\ell_{N}^{\Xi W})
, the
associated matrix of the primal lifting map and operator.

Let
(\bar{\mathbf{C}},\bar{s})
be the
\varrho
-SVD of
\bar{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times N}
as
computed on line
1
and let
\bar{c}_{p}=|\{i\in[N]:\bar{s}_{i}\leq 1\}|
be as computed on line
1
. By the guarantees of
\varrho
-
SVD
, we have that
\sigma(\bar{\ell})\leq\sqrt{\bar{s}}\leq\varrho\sigma(\bar{\ell})
, and therefore

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{\ell},1/\varrho\right)\leq\bar{c}_{p}\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{\ell},1\right).

(63)

Similarly, letting
\bar{k}_{p}\coloneqq\dim(\ker(\bar{\ell}))={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{\ell},0\right)
,
we have that

\sigma(\bar{\ell})_{[|N|]\setminus[|N|-\bar{k}_{p}]}=\bar{s}_{[|N|]\setminus[|N|-\bar{k}_{p}]}=\mathbf{0}_{[|N|]\setminus[|N|-\bar{k}_{p}]}.

By the guarantees of
\varrho
-
SVD
, this implies that

\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-\bar{k}_{p}+1})=\ker(\bar{\ell}),

(64)

since
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\bar{\ell}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-\bar{k}_{p}+1})}})=0
and
\dim(\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-\bar{k}_{p}+1}))=\bar{k}_{p}=\dim(\ker(\bar{\ell}))
.

Since
\bar{c}_{p}\geq\bar{k}_{p}
we get that
\bar{V}_{N}\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-\bar{c}_{p}+1})\supseteq\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq|N|-\bar{k}_{p}+1})=\ker(\bar{\ell})
is a
\varrho
-approximate singular subspace for
\bar{\ell}

of dimension
\bar{c}_{p}
containing
\ker(\bar{\ell})
. Let us use the shorthand
X\coloneqq\pi_{N}(\Xi W)
and
Y\coloneqq\pi_{B}(\Xi^{-1}W^{\perp})
, recalling that
\ell\colon X\rightarrow Y
. Defining
\widehat{V}_{N}\coloneqq\Pi_{X}(\bar{V}_{N})
, by
Lemma

2.26
we have
that
\widehat{V}_{N}
is a
\varrho
-approximate
singular subspace for
\ell
containing
\ker(\ell)
of dimension
\bar{c}_{p}-\dim(X^{\perp})
. Again
by
Lemma

2.26
, for
\tau\geq 0
we have
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,\tau\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{\ell},\tau\right)-\dim(X^{\perp})
, and hence we conclude
that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,1/\varrho\right)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{\ell},1/\varrho\right)-\dim(X^{\perp})\leq\bar{c}_{p}-\dim(X^{\perp})=\dim(\widehat{V}_{N})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{\ell},1\right)-\dim(X^{\perp})={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell,1\right).

(65)

By line
1
, recall that
\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}=\Xi^{-1}\bar{L}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq n-\bar{c}_{p}+1})
. The requirement
m_{p}\in[n]
, then follows from

m_{p}=\max\{1,\bar{c}_{p}\}\in[|N|]\subseteq[n]
. Recalling that

L_{N}^{\Xi W}(\Pi_{X}(\cdot))=L_{N}^{\Xi W}(\cdot)
by definition and
that
\bar{L}=\mathcal{M}(L_{N}^{\Xi W})
, we have that

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})=\Xi^{-1}L_{N}^{\Xi W}(\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq n-\bar{c}_{p}+1}))=\Xi^{-1}L_{N}^{\Xi W}(\Pi_{X}\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{C}}_{\geq n-\bar{c}_{p}+1}))

\displaystyle=\Xi^{-1}L_{N}^{\Xi W}(\widehat{V}_{N}).

(66)

By
Lemma

6.11
, we then get that
V
is cheap lift subspace for

(W,\Xi,B,N)
of dimension
\dim(V)=\dim(\widehat{V}_{N})
with lifting cost at
most

\varrho\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(V)+1}(\ell)=\varrho\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-\dim(V)+1}(\ell).

Together with (
65
) this
establishes (
2
), (
3
) for
V
. By

Lemma

6.7
, we have that
\pi_{N}(\Xi W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{N})=\ker(\ell)\subseteq\widehat{V}_{N}
and therefore

W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{N}=\Xi^{-1}L_{N}^{\Xi W}(\pi_{N}(\Xi W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{N}))\subseteq\Xi^{-1}L_{N}^{\Xi W}(\widehat{V}_{N})=V,

which verifies (
1
).
∎

7
The Subspace Layered Least Squares Algorithm

In this section, we present our SLLS based IPM (
2SLLS-IPM
)
and give the proof of
Theorem

1.1
from the introduction.
The pseudocode for our IPM is provided in
Section

7.1
, and its correctness is proved in
Section

7.2
. In

Section

7.3
, we bound the number of iterations needed to
traverse a polarized segment of the central path from which

Theorem

1.1
follows directly. We note that the iteration
bound depends only logarithmically on the SVD approximation factor
\varrho
. A
more fine-grained amortized iteration bound is deferred to

Section

8
.

1

Input :
Instance of (
LP
) with primal constraint matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n},\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{A})=m
, and initial iterate
(x^{0},s^{0})\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
, and SVD approximation factor
\varrho\geq 1
.

Output :

(x^{\star},s^{\star},v^{\star},w^{\star})
satisfying:

[rightmargin=1cm]

1.

x^{\star}\in\mathcal{P},s^{\star}\in\mathcal{D}
,
\left\langle x^{\star},s^{\star}\right\rangle=0
.

2.

B\coloneqq\{i\in[n]:x^{\star}_{i}>0\}
,
N\coloneqq\{i\in[n]:s^{\star}_{i}>0\}
satisfy
B\cup N=[n]
.

3.

v^{\star}\in W^{\perp}\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}),v^{\star}_{B}>\mathbf{0}_{B}
,
w^{\star}\in W\coloneqq\ker(\mathbf{A})
,
w^{\star}_{N}>\mathbf{0}_{N}
.

4.

\left\lVert(x^{\star}_{B}v^{\star}_{B},s^{\star}_{N}w^{\star}_{N})-\mathbf{1}_{n}\right\rVert\leq\beta
.

(x,s)\leftarrow(x^{0},s^{0})
;

2

while

\overline{\mu}(x,s)>0

do

3

Compute corrector direction
(\Delta x^{c},\Delta s^{c})
at
(x,s)
;

4

(x,s)\leftarrow(x+\Delta x^{c},s+\Delta s^{c})
;

5

Compute affine scaling direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
at
(x,s)
;

6

Set
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}
for
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
according to
Proposition

2.39
with parameter
\beta
;

7

\widetilde{B}\leftarrow\left\{i\in[n]:\left|\frac{\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}}{x_{i}}\right|<\left|\frac{\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}}{s_{i}}\right|\right\},\widetilde{N}\leftarrow[n]\setminus\widetilde{B}
;

8

(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm P},\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm D})\leftarrow\operatorname{Cheap-Lift-Subspaces}(\mathbf{A},\frac{1}{x},\widetilde{B},\widetilde{N},\varrho)
;

9

(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{U})\leftarrow(\operatorname{im}(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm P}),\operatorname{im}(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm D}))
;

10

Compute the subspace LLS direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
at
(x,s)
with respect to
(\widetilde{B},\widetilde{N},\widetilde{V},\widetilde{U})
using
Proposition

6.3
on input
(x,s,\widetilde{B},\widetilde{N},\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm P},\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\rm D})
;

11

Set
\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}
for
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
according to
Proposition

2.44
with parameters
\mu=\overline{\mu}(x,s)
,
\beta
;

12

if

\overline{\mu}((x+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},s+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}))<\overline{\mu}((x+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},s+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}))

then

13

(x,s)\leftarrow(x+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},s+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
;

14

else

15

(x,s)\leftarrow(x+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},s+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
;

16

17

(x^{\star},s^{\star})\leftarrow(x,s)
;

18

\mu\leftarrow\left\langle x^{\star}-\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},s^{\star}-\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}\right\rangle/n
;

19

(v^{\star},w^{\star})\leftarrow(-\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}/\mu,-\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}/\mu)
;

20

return

(x^{\star},s^{\star},v^{\star},w^{\star})
;

21

Algorithm 2

SLLS-IPM

7.1
Description of the Algorithm

We are ready to describe the predictor-corrector algorithm

2SLLS-IPM
, shown in Algorithm
2
. We
are given a starting iterate
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
. In each
iteration, we compute first compute a corrector step to move
z
into the

\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)
neighborhood. We then compute the affine scaling
direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
at
z
and identify the associated
partition
(\widetilde{B}_{z},\widetilde{N}_{z})
. Using this partition, we compute cheap lift
subspaces
\widetilde{V}
and
\widetilde{U}
using
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
(Algorithm
1
) from
Section

6.4
.

We then compute the subspace LLS direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})

for
(\widetilde{B},\widetilde{N},\widetilde{V},\widetilde{U})
. For both directions, we then compute the
feasible step-lengths according to the bounds in

Proposition

2.37
and
Proposition

2.44
, and use the better
of these two possible steps to obtain the next iterate.

Once the algorithm has found optimal solutions
(x^{\star},s^{\star})\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
, that is, satisfying
\overline{\mu}(x^{\star},s^{\star})=0
, it uses the direction of the last segment of the central
path to compute certificates
(v^{\star},w^{\star})
that certify that the optimal solutions
are close to the analytic centers of the respective optimal faces.

Remark 7.1

(Solving Linear Programs to Target Accuracy
\varepsilon\geq 0
)
.

One can easily modify
2SLLS-IPM
to terminate once it has
found a primal and dual solution
(x,s)
with gap
\left\langle x,s\right\rangle\leq\varepsilon
,
where
\varepsilon\geq 0
is the target accuracy. This is achieved by changing the
while loop check on line
2
from
\overline{\mu}((x,s))>0
to

\left\langle x,s\right\rangle>\varepsilon
. Once the while loop terminates, if
\varepsilon>0
, we immediately return

(x,s)\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
and skip the computation of the additional
certificates
(v^{\star},w^{\star})
. If
\varepsilon=0
, the IPM remains unchanged.
We prove approximately optimal iteration bounds for any target gap
\varepsilon\geq 0
in
Section

7.3
, and give more refined amortized
bounds in
Section

8
.

Remark 7.2

(Choice of Cheap Lift Subspace Scaling)
.

One may wonder why we use the scaling
1/x
instead of the
normalized scaling
1/\hat{x}\coloneqq\sqrt{\frac{s}{x\overline{\mu}(x,s)}}
in the call

\operatorname{Cheap-Lift-Subspaces}(\mathbf{A},\frac{1}{x},\widetilde{B},\widetilde{N},\varrho)
on
line
2
, which would have been more adapted to the
definition of the SLLS direction computed on
line
2
. The main reason is to avoid the need to
compute square roots within the algorithm, which is not a strongly
polynomial operation. Relying on the fact that
x\approx\hat{x}
(see

Proposition

2.42
), this choice of rescaling is safe and has little additional
impact on the analysis or the iteration bound.

7.2
Correctness

In this subsection, we prove that upon termination, the

2SLLS-IPM
satisfies its output requirements, namely it
outputs optimal primal and dual solutions that are close to the analytic
centers of the corresponding optimal faces. This will mainly depend on the
guarantees on the computed step lengths for subspace LLS and affine scaling,
which are given in
Proposition

2.44
and

Proposition

2.39
.

Lemma 7.3
.

The output of
2SLLS-IPM
is correct. Furthermore,
each iteration of the algorithm runs in strongly polynomial time.

Proof.

We first show that the output
(x^{\star},s^{\star},v^{\star},w^{\star})
satisfies
the
requirements (
1
), (
2
),(
3
),(
4
)
listed in the output description.

To argue this, we first claim that during the last iteration of the while
loop, either the affine scaling step length
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}
or the subspace LLS step length
\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}
equals
1
. Firstly, by the assumption that

\overline{\mu}(x_{0},s_{0})>0
, the algorithm enters the while loop. Secondly, for any
iterate
(x,s)
, by
Proposition

2.37
part
(i)
and
(ii)
, the corrector
step leaves
\overline{\mu}(x,s)
unchanged, whereas the affine scaling step satisfies

\overline{\mu}(x+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},s+\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})=(1-\alpha^{\mathrm{a}})\overline{\mu}(x,s)
. Similarly, by
Proposition

2.44
,

9/8(1-\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}})\overline{\mu}(x,s)\geq\overline{\mu}(x+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},s+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})\geq 7/8(1-\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}})\overline{\mu}(x,s)
. Thus, the only way to exit the loop,
corresponding to the condition
\overline{\mu}(x,s)=0
, is if
\max\{\alpha^{\mathrm{a}},\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\}=1
.

We now further claim that upon exiting the while loop we have
\max\{\alpha^{\mathrm{a}},\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\}=\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}=1
. For this purpose, let
(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)

be the iterate computed on line
2
during the last
iteration of the while loop. From here, let
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})
,
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
, be the affine scaling
and subspace LLS directions computed at
(x,s)
, and let
(\widetilde{B},\widetilde{N})
,

(\widetilde{V},\widetilde{U})
be the corresponding associated partition and cheap lift
subspaces at
(x,s)
. We now claim that if
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}=1
, then the affine
scaling and subspace LLS directions are equal. Assuming
\alpha^{\mathrm{a}}=1
, we have that
(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{D}
is an optimal primal-dual pair. In
particular,
\mathbf{0}_{n}=(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}})(s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})=\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}
, where the first equality is by complementary slackness and the second
equality is by the defining equation for the affine scaling
direction (
20
). Recalling that
\widetilde{B}=\{i\in[n]:|\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|/x_{i}<|\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}}_{i}|/s_{i}\}
,
\widetilde{N}=[n]\setminus\widetilde{B}
and that
x,s>\mathbf{0}_{n}
, we conclude that

\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}}=(\mathbf{0}_{\widetilde{B}},-x_{\widetilde{N}})\in W\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\widetilde{N}}
and
\Delta s=(-s_{\widetilde{B}},\mathbf{0}_{\widetilde{N}})\in W^{\perp}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\widetilde{B}}
. Therefore by
the output guarantee (
1
) of
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
, we have
that
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})\in\widetilde{V}\times\widetilde{U}
. Since

\left\lVert(x_{\widetilde{N}}+x_{\widetilde{N}}^{\mathrm{a}},s_{\widetilde{B}}+s^{\mathrm{a}}_{\widetilde{B}})\right\rVert=0
, we must have that

(x^{\mathrm{a}},s^{\mathrm{a}})\in\widetilde{V}\times\widetilde{U}
are the (unique) optimal solutions to the subspace LLS programs (
53
) and (
54
) at

(x,s)
with respect to
(\widetilde{B},\widetilde{N},\widetilde{V},\widetilde{U})
. In particular,
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{a}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{a}})=(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
, as claimed. By
Proposition

2.44
, using that
0=\left\lVert(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}})(s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})\right\rVert=\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}\right\rVert
, we get
\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}=1
, as needed.

Let
(x^{\star},s^{\star})
be the iterate defined right after the while loop on
line
2
, and let
B=\operatorname{supp}(x^{\star})
and
N=\operatorname{supp}(s^{\star})
. Since
\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}=1
by the above, we have that

(x^{\star},s^{\star})=(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},s+\Delta^{\mathrm{\ell}})\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{D}
and

\overline{\mu}(x^{\star},s^{\star})=0
. Therefore,
(x^{\star},s^{\star})
is an optimal primal-dual pair, which proves (
1
).

Let
\mu
be as defined on line
2
. By the above,
\mu=\overline{\mu}(x,s)
. By the guarantees of
Proposition

2.44
used on
line
2
, we must have that
\left\lVert\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}\right\rVert\leq\beta\mu/9
and
\left\lVert(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}})(s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})\right\rVert=0
. For
i\in[n]
, using that
|\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i}|\leq\beta\mu/9<(1-\beta/2)\mu\leq x_{i}s_{i}

(
Proposition

2.31
) and
(x_{i}+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i})(s_{i}+\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i})=0
, we conclude that either
x^{\star}_{i}=x_{i}+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i}>0
and
s^{\star}_{i}=s_{i}+\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i}=0
, and thus

i\in B
, or that
x^{\star}_{i}=x_{i}+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i}=0
and

s^{\star}_{i}=s_{i}+\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{i}>0
, and thus
i\in N
. In
particular, we see that
(B,N)
partitions
[n]
. This verifies the output
guarantee (
2
).

Given the above, for
(v^{\star},w^{\star})\coloneqq(-\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}/\mu,-\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}/\mu)\in W^{\perp}\times W
as
set on line
2
, we have that
v^{\star}_{B}=s_{B}/\mu>\mathbf{0}_{B}
and

u^{\star}_{N}=x_{N}/\mu>\mathbf{0}_{N}
which verifies (
3
). We now establish (
4
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lVert(x^{\star}_{B}v^{\star}_{B},s^{\star}_{N}w^{\star}_{N})-\mathbf{1}_{n}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{((x_{B}+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B})s_{B},(s_{N}+\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N})x_{N})}{\mu}-\mathbf{1}_{n}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{xs}{\mu}-\mathbf{1}_{n}-\frac{\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}}{\mu}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\frac{xs}{\mu}-\mathbf{1}_{n}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}}{\mu}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\beta/2+\beta/9\leq\beta.

(67)

For the strongly polynomial bound on each iteration, we must simply show that
all the computations performed within the while loop are strongly polynomial.
In particular, we must check the corrector, affine scaling and subspace LLS
steps can all be computed in strongly polynomial, and that the calls to

1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
run in strongly polynomial time (under the
assumption that the
\varrho
-
SVD
solver is strongly polynomial).
These claims are verified in
Proposition

2.39
(corrector and
affine scaling),
Proposition

6.3
(subspace LLS
direction),
Proposition

2.44
(subspace LLS step length) and

Lemma

6.20
(
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
). Thus, each
iteration runs in strongly polynomial time as needed.
∎

7.3
Bounding the Number of Iterations to Traverse a Polarized Segment

In this section, we prove the following bound.

Theorem 7.4
.

For
\mu_{0}>\mu_{1}\geq 0
, let
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
be
\gamma
-polarized for
\gamma\in(0,1]
. Then,
given an iterate
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
, with parameter
\overline{\mu}(z)\in(\mu_{1},\mu_{0})
, the algorithm
2SLLS-IPM
(Algorithm

2
) takes
O\left(\tfrac{n^{1.5}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\right)
many
iterations to find
z^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
such that
\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\mu_{1}
.

Together with
Theorem

3.5
, we obtain the proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

As in the statement of the theorem,
let
\Gamma\colon(\mu_{1},\mu_{0})\to\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{-\infty}(\theta)

be any piecewise linear curve
satisfying
\overline{\mu}\left(\Gamma(\mu)\right)=\mu
,
\forall\mu\in(\mu_{1},\mu_{0})
with
T
linear segments. According to
Theorem

3.5
, the segment
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
of the central path can be
decomposed into
T
segments that are

\frac{(1-\theta)^{2}}{16n^{3}}
-polarized.
By
Theorem

7.4
,
2SLLS-IPM
instantiated with the

2
-
SVD
solver (i.e.,
\varrho=2
) from
Theorem

6.16
traverses these segments in

O\left(\tfrac{n^{1.5}}{\beta}\log\left(\tfrac{n}{\beta(1-\theta)}\right)T\right)
iterations.
∎

The stronger form
Theorem

1.4
will be shown in
Section
8
by amortizing the running time estimates over
subsequent polarized segments. We now introduce the main potential for the analysis.

Our main focus will be on the analyzing the evolution of the
singular values of the lifting operator in the normalized subspace at the
current iterate. We define
z\coloneqq(x,s)
to be a
basic iterate

if it either corresponds to an iterate computed just after the corrector step
on line
2
during the course of the while loop of

2SLLS-IPM
, in which case
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)
, or if it
corresponds to the final iterate
z\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
after the end of the
while loop.

For a basic iterate
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)
with
\overline{\mu}(z)\in[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
, we
use the shorthands

\ell_{z}\coloneqq\ell_{N}^{\hat{x}^{-1}W}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad{\ell}_{z}^{\perp}\coloneqq\ell_{B}^{\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}}\,

to denote the primal and dual lifting operators with respect to
(B,N)
in
the respective normalized subspaces, as well as
\sigma(\ell_{z})
to be the vector of
singular values of the lifting operator. If
\mu_{1}=0
and
z\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
is the basic iterate with
\overline{\mu}(z)=0
, let us by convention
additionally define
\ell_{z}
to be the zero operator
\pi_{N}(W)
to

\pi_{B}(W^{\perp})
and
\ell_{z}^{\perp}
to be the zero operator from

\pi_{B}(W^{\perp})
to
\pi_{N}(W)
. Recall that
\sigma^{+}(\ell_{z})=\sigma^{+}(\ell_{z}^{\perp})
by
Lemma

6.8
, that is, the non-zero
singular values of
\ell_{z}
and
\ell_{z}^{\perp}
are identical. Define the
parameter

\tau\coloneqq\frac{\beta}{256n}\,.

The combinatorial potential we use to measure progress on the
polarized segment is

\zeta(z)\coloneqq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell^{\perp}_{z},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\infty)\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\infty)\right)\coloneqq\left|\left\{i\geq 1:\sigma_{i}(\ell_{z})>\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right\}\right|,\,

(68)

the number of singular values of
\ell_{z}
(and
\ell_{z}^{\perp}
) that are
larger than the threshold
\frac{\tau}{\varrho}
. Note that if

\zeta(z)>0
, then

\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})=\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell^{\perp}_{z})=\min\left\{\sigma_{i}(\ell_{z}):\sigma_{i}(\ell_{z})>\frac{\tau}{\varrho},i\geq 1\right\}\,.

By convention, recall that if
B=\emptyset
, then
\ell_{z}
is the unique
linear operator from
W
to
\{0\}
, and if
N=\emptyset
, then
\ell_{z}

if the unique linear operator from
\{0\}
to
W^{\perp}
. In particular,
(B,N)
is a trivial partition, then
\zeta(z)=0
. The key property of

2SLLS-IPM
is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
,
\overline{\mu}(z)\in(\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
, be a basic iterate
on the polarized segment
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
with a polarization partition
B\cup N=[n]
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{\rm it}\geq 1
, such that for any
basic iterate
z^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
computed after at least
\lfloor C_{\rm it}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\rfloor

iterations from
z
, either
\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\mu_{1}
or
\mu_{1}<\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\overline{\mu}(z)
and
\zeta(z^{\prime})<\zeta(z)
.

Remark 7.6
.

In the above lemma, if
\zeta(z)=0
, then
z^{\prime}
must pass the end of
segment
(\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
, since the potential is
\zeta(z^{\prime})
a
non-negative integer. For convenience, if
z^{*}
denotes the final optimal iterate, we define the
next basic iterate after
z^{*}
to be
z^{*}
itself. In this way,

Lemma

7.5
still directly applies if we reach
z^{*}
before

\lfloor C_{\rm it}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\rfloor

iterations after
z
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

The potential
\zeta(z)\leq\mathrm{rank}(\ell_{z})\leq n
at
the start of the segment and decreases by
1
every

\lceil C_{\rm it}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\rceil

iterations while we remain in the segment by
Lemma

7.5
.
∎

The rest of the section is dedicated to the proof of
Lemma

7.5
. We
will require the following technical lemma which describes the evolution of
the singular values of the lifting operator along a polarized segment. The
proof is deferred to
Section

7.3.1
.

Lemma 7.7

(Stability of singular values on polarized segments)
.

Let
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
be a
\gamma
-polarized segment of the central path with partition
B\cup N=[n]
. Let
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
z^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
, such that
\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z)
and
\mu^{\prime}\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})
satisfy
\mu_{0}\geq\mu\geq\mu^{\prime}\geq\mu_{1}
. Then we have:

\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4n^{2}}\cdot\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{\mu}\sigma(\ell_{z})\leq\sigma(\ell_{z^{\prime}})\leq\frac{4n^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\cdot\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{\mu}\sigma(\ell_{z}).

(69)

7.7
asserts that, up to
\operatorname{poly}(n/\gamma)
factors, the singular values
scale
down
by a
\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{\mu}
factor. Recalling that the normalized
subspaces for
z,z^{\prime}
are
\widehat{W}=\hat{x}^{-1}W
and
\widehat{W}^{\prime}=\hat{x}^{{}^{\prime}-1}W
, where
\hat{x}\coloneqq\sqrt{x\mu/s}\approx x
and
\hat{x}^{\prime}\coloneqq\sqrt{x^{\prime}\mu^{\prime}/s^{\prime}}\approx x^{\prime}
, the above relation follows
straightforwardly from the polarization guarantee
(x_{B},x_{B}^{\prime})\approx x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{B}(\mu_{0})
and
(x_{N},x^{\prime}_{N})\approx(\frac{\mu}{\mu_{0}}x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{N}(\mu_{0}),\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{\mu_{0}}x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{N}(\mu_{0}))
(up to
\operatorname{poly}(n/\gamma)

multiplicative factors) together with the variational characterization of
singular values.

The proof of
Lemma

7.5
relies on the following concepts.
For a segment
\mathrm{CP}[\mu^{\prime},\mu]
,
0\leq\mu^{\prime}\leq\mu
of the central
path, we say that
2SLLS-IPM
traverses this segment in
at most
T\geq 0
iterations, if the number of iterations from the first
iterate
z^{\rm start}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
with
\overline{\mu}(z^{\rm start})\leq\mu
to the first iterate
z^{\rm end}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
with
\overline{\mu}(z^{\rm end})\leq\mu^{\prime}
is at most
T
. Important to the analysis is the distinction
between
long
and
short
segments. We say that
\mathrm{CP}[\mu^{\prime},\mu]
is

long
if
\mu/\mu^{\prime}\geq\operatorname{poly}(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})
and
short
otherwise, where
\gamma\in(0,1]
is the polarization parameter. We recall from
(iv)
in
Proposition

2.37
, the standard predictor-corrector IPM traverses
\mathrm{CP}[\mu^{\prime},\mu]
in at most

\left\lceil\tfrac{3\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{\mu}{\mu^{\prime}})\right\rceil
iterations. In particular, short segments can be
traversed in
O(\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma}))
iterations. As

2SLLS-IPM
always takes predictor steps that are at least as
long as the affine scaling steps, the same guarantee holds for our IPM.

Proof of
Lemma

7.5
.

The analysis is divided into two cases.

Case 1:
\mu_{1}<\overline{\mu}(z)\leq\mu_{1}\tfrac{72n^{1.5}}{\beta\gamma}
or
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tau/\varrho,\varrho/\tau]\right)\neq 0

If the first condition holds, the segment

\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\overline{\mu}(z)]
is short, and hence the IPM passes the end of the
polarized segment in at most
O(\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma}))

iterations, as needed. Let us now assume that

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tau/\varrho,\varrho/\tau]\right)\neq 0
(note that
(B,N)
is non-trivial in this case), or equivalently,
that
\zeta(z)\neq 0
and

\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})\in(\tau/\varrho,\varrho/\tau]
.

Using the stability of singular values, we will show that the number of large
singular values quickly drops by one as long as we remain in the segment. Let
z^{\prime}
be any basic iterate satisfying
\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\cdot\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4n^{2}}\cdot\overline{\mu}(z)
. If
\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\geq\mu_{1}
and
i\geq\zeta(z)
, then by
7.7
,

\sigma_{i}(\ell_{z^{\prime}})\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\cdot\sigma_{i}(\ell_{z})\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\cdot\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})\leq\frac{\tau^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\cdot\frac{\varrho}{\tau}=\frac{\tau}{\varrho}\,.

(70)

In particular, if
\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\geq\mu_{1}
, then
\zeta(z^{\prime})<\zeta(z)
.
Since the IPM traverses
\mathrm{CP}\left[\frac{\tau^{2}}{\varrho^{2}}\cdot\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4n^{2}}\cdot\overline{\mu}(z),\overline{\mu}(z)\right]
in at most

O(\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma}))

iterations, recalling that
\tau=\frac{\beta}{256n}
, this
proves the lemma in this case.

Case 2:
\overline{\mu}(z)>\mu_{1}\tfrac{72n^{1.5}}{\beta\gamma}
and

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tau/\varrho,\varrho/\tau]\right)=0

We first note that the condition on the singular values can be restated
equivalently as
\zeta(z)=0
(that is,

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\infty)\right)=0
) or
\zeta(z)>0
and
\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})>\varrho/\tau
. In this case,
there is a large gap in the singular values around the threshold, namely, for

i>\zeta(z)
, we have
\sigma_{i}(\ell_{z})\leq\tau/\varrho
, and for

1\leq i\leq\zeta(z)
, we have
\sigma_{i}(\ell_{z})>\varrho/\tau
.

In this setting, an identical analysis as in (
70
) applies as
long as we can
quickly
compute a basic iterate
z^{\prime}
such that

\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\hat{\mu}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mu_{1}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)=0\,,\\
\max\left\{\mu_{1},\frac{1}{\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}\cdot\frac{\tau}{\varrho}\cdot\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4n^{2}}\cdot\overline{\mu}(z)\right\}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)\geq 1\,.\end{cases}

(71)

That is, for any basic iterate
z^{\prime}
as above, either
\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\mu_{1}
or

\mu_{1}<\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\overline{\mu}(z)
and
\zeta(z^{\prime})<\zeta(z)
. Note that

\overline{\mu}(z)/\hat{\mu}
may be
arbitrarily large
in this setting, and thus, we
may need to take a
huge step
down the
central path. It is precisely for this purpose that we require subspace LLS
steps.

Let
z^{+}
denote the next basic iterate after
z
. Then, by
Lemma
7.8
below, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\mu}(z^{+})}{\hat{\mu}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{11}{\beta}\begin{cases}\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\hat{\mu}}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)=0\,,\\
\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\hat{\mu}}+\frac{12n}{\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}\frac{\overline{\mu}(z)}{\hat{\mu}}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)\geq 1\,.\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq\,\frac{11}{\beta}\begin{cases}\frac{4n^{1.5}}{\gamma}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)=0\,,\\
\frac{4n^{1.5}}{\gamma}+\frac{48n^{3}\varrho}{\tau\gamma^{2}}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)\geq 1\,.\end{cases}

(72)

This guarantees that after the next basic iterate
z^{+}
after
z
,
at most
O(\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma}))
additional iterations are needed to obtain
z^{\prime}
as in
(
71
).

Combining the two case analyses, the constant
C_{\rm it}\geq 1
in the lemma
statement can be chosen so that
C_{\rm it}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})
iterations is an upper bound on
1
plus the
number of affine scaling iterations needed to divide the normalized gap by a
factor

\frac{11}{\beta}\max\left\{\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\cdot\frac{4n^{2}}{\gamma^{2}},\frac{4n^{1.5}}{\gamma}+\frac{48n^{3}\varrho}{\tau\gamma^{2}}\right\}\leq\left(\frac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma}\right)^{12}\,,

(73)

where we have used
\tau\coloneqq\frac{\beta}{256n}
and
\beta\in(0,1/6]
. Thus, by
(iv)
in
Proposition

2.37
,
the choice
C_{\rm it}=3\cdot 12+2=38
is sufficient.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
be a
\gamma
-polarized segment with polarization partition
B\cup N=[n]
. Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}(\beta/2)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
be a basic iterate satisfying
\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z)\in(\tfrac{72n}{\beta\gamma}\cdot\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\tfrac{\varrho}{\tau}]\right)=0
. Then, the next basic iterate
z^{+}
computed in
2SLLS-IPM
after
z
satisfies

\overline{\mu}(z^{+})\leq\frac{11}{\beta}\cdot\begin{cases}\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)=0\,,\\
\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma}+\frac{12n}{\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}\overline{\mu}(z)\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)\geq 1\,.\end{cases}

(74)

It remains to prove
Lemma

7.8
. We will show the subspace LLS step from

z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta/2)
achieves (
74
). We will show this by comparing
these steps to the
ideal direction
, which goes straight to the end of
the polarized segment, as defined below:

Definition 7.9

(Ideal Direction)
.

For an
z\in\mathcal{N}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
, satisfying
\overline{\mu}(z)>\mu_{1}
, we define the
ideal direction
from
z
towards
\mu_{1}
to be

\Delta z^{\mathrm{ideal}}\coloneqq z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})-z\coloneqq(x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})-x,s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})-s)\eqqcolon(\Delta x^{\mathrm{ideal}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{ideal}}).

That is,
\Delta z^{\mathrm{ideal}}
is the direction from the current iterate to the
central path point
z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})
.

We now recall the notation used for cheap lift subspaces in the algorithm.
We define

\displaystyle(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P},\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})\leftarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Cheap-Lift-Subspace}\left(\mathbf{A},\frac{1}{x},B,N,\varrho\right),

\displaystyle(V,U)\leftarrow

\displaystyle(\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P}),\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})),

(75)

to be the output of the call to algorithm

1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
with respect to the
true
partition

(B,N)
. By the guarantees of

1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
, recall that if
(B,N)=(\emptyset,[n])
, then
(V,U)=(W,\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\})
, and if
(B,N)=([n],\emptyset)
, then
(V,U)=(\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\},W^{\perp})
.

The crux of the argument will be to show that a suitable projection of

\Delta x^{\mathrm{ideal}}
onto
V
and
\Delta s^{\mathrm{ideal}}
onto
U
induces a step
satisfying (
74
). We recall the notation
\hat{\xi}\coloneqq\sqrt{xs/\mu}
,
(\hat{x},\hat{s})\coloneqq(x\hat{\xi}^{-1},s\hat{\xi}^{-1})
,
\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z)
,
\widehat{W}\coloneqq\hat{x}^{-1}W
, and
\widehat{W}^{\perp}\coloneqq\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}
from
Definition

2.40
. Similarly, we let
\widehat{V}\coloneqq\hat{x}^{-1}V
,
\widehat{U}\coloneqq\hat{s}^{-1}U
and

(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}})\coloneqq(\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{ideal}},\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{ideal}})
.

We now define the projected ideal directions
