\Delta x^{\mathrm{proj}}\coloneqq\argmin_{v\in V}\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}_{N}(v_{N}-\Delta x_{N}^{\mathrm{ideal}})\right\rVert,\quad\quad\Delta s^{\mathrm{proj}}\coloneqq\argmin_{u\in U}\left\lVert\hat{s}^{-1}_{B}(u_{B}-\Delta s_{B}^{\mathrm{ideal}})\right\rVert,

where we recall that the minimizers are unique since
\dim(\pi_{N}(V))=\dim(V)
and
\dim(\pi_{B}(U))=\dim(U)
. Define the normalized projections by
(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}})\coloneqq(\hat{x}^{-1}\Delta x^{\mathrm{proj}},\hat{s}^{-1}\Delta s^{\mathrm{proj}})
. By construction of the projected ideal directions, note that

(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B})=(\Pi_{\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})}(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N}),\Pi_{\pi_{B}(\widehat{U})}(\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B})).

(76)

Let us now define

\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}\coloneqq\pi_{N}(\widehat{W})\cap\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})^{\perp}\quad\quad\widehat{U}^{\perp}_{B}\coloneqq\pi_{B}(\widehat{W}^{\perp})\cap\pi_{B}(\widehat{U})^{\perp},

(77)

to be the corresponding orthogonal complements inside
\pi_{N}(\widehat{W})
and

\pi_{B}(\widehat{W}^{\perp})
, where we note that

\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}\in\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}\quad\quad\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B}-\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}\in\widehat{U}^{\perp}_{B}.

Our goal will be to show that
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{proj}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{proj}})
induces a
trust-region step achieving the guarantees of (
74
). This will
then imply that the subspace LLS step
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})

also achieves the guarantee in (
74
).

The analysis relies on two auxiliarly lemmas. The first one exposes basic
properties of the ideal direction, while the second one gives basic
properties of the cheap lift subspaces. The proofs are deferred to
subsections
7.3.2
and
7.3.3
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
be a
\gamma
-polarized
segment with partition
B\cup N=[n]
. Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}(\beta)
,

\beta\in(0,1/6]
, with
\mu\coloneqq\overline{\mu}(z)\in(\tfrac{72n^{1.5}}{\beta\gamma}\cdot\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
. Then, the ideal direction
\Delta z^{\mathrm{ideal}}
towards
\mu_{1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 4n,

(78)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\mu}\leq\frac{\beta}{36}.

(79)

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
be a

\gamma
-polarized segment with partition
B\cup N=[n]
. Let
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6]
. Let
V\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm P})
,
U\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\rm D})
be the subspaces returned by

1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
on
(\mathbf{A},x^{-1},B,N,\varrho)
as
in (
75
), and
\ell_{z}\coloneqq\ell^{\hat{x}^{-1}W}_{N}
,
\ell^{\perp}_{z}\coloneqq\ell^{\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}}_{B}
. Then, if

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tau/\varrho,\varrho/\tau]\right)=0
, we have

1.

\dim(V)=\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-\zeta(z)
,
\dim(U)=\dim(\pi_{B}(W^{\perp}))-\zeta(z)
.

2.

\left\lVert\hat{v}_{B}\right\rVert\leq 2\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z})\left\lVert\hat{v}_{N}\right\rVert\leq 2\tau\left\lVert\hat{v}_{N}\right\rVert
,
\forall\hat{v}\in\widehat{V}\coloneqq\hat{x}^{-1}V
, and

\left\lVert\hat{u}_{N}\right\rVert\leq 2\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell^{\perp}_{z})\left\lVert\hat{u}_{B}\right\rVert\leq 2\tau\left\lVert\hat{u}_{B}\right\rVert
,
\forall\hat{u}\in\widehat{U}\coloneqq\hat{s}^{-1}U
.

We are ready to prove
Lemma

7.8
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.8
.

We show that the subspace LLS step
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
with respect to
(B,N)
is sufficient to achieve the guarantees of (
74
). As we show below, this will correspond to a long trust-region step and hence
Lemma

5.3
will guarantee that
(\widetilde{B}_{z},\widetilde{N}_{z})=(B,N)
. In particular, the subspace LLS direction computed by the algorithm will be the same as
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
.

We now analyze the norms and residuals of the subspace LLS direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
with respect to
(B,N)
. We first provide sufficient conditions to ensure that
z^{\mathrm{\ell}}\coloneqq(x+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},s+\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
, corresponding
to the subspace LLS step applied to
z
, satisfies (
74
). Letting
\mu\coloneq\overline{\mu}(z)
, we will show below that

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B},\Delta\hat{s}_{N}^{\mathrm{\ell}})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{18}\,,

(80)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{2n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\mu}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)=0\,,\\
\frac{2n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\mu}+\frac{6n}{\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)\geq 1\,.\end{cases}

(81)

By our assumption that
\mu>\tfrac{72n^{1.5}}{\beta\gamma}\cdot\mu_{1}
and, if
\zeta(z)\geq 1
, that
\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})>\frac{\varrho}{\tau}\geq\tfrac{256n}{\beta}
, the upper bound in (
81
) is always at most
\beta/18
.
From
Proposition

5.1
, we now get

\begin{aligned} \delta\coloneqq\left\lVert\frac{\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}}\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}}}{\mu}\right\rVert&\leq\frac{\beta}{9}\quad\mbox{ and }\\
\varepsilon\coloneqq\left\lVert\frac{(x+\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}})(s+\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})}{\mu}\right\rVert&\leq\begin{cases}\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\mu}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)=0\,,\\
\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\mu}+\frac{12n}{\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)\geq 1\,.\end{cases}\end{aligned}\,,

and
\varepsilon\leq\beta/9
. Consequently,

Proposition

2.44
is applicable. Choosing
1-\frac{9\varepsilon}{\beta}\leq\alpha^{\mathrm{\ell}}\leq 1-\frac{8\varepsilon}{\beta}
, we have

\overline{\mu}(z^{\mathrm{\ell}})\leq(1+\tfrac{1}{8})\tfrac{9\varepsilon\mu}{\beta}\leq\frac{11}{\beta}\varepsilon\mu\leq\frac{11}{\beta}\cdot\begin{cases}\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma}\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)=0\,,\\
\frac{4n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma}+\frac{12n}{\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}\mu\,,&\mbox{if }\zeta(z)\geq 1\,.\end{cases}\,,

which is precisely the guarantee required for (
74
).
Since (
80
) and (
81
) are at most
\beta/18\leq 1/30
, then assuming these bounds hold,
Lemma

5.3

implies that the associated partition
(\widetilde{B}_{z},\widetilde{N}_{z})=(B,N)
. In particular,
if the bounds hold,
2SLLS-IPM
correctly computes the
subspace LLS direction
(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
. Since
\overline{\mu}(z^{+})\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{\mathrm{\ell}})
, as we take the better of the AS and subspace LLS step, this will yield the claimed bound (
74
).

We now prove (
80
). By
7.11

part (
2
), we have that the subspace LLS direction

(\Delta x^{\mathrm{\ell}},\Delta s^{\mathrm{\ell}})
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z})\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B})\right\rVert\leq 2\tau\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B})\right\rVert=2\tau\sqrt{n}\leq\frac{\beta}{18},

as needed, where the second inequality uses
(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B})=(\Pi_{\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})}(\hat{\xi}_{N}),\Pi_{\pi_{B}(\widehat{U})}(\hat{\xi}_{B}))
as per
Remark

6.2
.

We now prove (
81
). By definition of subspace LLS (
Definition

6.1
) and the
triangle inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{\ell}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B})\right\rVert+

\displaystyle\quad\,\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}-\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\mu}+\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}-\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B})\right\rVert,

(82)

where the last inequality follows by (
79
) in

7.10
.

We now bound the second term of (
82
). Note that if

\zeta(z)=0
, then by
7.11
part (
1
), we
have that
\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})=\pi_{N}(\widehat{W})
and
\pi_{B}(\widehat{U})=\pi_{B}(\widehat{W}^{\perp})

and hence
\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}=\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
and
\widehat{U}^{\perp}_{B}=\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
. In particular,

\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N}\right\rVert=0\quad\quad\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}-\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B}\right\rVert=0.

Thus (
81
) follows directly from (
82
).
Now assume that
\zeta(z)\geq 1
. Note that this implies that
(B,N)
is a non-trivial partition. By
7.11
, we have that

\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})
is a
(2\varrho)
-approximate singular subspace for
\ell_{z}
with
dimension
\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))-\zeta(z)<\dim(\pi_{N}(\widehat{W}))
. By our assumption that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tau/\varrho,\varrho/\tau])\right)=0
, we have that

\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})>\frac{\varrho}{\tau}=\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\cdot\frac{\tau}{\varrho}\geq\frac{\varrho^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z})\geq 6\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z}).

By
Lemma

2.27
, recalling that
\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}\coloneqq\pi_{N}(\widehat{W})\cap\pi_{N}(\widehat{V})^{\perp}
, we therefore have that

\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}}})^{2}\geq\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})^{2}-(2\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z}))^{2}\geq\frac{8}{9}\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})^{2}.

(83)

By a symmetric argument, we have that
\widehat{U}^{\perp}_{B}\coloneqq\pi_{B}(\widehat{W}^{\perp})\cap\pi_{B}(\widehat{U}_{B})^{\perp}
is a
\varrho
-approximate singular subspace for
\ell^{\perp}

of dimension
\dim(\pi_{B}(\widehat{W}^{\perp}))-\zeta(z)
and satisfies

\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}^{\perp}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{U}^{\perp}_{B}}})^{2}\geq\frac{8}{9}\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z}^{\perp})^{2}=\frac{8}{9}\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})^{2}.

(84)

We now bound the second term in (
82
) as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\lVert\ell_{z}(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N})\right\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\rm min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}}})^{2}}\leq\frac{\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\rm min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}+2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\rm min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(2\tau)^{2}\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}+2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\rm min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N}\right\rVert^{2}+2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\rm min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}}})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}\right\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{V}^{\perp}_{N}}})^{2}}\leq\frac{9\cdot 2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}\right\rVert^{2}}{8\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})^{2}}=\frac{9\left\lVert\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}\right\rVert^{2}}{4\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})^{2}}.

(85)

By a symmetric argument,

\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}-\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{2\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}\right\rVert^{2}}{\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell^{\perp}_{z}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widehat{U}^{\perp}_{B}}})^{2}}\leq\frac{9\left\lVert\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}\right\rVert^{2}}{4\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})^{2}}.

(86)

Combining (
85
) and (
86
) together with (
78
) in
7.10
, we get that

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{N}-\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{proj}}_{B}-\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}})\right\rVert}{2\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}\leq\frac{3\cdot 4n}{2\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}

\displaystyle=\frac{6n}{\sigma_{\zeta(z)}(\ell_{z})}.

(87)

The desired bound (
81
) now follows by
combining (
82
) and (
87
).
∎

7.3.1
Stability of Singular Values on Polarized Segments

We now present the proof of
7.7
on the evolution of singular values of the map
\ell_{z}
on polarized segments of the central path. This will rely on the next lemma that bounds the change in the singular values under a rescaling of the space.

Lemma 7.12

(Stability of singular values for multiplicative perturbation)
.

Let
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a subspace, and let
B\cup N=[n]
be a non-trivial
partition. Let
y\in\mathbb{R}_{++}^{n}
, and let
\ell_{N}^{y^{-1}W}\colon\pi_{N}(y^{-1}W)\to\pi_{B}(yW^{\perp})
and
\ell_{N}^{W}\colon\pi_{N}(W)\to\pi_{B}(W^{\perp})
be defined according to
Definition

6.4
. Let
\sigma\coloneqq\sigma(\ell_{N}^{y^{-1}W})
and
\hat{\sigma}\coloneqq\sigma(\ell_{N}^{W})
denote their respective singular values. Then, we have that

\frac{1}{\left\lVert y_{B}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert y_{N}\right\rVert_{\infty}}\sigma\leq\hat{\sigma}\leq\left\lVert y_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert y_{N}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\sigma\,.

(88)

Proof.

We only prove the second inequality. The first inequality then follows by
swapping
y
with
y^{-1}
and
\sigma
with
\hat{\sigma}
. We denote
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{N}^{y^{-1}W}
and
\hat{\ell}\coloneqq\ell_{N}^{W}
. Since
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}
, we clearly have that
p\coloneqq\dim(\pi_{N}(W))=\dim(\pi_{N}(y^{-1}W))
. Therefore
\sigma
and
\hat{\sigma}
are both vectors
in
\mathbb{R}^{p}_{+}
. By (
15
), recall that for
i\in[p]
, we have
that

\sigma_{k}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq\pi_{N}(y^{-1}W)\\
\dim(S)\geq p-k+1\end{subarray}}\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}}),\quad\quad\hat{\sigma}_{k}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{S}\subseteq\pi_{N}(W)\\
\dim(\hat{S})\geq p-k+1\end{subarray}}\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\hat{\ell}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\hat{S}}}).

To prove the inequality, we will show that for any
S\subseteq\pi_{N}(y^{-1}W)
, there exists
\hat{S}\subseteq\pi_{N}(W)
with
\dim(S)=\dim(\hat{S})
satisfying
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\hat{\ell}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\hat{S}}})\leq\left\lVert y_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert y_{N}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})
. For this
purpose, define
\hat{S}=y_{N}S
, where clearly
\dim(\hat{S})=\dim(S)
and
\hat{S}\subseteq y_{N}\pi_{N}(y^{-1}W)=\pi_{N}(W)
. From here, we have that

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\hat{\ell}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\hat{S}}})

\displaystyle=\max_{\hat{x}\in\hat{S}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\hat{\ell}(\hat{x})\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\hat{x}\right\rVert}=\max_{x\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\hat{\ell}(y_{N}x)\right\rVert}{\left\lVert y_{N}x\right\rVert}\leq\max_{x\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert y_{B}\ell(x)\right\rVert}{\left\lVert y_{N}x\right\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\left\lVert y_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert y_{N}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\frac{\left\lVert\ell(x)\right\rVert}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert}=\left\lVert y_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert y_{N}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}}),

where
\left\lVert\hat{\ell}(y_{N}x)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert y_{B}\ell(x)\right\rVert
follows since
(y_{B}\ell(x),y_{N}x)\in W
and by the optimality of the lift provided by
\hat{\ell}(y_{N}x)
. The statement thus follows.
∎

See
7.7

Proof.

Let
z\coloneqq(x,s)
and
z^{\prime}\coloneqq(x^{\prime},s^{\prime})
. Since
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
, note that
\mu=\overline{\mu}(z)>0
. If
(B,N)
is a trivial partition, then

\ell_{z},\ell_{z^{\prime}}
are both zero operators on input spaces of the same dimension and hence the statement trivially holds. If
\mu^{\prime}=0
, then
\ell_{z^{\prime}}
is a zero operator on
\pi_{N}(W)
, and hence again the
statement holds trivially. Thus, we may assume that
\mu^{\prime}>0
and that
(B,N)
is a non-trivial partition of
[n]
. Consequently,

z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
.

Let
\hat{x}=\sqrt{\frac{x\mu}{s}}
and
\hat{x}^{\prime}=\sqrt{\frac{x^{\prime}\mu}{s^{\prime}}}
denote the normalized iterates. To prove the bounds, we apply
Lemma

7.12

to the subspace
\frac{1}{\hat{x}^{\prime}}W
and
y\coloneqq\frac{\hat{x}}{\hat{x}^{\prime}}
, noting that
y\hat{x}^{\prime}=\hat{x}
. Since
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
, these subspaces are well-defined as
\hat{x},\hat{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{++}
. By
Proposition

2.42
, we have that

\frac{\sqrt{1-\beta}}{\sqrt{1+\beta}}\frac{x}{x^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\hat{x}}{\hat{x}^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\frac{x}{x^{\prime}}.

(89)

Combining the above with
Proposition

2.32
and
Corollary

3.4
, we get that

\displaystyle\left\lVert y_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{\hat{x}_{B}}{\hat{x}^{\prime}_{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\left\lVert\frac{x_{B}}{x^{\prime}_{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}(1+2\beta)}{(1-\beta)^{3/2}}\left\lVert\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{B}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{2n}{\gamma},

(90)

\displaystyle\left\lVert y_{N}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{\hat{x}^{\prime}_{N}}{\hat{x}_{N}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\left\lVert\frac{x^{\prime}_{N}}{x_{N}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}(1+2\beta)}{(1-\beta)^{3/2}}\left\lVert\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{N}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{N}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{2n}{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{\mu},

and

\displaystyle\left\lVert y_{B}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{\hat{x}^{\prime}_{B}}{\hat{x}_{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\left\lVert\frac{x^{\prime}_{B}}{x_{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}(1+2\beta)}{(1-\beta)^{3/2}}\left\lVert\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{B}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{2n}{\gamma},

(91)

\displaystyle\left\lVert y_{N}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\frac{\hat{x}_{N}}{\hat{x}^{\prime}_{N}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}\left\lVert\frac{x_{N}}{x^{\prime}_{N}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{\sqrt{1+\beta}(1+2\beta)}{(1-\beta)^{3/2}}\left\lVert\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{N}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{N}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{2n}{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mu}{\mu^{\prime}},

using
\frac{(1+2\beta)(1+\beta)^{1/2}}{(1-\beta)^{3/2}}\leq 2
for
\beta\in(0,1/6]
,
\left\lVert\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{B}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{B}}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i\in B}\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{i}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})_{i}}\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})_{i}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{i}}\leq n\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma}
and
\left\lVert\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{N}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{N}}\right\rVert_{\infty}

=\max_{i\in N}\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{\prime})_{i}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})_{i}}\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{0})_{i}}{x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)_{i}}\leq\frac{\mu^{\prime}}{\gamma\mu_{0}}\cdot\frac{n\mu_{0}}{\mu}
, together with the the analogous bounds for the remaining inequalities.

Plugging these estimates into
Lemma

7.12
yields the result.
∎

7.3.2
Properties of the Ideal Direction

See
7.10

Proof.

We first prove (
78
)

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}},\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}})\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\hat{x}^{-1}x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1})-\hat{\xi},\hat{s}^{-1}s^{\mathrm{cp}}_{N}(\mu_{1})-\hat{\xi})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\hat{x}^{-1}x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1}),\hat{s}^{-1}s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1}))\right\rVert+\left\lVert(\hat{\xi},\hat{\xi})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\hat{x}^{-1}x^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1}),\hat{s}^{-1}s^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu_{1}))\right\rVert_{1}+\sqrt{2n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n}{\sqrt{1-\beta}}(1+\mu_{1}/\mu)+\sqrt{2n}\quad\left(\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:local-norm-monotone} }\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4n,

where the last inequality follows from
\beta\in(0,1/6]
and
\mu\geq 6\mu_{1}
.

We now continue with (
79
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\hat{\xi}_{N}+\Delta\hat{x}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{N},\hat{\xi}_{B}+\Delta\hat{s}^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\hat{x}^{-1}_{N}x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{N}(\mu_{1}),\hat{s}^{-1}_{B}s^{\mathrm{cp}}_{B}(\mu_{1}))\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left(\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{N}(\mu_{1})}{x_{N}},\frac{s^{\mathrm{cp}}_{B}(\mu_{1})}{s_{B}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert(\hat{x}^{-1}_{N}x_{N},\hat{s}^{-1}_{B}s_{B})\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\left(\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{N}(\mu_{1})}{x_{N}},\frac{s^{\mathrm{cp}}_{B}(\mu_{1})}{s_{B}}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{1-\beta}\left\lVert\left(\frac{x^{\mathrm{cp}}_{N}(\mu_{1})}{x_{N}^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu)},\frac{s^{\mathrm{cp}}_{B}(\mu_{1})}{s^{\mathrm{cp}}_{B}(\mu)}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}\sqrt{n}\quad\left(\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:near-central} }\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n\mu_{1}}{(1-\beta)\mu\gamma}\cdot\sqrt{n}\leq\frac{2n^{1.5}\mu_{1}}{\gamma\mu},\quad\left(\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:polarization} }\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\beta}{36}.\quad\left(\text{ since }\mu\geq\mu_{1}\tfrac{72n^{1.5}}{\beta\gamma}~\right)

∎

7.3.3
Dimension and Lifting Cost of Cheap Lift Subspaces

See
7.11

Proof.

Assume first that
(B,N)
is a trivial partition. Recall that

\zeta(\ell_{z})=\zeta(\ell^{\perp}_{z})=0
. By the guarantees of

1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
, we have
(V,U)=(W,\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\})
if
B=\emptyset

and
(V,U)=(\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\},W^{\perp})
if
N=\emptyset
, and
thus (
1
) and (
2
) follow directly.

Now assume that
(B,N)
is a non-trivial partition. We first prove the dimension guarantees for
V
and
U
. By
Lemma

7.12
, using that
x^{-1}W=\hat{\xi}^{-1}\hat{x}^{-1}W
, we have that

\left(\left\lVert\hat{\xi}^{-1}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)^{-1}\sigma(\ell_{N}^{x^{-1}W})\leq\sigma(\ell_{N}^{\hat{x}^{-1}W})=\sigma(\ell_{z})\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}^{-1}_{N}\right\rVert_{\infty}\sigma(\ell_{N}^{x^{-1}W}).

By
Proposition

2.42
,
\sqrt{1-\beta}\mathbf{1}_{n}\leq\hat{\xi}\leq\sqrt{1+\beta}\mathbf{1}_{n}
, and since
\beta\in(0,1/6]
we have that

\max\{\left\lVert\hat{\xi}^{-1}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}\right\rVert_{\infty},\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}^{-1}_{N}\right\rVert_{\infty}\}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1+\beta}{1-\beta}}\leq\sqrt{2}.

Applying the same argument to
\ell_{B}^{xW^{\perp}}
and
\ell_{B}^{\hat{s}^{-1}W^{\perp}}=\ell_{z}^{\perp}
, using that
xW^{\perp}=\tfrac{x}{\mu}W^{\perp}=\hat{\xi}\hat{s}^{-1}W
, we get that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma(\ell_{N}^{x^{-1}W})\leq\sigma(\ell_{z})\leq\sqrt{2}\sigma(\ell_{N}^{x^{-1}W}),\quad\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma(\ell_{B}^{xW^{\perp}})\leq\sigma(\ell_{z}^{\perp})\leq\sqrt{2}\sigma(\ell_{B}^{xW^{\perp}}),

(92)

where we recall that
\sigma^{+}(\ell_{z})=\sigma^{+}(\ell_{z}^{\perp})
by

Lemma

6.8
. By the guarantees of
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
and (
92
), we have that

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}\varrho}\right)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{N}^{x^{-1}W},1/\varrho\right)\leq\dim(V)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{N}^{x^{-1}W},1\right)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},\sqrt{2}\right).

Since
{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\tfrac{\varrho}{\tau}]\right)=0
and
\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\leq\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}\varrho}\leq\sqrt{2}\leq\tfrac{\varrho}{\tau}
, we have
\dim(V)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},\tfrac{\varrho}{\tau}\right)
. In particular,

\displaystyle\dim(V)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},[0,\infty)\right)-{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\infty)\right)\coloneqq\dim(\pi_{N}(\hat{x}^{-1}W))-\zeta(z)

\displaystyle=\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-\zeta(z).

By a completely symmetric argument on the dual, we get that

\dim(U)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z}^{\perp},\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right)=\dim(\pi_{B}(W^{\perp}))-\zeta(z).

We now prove the lifting cost guarantees. For
v\in V
and the guarantees of
1Cheap-Lift-Subspaces
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}_{B}v_{B}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert x^{-1}_{B}v_{B}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\varrho\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-\dim(V)+1}(\ell^{x^{-1}W}_{N})\left\lVert x^{-1}_{N}v_{N}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{B}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\hat{\xi}_{N}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\sqrt{2}\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z})\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}_{N}v_{N}\right\rVert\leq 2\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z})\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}_{N}v_{N}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2\tau\left\lVert\hat{x}^{-1}_{N}v_{N}\right\rVert.

By a symmetric algorithm for the dual, for
u\in U
, we have that

\left\lVert\hat{s}^{-1}_{N}u_{N}\right\rVert\leq 2\varrho\sigma_{\zeta(z)+1}(\ell_{z}^{\perp})\left\lVert\hat{s}^{-1}_{B}u_{B}\right\rVert\leq 2\tau\left\lVert\hat{s}^{-1}_{B}u_{B}\right\rVert,

as needed.
∎

8
Amortized Iteration Bound for
2SLLS-IPM

In this section we prove
Theorem

1.4
. For this purpose we
will rely on
Theorem

1.8
, which decomposes the central path into
polarized segments where the sum of partition changes is bounded by the
straight line complexity. The proof of
Theorem

1.4
will
then follow from the following theorem, which shows that the number of
iterations of our IPM can be upper bounded in terms of a sum of partition
changes.

Theorem 8.1
.

For any
T\in\mathbb{N}
let
0\leq\mu^{(T)}<\mu^{(T-1)}<\ldots<\mu^{(1)}<\mu^{(0)}
such that for all
1\leq i\leq T
we have that
\mathrm{CP}[\mu^{(i)},\mu^{(i-1)}]
is
\gamma
-polarized with polarizing partition
B^{(i)}\cup N^{(i)}
, where
N^{(0)}\coloneqq\emptyset
.
Then,
2SLLS-IPM
equipped with a
\varrho
-
SVD
solver, starting from any point
z^{(0)}\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
such that
\overline{\mu}(z^{(0)})\leq\mu^{(0)}
, finds a point
z\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
such that
\overline{\mu}(z)\leq\mu^{(T)}
in

O\left(\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\sum_{i=1}^{T}\left(\left|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}\right|+1\right)\right)

(93)

many iterations.

As explained in the introduction, the above theorem strengthens

Theorem

1.1
by reducing the
n^{1.5}
factor in the
iteration bound for traversing the
i
th polarized segment down to

\sqrt{n}\left|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-i)}\right|
. This will yield an overall
amortized improvement as long as the polarizing partitions do not change too
rapidly.

We are now ready to give the proof of
Theorem

1.4
.

Proof of
Theorem

1.4
.

By
Lemma

7.3
, the output
(x^{*},s^{*},v^{*},w^{*})
of
2SLLS-IPM
is correct when
\mu_{1}=0
.
The (trivial) modification to
2SLLS-IPM
for
\mu_{1}>0
,
where it only outputs
z^{1}=(x^{1},s^{1})
with
\overline{\mu}(z^{1})\leq\mu_{1}
, is
explained in
Remark

7.1
. The desired upper bound on the
number of iterations to traverse the segment
\mathrm{CP}[\mu_{1},\mu_{0}]
follows
directly by combining
Theorem

1.8
and
Theorem

8.1
with

\varrho=2
, where we use
Theorem

6.16
to instantiate the

2
-
SVD
solver.
∎

It remains to prove
Theorem

8.1
. In
Section

8.1
, we show
that the singular values cannot change too quickly under a change in
polarization partition, and finally we prove
Theorem

8.1
in

Section

8.2
.

8.1
Stability of Singular Values under a Partition
Change

The crucial statement towards proving
Theorem

8.1
is the following lemma on lifting operators for different partitions. This will ensure that we do not lose too much progress, in terms gaining large singular values, when moving from one partition to another.

Lemma 8.2
.

Given a subspace
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and two partitions
B\cup N=[n]
and
\hat{B}\cup\hat{N}=[n]
, the lifting operators
\ell\coloneqq\ell_{N}^{W}
and
\hat{\ell}\coloneqq\ell_{\hat{N}}^{W}
satisfy

\sigma_{i}(\ell)\geq\sigma_{i+|N\Delta\hat{N}|}(\hat{\ell}),~\forall i\geq 1\,.

(94)

Proof.

If either
\hat{B}
or
\hat{N}
is empty,
\sigma_{i}(\hat{\ell})=0
,

\forall i\geq 1
, by convention, and hence the statement is trivial. If
N\cap\hat{N}=\emptyset
, then
\sigma_{i+|N\Delta\hat{N}|}(\hat{\ell})\leq\sigma_{i+|\hat{N}|}(\hat{\ell})=0
since
\mathrm{rank}(\hat{\ell})\leq|\hat{N}|
, and hence again the statement is trivial. Thus, we may assume that

(\hat{B},\hat{N})
is non-trivial and that
N\cap\hat{N}\neq\emptyset
.

Let
\check{N}\coloneqq N\cap\hat{N}\neq\emptyset
,
\check{B}\coloneqq[n]\setminus\check{N}=B\cup\hat{B}\neq\emptyset
, and define
\check{\ell}\coloneqq\ell_{\check{N}}^{W}
. By our
assumptions, note that
(\check{B},\check{N})
is a non-trivial partition of

[n]
.

First, we are going to prove that for all
i\geq 1
we have
\sigma_{i}(\ell)\geq\sigma_{i+|N\setminus\check{N}|}(\check{\ell})
. Note that
\dim(\pi_{N}(W))\geq\dim(\pi_{\check{N}}(W))
.
For
i>\dim(\pi_{N}(W))
, we have
\sigma_{i}(\ell)=\sigma_{i+|N\setminus\check{N}|}(\check{\ell})=0
.
Assume now
i\leq\dim(\pi_{N}(W))
. By (
15
), there exists a subspace
S^{(i)}\subseteq\pi_{N}(W)
,
\dim(S)=\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-i+1
with

\sigma_{i}(\ell)=\sigma_{\max}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S^{(i)}}}\right)\,.

(95)

Consider
T^{(i)}\coloneqq S^{(i)}\cap\mathbb{R}_{\check{N}}^{N}
.
Note that
\dim(\pi_{\check{N}}(T^{(i)}))=\dim(T^{(i)})
as
\pi_{N\setminus\check{N}}(T^{(i)})=\{\mathbf{0}_{N\setminus\check{N}}\}
by definition. Further,
\pi_{\check{N}}(T^{(i)})\subseteq\pi_{\check{N}}(W)
by construction.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\dim(\pi_{\check{N}}(T^{(i)}))

\displaystyle=\dim(T^{(i)})\geq\dim(S^{(i)})-|N\setminus\check{N}|

(96)

\displaystyle=\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-i+1-|N\setminus\check{N}|\,,

where the inequality follows as
T^{(i)}
arises from
S^{(i)}
by adding
N\setminus\check{N}
homogeneous linear equations to its defining system.
Finally, note that for any
v\in\pi_{\check{N}}(T^{(i)})
we have that

\left\lVert\check{\ell}(v)\right\rVert=\min_{w\in W,w_{\check{N}}=v}\left\lVert w_{[n]\setminus\check{N}}\right\rVert\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in W,w_{\check{N}}=v,\\
w_{N\setminus\check{N}}=\mathbf{0}_{N\setminus\check{N}}\end{subarray}}\left\lVert w_{[n]\setminus\check{N}}\right\rVert=\left\lVert\ell(\mathbf{0}_{N\setminus\check{N}},v)\right\rVert\,,

(97)

and so in particular

\sigma_{\max}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\check{\ell}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\pi_{\check{N}}(T^{(i)})}}\right)\leq\sigma_{\max}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{T^{(i)}}}\right)\,.

(98)

From here, since
\dim(\pi_{\check{N}}(W))\leq\dim(\pi_{N}(W))
we get that

\displaystyle\sigma_{i+|N\setminus\check{N}|}(\check{\ell})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{eq:dim-bound}}}\sigma_{\dim(\pi_{N}(W))-\dim(\pi_{\check{N}}(T^{(i)}))+1}(\check{\ell})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:min-max-sing}}}{{\leq}}\sigma_{\max}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\check{\ell}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\pi_{\check{N}}(T^{(i)})}}\right)

(99)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\eqref{eq:dom-lift-bar-lift}}}\sigma_{\max}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{T^{(i)}}}\right)\leq\sigma_{\max}\left({\left.\kern-1.2pt\ell\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S^{(i)}}}\right)=\sigma_{i}(\ell)\,.

It remains to show that we can use similar shift argumentation between the
operators
\check{\ell}
and
\hat{\ell}
, that is, that
\sigma_{i}(\check{\ell})\geq\sigma_{i+|\hat{N}\setminus\check{N}|}(\hat{\ell})
,
\forall i\geq 1
.
Using
Lemma

6.8
, we may equivalently consider the dual
operator
\ell_{\check{B}}^{W^{\perp}}=-\operatorname{ad}(\ell_{\check{N}}^{W})
, where
\check{B}=[n]\setminus\check{N}
, and show that

\sigma_{i}(\ell_{\check{B}}^{W^{\perp}})\geq\sigma_{i+|\check{B}\setminus\hat{B}|}(\ell_{\hat{B}}^{W^{\perp}})\,,\,\forall i\geq 1.

(100)

Since
\emptyset\neq\hat{B}\subseteq\check{B}
,
the inequality above follows directly from same arguments we used to obtain (
99
). Therefore, for all
i\geq 1
, by
Lemma

6.8
we get that

\sigma_{i}(\check{\ell})=\sigma_{i}(\ell^{W^{\perp}}_{\check{B}})\geq\sigma_{i+|\check{B}\setminus\hat{B}|}(\ell^{W^{\perp}}_{\hat{B}})=\sigma_{i+|\hat{N}\setminus\check{N}|}(\hat{\ell})\,

(101)

holds, where we used that
|\hat{N}\setminus\check{N}|=|\check{B}\setminus\hat{B}|
.
Combining (
99
) and (
101
) gives the result as now

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(\ell)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{i+|N\setminus\check{N}|}(\check{\ell})=\sigma_{i+|N\setminus\hat{N}|}(\check{\ell})\geq\sigma_{i+|N\setminus\hat{N}|+|\hat{N}\setminus\check{N}|}(\hat{\ell})

(102)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{i+|N\setminus\hat{N}|+|\hat{N}\setminus N|}(\hat{\ell})=\sigma_{i+|N\Delta\hat{N}|}(\hat{\ell}),~\forall i\geq 1.

∎

8.2
Proof of the Amortized Bound

We need few more preparations to prove
Theorem

8.1
. First,
consider the basic iterates
z^{(0)},\ldots,z^{(K)}
of the algorithm,
where basic is as defined in
Section

7.3
, with
z^{(K)}

being the first basic iterate with
\overline{\mu}(z^{(K)})\leq\mu^{(T)}
. Without
loss of generality, we may assume that
\mu^{(1)}<\overline{\mu}(z^{(0)})\leq\mu^{(0)}
.

For
i\in\{0,\dots,T\}
, define
t_{i}
to be the smallest index of an
iterate with
\mu(z^{(t_{i})})\leq\mu^{(i)}
. With this definition, note that

t_{0}=0
,
t_{T}=K
, and for
i\in[T]
, that
t_{i}-t_{i-1}=|\{j\in[K]:\mu^{(i)}<\overline{\mu}(z^{(j)})\leq\mu^{(i-1)}\}|
. Further, define
y^{(i)}\coloneqq z^{\mathrm{cp}}(\mu^{(i)})
,
i\in\{0,\dots,T\}
, to be the central path
point with parameter
\mu^{(i)}
.

For
z=(x,s)\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6)
, let us extend the
notation (
68
) to
\ell_{z,N^{(i)}}\coloneqq\ell^{\hat{x}^{-1}W}_{N^{(i)}}
and

\zeta^{(i)}(z)\coloneqq\left|\left\{j\geq 1:\sigma_{j}(\ell_{z,N^{(i)}})>\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\right\}\right|={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z,N^{(i)}},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\infty)\right)\,.

(103)

Proof Overview

We start by giving an outline of the proof of
Theorem

8.1
.
Assume for simplicity of presentation that
\overline{\mu}(z^{(t_{i})}))=\mu^{(i)}

(i.e.,
y^{(i)}=z^{(t_{i})}
),
i\in\{0,\dots,T\}
, which can be achieved
by adding artificial iterates in each segment. Consider the
behaviour of the algorithm on the polarized segment

\mathrm{CP}[\mu^{(i)},\mu^{(i-1)}]
with partition
(B^{(i)},N^{(i)})
, which starts
with
z^{(t_{i-1})}
and ends with
z^{(t_{i})}
. By

Lemma

7.5
, we have that after every
D=\Theta(\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\ln(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma}))

iterations, either we decrease the potential
\zeta^{(i)}(\cdot)
by one
or we pass the end of the segment. Specifically, for
k\geq 1
, either
t_{i}+kD>t_{i+1}
, or
\zeta^{(i)}(z^{i+kD})\leq\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i})})-k
.

By
7.7
, for
z^{(t^{\prime})},z^{(t)}
with
t_{i}\leq t^{\prime}<t\leq t_{i-1}
(i.e.,
\mu^{(i)}\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{(t^{\prime})})<\overline{\mu}(z^{(t)})\leq\mu^{(i-1)}
), recall that
\sigma(\ell_{z^{(t^{\prime})},N^{(i)}})\leq\Gamma\frac{\overline{\mu}(z^{(t^{\prime})})}{\overline{\mu}(z^{(t)})}\sigma(\ell_{z^{(t)},N^{(i)}})
where

\Gamma=\frac{4n^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}
. Let us assume for now that the inequality
holds for
\Gamma=1
instead of
\frac{4n^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}
. Then, under
this assumption, the singular values would be non-increasing over the
segment, and hence the potential
\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t)})
would be monotonic in
t
.
Therefore, we would have that

\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i})})\leq\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i-1})})-\lfloor(t_{i}-t_{i-1})/D\rfloor\Rightarrow t_{i}-t_{i-1}\leq D(\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i-1})})-\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i})})+1).

(104)

That is, the drop in potential from the start to the end of the segment pays
for the number of iterations. From here, the handover to the next segment is
controlled by
Lemma

8.2
, which
implies that
\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i-1})})\leq|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|+\zeta^{(i-1)}(z^{(t_{i-1})})
for
i\in[T]
. Putting these bounds
together, we would get

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=t_{T}-t_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{T}t_{i}-t_{i-1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}D(\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i-1})})-\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i})})+1)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}D(|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|+1)+D(\zeta^{(i-1)}(z^{(t_{i-1})})-\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t_{i})}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{T}D(|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|+1)+D(\zeta^{(0)}(z^{(0)})-\zeta^{(T)}(z^{(t_{T})}))

\displaystyle\leq D\sum_{i=1}^{T}(|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|+1),

(105)

where the last inequality follows since
\zeta^{(0)}(z^{(0)})=0
as

N^{(0)}=\emptyset
. This is precisely the desired bound in

Theorem

8.1
.

Unfortunately, this argument breaks due to the
(4n^{2}/\gamma^{2})
factor in
7.7
. The singular values may in fact increase on the short term. This would not be a problem on sufficiently ‘long’ segments. However, on ‘short’ segments, this could lead to an increase in singular values, where there may not be sufficiently many—
D
—iterations to compensate. Such increments could even aggregate over a sequence of short segments.

The proof below works with a more robust version of the potential in
(
103
). The robust potential is defined as a minimum
over parametrized potentials: for
i\geq 0
and
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,

\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(z)\coloneqq\min_{q\geq 0}\zeta^{(i)}(z,q),\quad\quad\zeta^{(i)}(z,q)\coloneqq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z,N^{(i)}},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\Gamma^{q},\infty)\right)+q,\,\,\mbox{for}\,q\geq 0\,,

(106)

where
\Gamma\coloneqq\tfrac{4n^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}
as above.

Each parametrized potential keep track of the singular values above a
threshold
\Gamma^{q}\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}
for some
q\geq 0
. The additional
factor represents possible increases accumulated on previous short segments.
However, the contributions
|N^{(j)}\Delta N^{(j-1)}|+1
of these short
segments will ‘pay’ for bringing down large singular values later, which
corresponds to the additive
q
factor in these potentials. Note that the
original potential in (
103
) corresponds to the
parametrized potential with
q=0
.

The following technical lemma shows that the drop in robust potential

\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(\cdot)
over a polarized segment indeed pays for the number
of iterations, in analogy to (
104
) in the ideal setting
(where we assumed monotonicity of the potential). With this lemma in hand,
the iteration bound in
Theorem

8.1
follows along identical
lines to the analysis in (
105
).

Lemma 8.3
.

Define
D_{\rm it}\coloneqq 3\lfloor C_{\rm it}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\rfloor
,
where
C_{\rm it}
is as in
Lemma

7.5
. Let
t_{i},N^{(i)},y^{(i)}
,
i\in\{0,\dots,T\}
be as defined above, let
k_{i}\coloneqq\max\{0,\lfloor(t_{i}-t_{i-1}-1)/D_{\rm it}\rfloor\}
,
i\in[T]
. Then, for
i\in[T]
, the
following holds

(i)

\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)})\leq\bar{\zeta}^{(i-1)}(y^{(i-1)})+|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|
.

(ii)

\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i)})+k_{i}\leq\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)})+1
.

Proof.

We prove
(i)
and
(ii)
separately below.

Proof of
(i)

By

Lemma

8.2
,
\sigma_{i+|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|}(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i)}})\leq\sigma_{i}(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i-1)}})
,
\forall i\geq 1
. This implies

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i-1)}},(\frac{\tau}{\varrho}\Gamma^{q},\infty)\right)+|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|\geq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i)}},(\frac{\tau}{\varrho}\Gamma^{q},\infty)\right),

and hence
\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)},q)\leq\zeta^{(i-1)}(y^{(i-1)},q)+|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|
, for
q\geq 0
. Therefore,

\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)})\leq

\bar{\zeta}^{(i-1)}(y^{(i-1)})+|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|
.

Proof of
(ii)

For
q\geq 0
, we claim that

\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i)},\max\{0,q+1-k_{i}\})+k_{i}\leq\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)},q)+1.

(107)

Assuming the claim,
(ii)
follows noting that

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i)})+k_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{q\geq 0}\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i)},\max\{0,q+1-k_{i}\})+k_{i}\leq\min_{q\geq 0}\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)},q)+1

\displaystyle=\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)})+1\,.

To prove (
107
), we need the following intermediate inequality:

\sigma(\ell_{y^{(i)},N^{(i)}})\leq\Gamma^{1-k_{i}}\sigma(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i)}})\,.

(108)

We prove this inequality first. Recall that
\mathrm{CP}[\mu^{(i)},\mu^{(i-1)}]
is a
\gamma
-polarized segment with partition
(B^{(i)},N^{(i)})
. Therefore, for
z^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\beta)
,
z\in\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
\beta\in(0,1/6)
, with
\mu^{(i)}\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})\leq\overline{\mu}(z)\leq\mu^{(i-1)}
, by
7.7
we have
that

\sigma(\ell_{z^{\prime},N^{(i)}})\leq\Gamma\frac{\overline{\mu}(z^{\prime})}{\overline{\mu}(z)}\sigma(\ell_{z,N^{(i)}}).

(109)

Assume that
k_{i}=0
. Then, (
108
) follows
from (
109
) with
z^{\prime}=y^{(i)}
and
z=y^{(i-1)}
, noting
that
\overline{\mu}(y^{(i)})=\mu^{(i)}<\mu^{(i-1)}=\overline{\mu}(y^{(i-1)})
and
\Gamma=\Gamma^{1-k_{i}}
.

Now assume that
k_{i}\geq 1
. Let
z^{(i)}
,
i\in\{0,\dots,K\}
, be the
basic iterates as defined in the overview, and let
t=t_{i-1}+D_{\rm it}/3
and

t^{\prime}=t_{i}-1-D_{\rm it}/3
. Since
k_{i}\geq 1
, we have that

t^{\prime}-t\geq k_{i}D_{\rm it}-2D_{\rm it}/3\geq k_{i}D_{\rm it}/3.

(110)

By the choice of
C_{\rm it}
in (
73
),
D_{\rm it}/3=\lfloor C_{\rm it}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\rfloor

iterations are sufficient to divide the normalized gap by
\Gamma
. That is,

\overline{\mu}(z^{(i+D_{\rm it}/3)})\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{(i)})/\Gamma
, for
i\in\{0,\dots,K-D_{\rm it}/3\}
. In particular,

\overline{\mu}(z^{(t^{\prime})})\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{(t)})/\Gamma^{\lfloor\tfrac{3(t^{\prime}-t)}{D_{\rm it}}\rfloor}\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{(t)})/\Gamma^{k_{i}}.

Similarly,
\mu^{(i)}\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{(t_{i}-1)})\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{(t^{\prime})})/\Gamma
and

\overline{\mu}(z^{(t)})\leq\overline{\mu}(z^{(t_{i-1})})/\Gamma\leq\mu^{(i-1)}/\Gamma
. Therefore, by (
109
) we have that

\sigma(\ell_{y^{(i)},N^{(i)}})\leq\sigma(\ell_{z^{(t^{\prime})},N^{(i)}})\leq\sigma(\ell_{z^{(t)},N^{(i)}})/\Gamma^{k_{i}-1}\leq\sigma(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i)}})/\Gamma^{k_{i}-1}\,,

(111)

completing the proof of (
108
).

We now prove claim (
107
) by dividing the analysis into two
additional cases.

Case I.

\boldsymbol{k_{i}\leq q+1:}

\displaystyle\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i)},q+1-k_{i})+k_{i}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{y^{(i)},N^{(i)}},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\Gamma^{q+1-k},\infty)\right)+(q+1-k_{i})+k_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:sing-drop-1}}}{{\leq}}{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i)}},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\Gamma^{q},\infty)\right)+q+1

\displaystyle=\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)},q)+1,\text{ as needed.}

Case II.

\boldsymbol{k_{i}\geq q+2:}
Let
\hat{t}=t+(q+1)D_{\rm it}/3
. By our assumption that
k_{i}\geq q+2
, using (
110
) we have that
that
t^{\prime}-\hat{t}\geq(k_{i}-q-1)D_{\rm it}/3\geq D_{\rm it}/3
. Via an identical
calculation to (
111
), we have that

\sigma(\ell_{z^{(\hat{t})},N^{(i)}})\leq\sigma(\ell_{z^{(t)},N^{(i)}})/\Gamma^{q}\leq\sigma(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i)}})/\Gamma^{q}.

Using the above, we get that

\displaystyle\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(\hat{t})})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{z^{(\hat{t})},N^{(i)}},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho},\infty)\right)\leq{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\ell_{y^{(i-1)},N^{(i)}},(\tfrac{\tau}{\varrho}\Gamma^{q},\infty)\right)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)},q)-q.

(112)

By
Lemma

7.5
, since
t^{\prime}-\hat{t}\geq(k_{i}-q-1)D_{\rm it}/3=(k_{i}-q-1)\lfloor C_{\rm it}\tfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\beta}\log(\tfrac{n\varrho}{\beta\gamma})\rfloor
, we have that

\displaystyle\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i)},0)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:robust-sing-rel}}}{{\leq}}\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(t^{\prime})})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{lem:progress}}}{{\leq}}\zeta^{(i)}(z^{(\hat{t})})+q+1-k_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:zeta-dec-1}}}{{\leq}}\zeta^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)},q)+1-k_{i}.

(113)

The claimed bound now follows by rearranging.

∎

Proof of
Theorem

8.1
.

Let
t_{i},y^{(i)}
,
N^{(i)}
,
i\in\{0,\dots,T\}
be as defined in the overview. Recall that
N^{(0)}=\emptyset
,
t_{0}=0
and
t_{T}=K
. Let
D_{\rm it}
and
k_{i}\coloneqq\max\{0,\lfloor(t_{i}-t_{i-1}-1)/D_{\rm it}\rfloor\}
,
i\in[T]
, be as in
Lemma

8.3
. Proceeding in a similar manner to (
105
), we bound the number of iteration as
follows:

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=t_{T}-t_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{T}(t_{i}-t_{i-1}-1)+1\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}D_{\rm it}(k_{i}+1)\quad\left(~b+1\leq a(\lfloor\tfrac{b}{a}\rfloor+1),a,b\in\mathbb{N}~\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}D_{\rm it}\left(\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i-1)})-\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i)})+2\right)\hskip 92.0001pt\left(\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Gamma-D-change}\ref{it:zeta-dec} }\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}D_{\rm it}\left(\bar{\zeta}^{(i-1)}(y^{(i-1)})-\bar{\zeta}^{(i)}(y^{(i)})+|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|+2\right)\quad\left(\text{ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:Gamma-D-change}\ref{it:zeta-next} }\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{T}D_{\rm it}\left(|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|+2\right)\right)+D_{\rm it}(\bar{\zeta}^{(0)}(y^{(0)})-\bar{\zeta}^{(T)}(y^{(T)}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}D_{\rm it}\left(|N^{(i)}\Delta N^{(i-1)}|+2\right)\,,

where the last inequality uses that
\bar{\zeta}^{(0)}(y^{(0)})\leq\zeta^{(0)}(y^{(0)})=0
since
N^{(0)}=\emptyset
.
∎

9
Computing Approximate Singular Value Decompositions

In this section, we give our algorithm for computing approximate SVDs and prove
Theorem

6.16
.

We begin by presenting a deterministic strongly polynomial method for obtaining a
(n2^{n})
-approximate SVD for matrices
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
using QR decomposition with greedy column permutations. In the next
subsection, we demonstrate how to enhance the approximation factor to
(1+\varepsilon)
by leveraging
the classical power iteration.

Using QR decompositions to approximate the SVD is a well-established technique that dates back to
the mid-1960s
[
10
,
26
]
. Many existing approaches focus on finding
rank-revealing QR factorizations instead of a full
\varrho
-approximate SVD. These methods aim to
identify a number
k\leq n
such that
\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{M})\gg\sigma_{k+1}(\mathbf{M})
, along with an
approximate subspace for the eigenvalues
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{M}),\ldots,\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{M})
. This is achieved by
greedily selecting column permutations, followed by a standard QR factorization. The top
k\times k
block of the resulting upper triangular matrix provides an approximation of the subspace
corresponding to the top
k
singular values.

One of the earliest algorithms that utilizes column pivoting and subsequent QR decomposition was
proposed by Chan
[
12
]
, who attributes the procedure to
[
27
]
. He
achieves approximation guarantees similar to ours in
Lemma

9.3
. Algorithms with tighter
bounds (even polynomial instead of exponential) for rank-revealing QR factorizations can be found in

[
13
,
31
,
50
]
.

However, these algorithms are designed to work only for a fixed
k
, not as the
\varrho
-approximate
SVD requires for all
k
simultaneously. Further, the initial algorithms

[
10
,
26
]
critically rely on Householder reflection, which intrinsically make
use of unit vectors in the computations. Unit vectors however, can not in general be used in the
strongly polynomial model as they require the computation of square roots. The mentioned approach by
Chan
[
12
]
requires the computation of the smallest singular vector of the matrix. This
again can not be done in strongly polynomial time. More precisely, given a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, the algorithms requires solving
\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}x\right\rVert
. The
algorithm we present in this section, instead solves the problem
\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{\infty}=1}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}x\right\rVert
. As we will see below, this problem can be solved in strongly polynomial time. Also
note, that the objective values of the two minimization problems above, differ by at most a factor
of
\sqrt{m}
. This will explain why our algorithm achieves similar approximation guarantees as the
algorithm of Chan
[
12
]
.

In general, most works in the literature have not focused on strong polynomiality. Beyond the use
of square roots, many of these results require the computation of orthonormal matrices, which in
general is not achievable in strongly polynomial space. Additionally, it is important to avoid the
sequential computation of
\Omega(n)
Gram-Schmidt orthogonalizations (GSO). While a single GSO can
be performed in strongly polynomial time
[
30
]
, the size of the numbers may increase by a
polynomial factor. It remains unclear whether a sequence of super-logarithmically many GSO
computations can be performed in strongly polynomial time.

A good overview of QR-type algorithms with column pivoting can be found in

[
13
]
. They also present new algorithms for rank-revealing QR factorizations
with improved approximation guarantees. Although the authors do not emphasize this fact, some
algorithms in
[
13
]
, in particular
Greedy-I.1
,
Greedy-I.2
and

Greedy-I.3
, can be implemented in strongly polynomial time. Furthermore, they give similar
approximation guarantees as us in
Lemma

9.3
for the algorithm
Greedy-I.3
.

Nonetheless, we present a self-contained new algorithm here for the sake of completeness and to
focus on achieving strong polynomiality. While the framework of our algorithm fits into the regime
of performing column pivoting and subsequent QR decomposition, we believe that the exact rule for
the column pivoting is novel.

Very recently, Diakonikolas, Tzamos, and Kane
[
22
]
also
provided a strongly polynomial
(1+\varepsilon)
-singular value decomposition
algorithm based on randomized power iteration (the randomness corresponds to
a random choice of initial basis of the input space). At a high level, our
algorithm removes the need for randomness by using a suitable greedy QR
decomposition (see the description below). In constrast to our algorithm
however, the approximate singular value decomposition
of
[
22
]
outputs a decomposition of
a matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}
(as in
Definition

2.16
) that is
\varepsilon
-“spectrally close” to
\mathbf{M}
. This provides a
somewhat stronger guarantee than what is needed for our formalization of the

(1+\varepsilon)
-
SVD
problem, which only requires an orthogonal basis inducing a chain of
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate singular subspaces. While we expect that one can extract such a
decomposition in a blackbox manner from any
(1+\varepsilon^{\prime})
-
SVD

solution, for a suitably chosen
\varepsilon^{\prime}\ll\varepsilon
, for the sake of simplicity
we do not pursue this direction here.

We proceed by describing our main algorithm on a high level. For a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
,
we will use the following procedure (Algorithm
3
): For all columns
j
of
\mathbf{M}
,
consider the projection of the column
\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,j}
onto the orthogonal complement of the span of
all the other columns
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,[n]\setminus\{j\}})
. Then, remove the column
j_{\min}

from the matrix for which the norm of this projection is the smallest and recurse on the remaining
matrix
\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,[n]\setminus\{j_{\min}\}}
. When this process finishes, we obtain a permutation

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}
of the columns, given by the order in which they were removed from the matrix. It turns
out, that the norms of the columns of the orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{Q}
obtained from a Gram-Schmidt
process on the permuted matrix
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}
(the first column removed from
\mathbf{M}
is the last column in
its reordering) provide an exponential approximation of the singular values of the original matrix

\mathbf{M}
. The main observation for the proof is that the matrix
\mathbf{R}
in the Gram-Schmidt process,
uniquely defined by
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{Q}
, has only exponential condition number.

1

Input :
Matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
.

Output :
Matrix
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and a vector
s\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
(\mathbf{B},s)
is a
(n2^{n})
-approximate SVD
(
Definition

6.13
).

2

J_{n}\leftarrow[n]
;

3

for

t=n
down to
1

do

4

for

i\in J_{t}

do

5

w^{(t,i)}\leftarrow\mathbf{0}_{n}
;

6

w^{(t,i)}_{i}\leftarrow 1
;

7

w^{(t,i)}_{J_{t}\setminus\{i\}}\leftarrow-[\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}}]^{+}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}
;

8

K_{t}\leftarrow\argmin_{j\in J_{t}}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,j)}\right\rVert
;

9

\pi(t)\leftarrow\text{any element in }\argmin_{j\in K_{t}}\left\lVert w^{(t,j)}\right\rVert
;

10

11

v^{(t)}\leftarrow w^{(t,\pi(t))}
;

12

q^{(t)}\leftarrow\mathbf{M}v^{(t)}
;

13

J_{t-1}\leftarrow J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}
;

14

15

\mathbf{P}\leftarrow(1_{i=\pi(j)})_{i,j\in[n]}
;

16

\mathbf{R}\leftarrow\mathbf{P}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}v^{(1)}&\dots&v^{(n)}\end{bmatrix}
;

17

\mathbf{Q}\leftarrow\begin{bmatrix}q^{(1)}&\dots&q^{(n)}\end{bmatrix}
;

18

\mathbf{B}\leftarrow\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}
;

19

for

i=1,\ldots,n-1

do

20

\mathbf{B}_{\bullet,i}\leftarrow\mathbf{B}_{\bullet,i}-\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\bullet,>i})}(\mathbf{B}_{\bullet,i})

;

21

//
Reverse Gram-Schmidt Orthogonalization

22

s\leftarrow\mathbf{0}_{n}
;

23

for

i=1,\ldots,n

do

24

s_{i}\leftarrow 2^{n}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert_{1}
;

25

return

(\mathbf{B},s)

Algorithm 3

Greedy-SVD

It is not hard to see that the output of
3Greedy-SVD
satisfies that
\mathbf{Q}
is precisely
the result of Gram Schmidt orthogonalization (GSO) on the matrix
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}
, i.e., the matrix
\mathbf{M}

after its columns have been permuted according to
\mathbf{P}
.

Before we begin the analysis, we introduce some additional notation and
recall some fundamental matrix inequalities. For a matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, we use
\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2}\coloneqq\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{M})
to denote the
operator norm,
\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{\rm F}\coloneqq\sqrt{\sum_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}\mathbf{M}_{ij}^{2}}
to denote the Frobenius norm, and
\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{\infty}\coloneqq\max_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}|\mathbf{M}_{ij}|
to denote the maximum absolute value
of any entry. We will use the inequality
\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\right\rVert_{\rm F}
,
and if
\mathbf{M}
is invertible, the relation
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{M})=\frac{1}{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}^{-1}\right\rVert_{2}}\geq\frac{1}{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\rm F}}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

The matrices
\mathbf{P}
,
\mathbf{R}
, and
\mathbf{Q}

constructed in
3Greedy-SVD
(Algorithm
3
) have the following properties:

(i)

\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a permutation matrix,

(ii)

\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is upper triangular,

(iii)

\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
has orthogonal columns,

(iv)

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{Q}
,

(v)

\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,s}\right\rVert\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,t}\right\rVert
for all
s,t\in[n]
such that
s\leq t
,

(vi)

\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,t}\right\rVert=\min_{j\in[t]}\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{t},x_{j}=1}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,[t]}x\right\rVert
,

(vii)

\left\lVert\mathbf{R}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
and
\mathbf{R}_{t,t}=1
,
\forall t\in[n]
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\pi
is a
bijection from
[n]
to
[n]
. Therefore, by definition of
\mathbf{P}
in line
3
we
obtain
(i)
.

Let
t\in[n]
and
i\in J_{t}
, Then, noting that
w^{(t,i)}_{i}=1
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}w^{(t,i)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}}w^{(t,i)}_{J_{t}\setminus\{i\}}+\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}

(114)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}}[\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}}]^{+}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}+\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}

\displaystyle=-\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}})}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}+\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}})^{\perp}}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}\,,

where the third equality follows by
Proposition

2.14
part (
1
).
This furthermore implies, that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,i)}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}})^{\perp}}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert=\min_{z\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{i\}})}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,i}+z\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{J_{t}},x_{i}=1}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}}x\right\rVert\,.

(115)

Now,
(vi)
follows by (
115
) and the definition
of
\mathbf{P}
, which gives
\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,[t]}
as
\pi([t])=J_{t}
.
From here, we also conclude that for any
j\in J_{t}\setminus\{i\}
with
w^{(t,i)}_{j}\neq 0
, we
have that
[w^{(t,i)}_{j}]^{-1}w^{(t,i)}_{J_{t}}
is a feasible vector for the minimization problem
on the right most side of (
115
) for index
j
. Therefore, we have that

\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,j)}\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{|w^{(t,i)}_{j}|}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,i)}\right\rVert\,.

(116)

We distinguish two cases for the norm of
\mathbf{M}w^{(t,\pi(t))}
. If
\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,\pi(t))}\right\rVert>0
, then for all
j\in J_{t}
, the inequality (
116
) gives
|w_{j}^{(t,\pi(t))}|\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,\pi(t))}\right\rVert/\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,i)}\right\rVert\leq 1
by
\pi(t)\in K_{t}
and the definition of
K_{t}
.

In the other case we have that
\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,\pi(t))}\right\rVert=0
. Then, notice that for any
j\in J_{t}
such that
w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{j}\neq 0
, we have by (
116
) that
\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,j)}\right\rVert=0
and therefore
j\in K_{t}
. In particular, we have that
\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,j}\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{j\}})
. Therefore, by
Proposition

2.14
part (
2
), for
j\in\operatorname{supp}(w^{(t,\pi(t)})
we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert w^{(t,j)}_{J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{j\}})^{+}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,j}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\min\big\{\left\lVert u\right\rVert:u\in\mathbb{R}^{|J_{t}|-1},\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}u=\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{j\}})}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,j})\big\}

\displaystyle=\min\big\{\left\lVert u\right\rVert:u\in\mathbb{R}^{|J_{t}|-1},\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}u=\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,j}\big\}

(117)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\lVert w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}\right\rVert}{|w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{j}|}\,,

where the inequality follows by noting that the vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}
defined as
u=-[w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{j}]^{-1}w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}
is a feasible solution to the minimization problem in (
117
) as

\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}u=-[w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{j}]^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{J_{t}\setminus\{j\}}=[w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{j}]^{-1}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,j}w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{j}=\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,j}\,.

This gives for all
j\in\operatorname{supp}(w^{(t,\pi(t))})\subseteq K_{t}
that

|w_{j}^{(t,\pi(t))}|\leq\frac{\left\lVert w_{J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}}^{(t,\pi(t))}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert w_{J_{t}\setminus\{i\}}^{(t,i)}\right\rVert}=\frac{\sqrt{\left\lVert w^{(t,\pi(t))}\right\rVert^{2}-1}}{\sqrt{\left\lVert w^{(t,i)}\right\rVert^{2}-1}}\leq 1\,,

where the last inequality follows by definition of
\pi(t)
. We can therefore conclude that
\left\lVert v^{(t)}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert w^{(t,\pi(t))}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
for all
t\in[n]
, recalling that
v^{(t)}_{\pi(t)}=w^{(t,\pi(t))}_{\pi(t)}=1
.

Let us now show the statements for
\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{P}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}v^{(1)}&\dots&v^{(n)}\end{bmatrix}
. As
\mathbf{P}
is a permutation matrix by
(i)
, we have
shown that
\left\lVert\mathbf{R}\right\rVert_{\infty}=1
and furthermore
\mathbf{R}_{t,t}=1
,
\forall t\in[n]
, as
\mathbf{R}_{t,t}=(\mathbf{P}^{\top})_{t,\bullet}v^{(t)}=v^{(t)}_{\pi(t)}=1
for all
t\in[n]
. Therefore,
(vii)
holds. To show that
\mathbf{R}
is upper triangular,
recall that
\operatorname{supp}(v_{t})\subseteq J_{t}
and
\pi([t])=J_{t}
. Therefore, letting
\pi^{-1}
denote the inverse permutation, we get
\operatorname{supp}(\mathbf{P}^{\top}v^{(t)})\subseteq\pi^{-1}(J_{t})=[t]
as needed for
(ii)
.

Further, note that by construction
\mathbf{M}v^{(t)}=q^{(t)}
,
t\in[n]
. Therefore,

\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{P}^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}v^{(1)}&\dots&v^{(n)}\end{bmatrix}=\mathbf{M}\begin{bmatrix}v^{(1)}&\dots&v^{(n)}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}q^{(1)}&\dots&q^{(n)}\end{bmatrix}=\mathbf{Q},

as needed for
(iv)
. To see
that
\mathbf{Q}
has orthogonal columns, note that for by definition of
q^{(t)}
we have with
(
114
) that

q^{(t)}=\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}})^{\perp}}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,\pi(t)}\,,

(118)

and so in particular
q^{(t)}\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}})^{\perp}
.
However, since for
1\leq s<t
we have that
q^{(s)}\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}})
. We conclude that
q^{(t)}
is
orthogonal to
q^{(s)}
, proving
(iii)
.

To show
(v)
, note that for
1\leq s<t\leq n
, the inclusion

\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{s}\setminus\{\pi(s)\}})\subseteq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}})
holds, and hence

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,s}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert q^{(s)}\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{s}\setminus\{\pi(s)\}})^{\perp}}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,\pi(s)}\right\rVert\quad\left(\text{by \eqref{eq:projection-expression-for-q}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}})^{\perp}}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,\pi(s)}\right\rVert\quad\left(\text{$\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}})^{\perp}\subseteq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{s}\setminus\{\pi(s)\}})^{\perp}$}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,\pi(s))}\right\rVert\quad\left(\text{by \eqref{eq:projection-expression-for-w-t-i}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{M}w^{(t,\pi(t))}\right\rVert\quad\left(\text{by choice of $\pi(t)$ }\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Pi_{\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,J_{t}\setminus\{\pi(t)\}})^{\perp}}\mathbf{M}_{\bullet,\pi(t)}\right\rVert\quad\left(\text{by \eqref{eq:projection-expression-for-w-t-i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert q^{(t)}\right\rVert\quad\left(\text{by \eqref{eq:projection-expression-for-q}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,t}\right\rVert\,.

This proves the lemma.
∎

The following proposition shows that the matrix
\mathbf{R}
computed by

3Greedy-SVD
is “well-conditioned”.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
\mathbf{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be an upper triangular matrix with diagonal
\mathbf{1}_{n}

and with entries of absolute value at most
1
. Then,
\mathbf{R}^{-1}
is upper triangular with
diagonal
\mathbf{1}_{n}
and
|\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{ij}|\leq\max\{1,2^{j-i-1}\}
,
i,j\in[n]
,
i\leq j
. In
particular,
\left\lVert\mathbf{R}^{-1}\right\rVert_{2}\leq 2^{n}
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction on
n
. The base case
n=1
is trivial, so assume
n>1
. Then, it
is directly verifiable that

\mathbf{R}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{R}_{[n-1],[n-1]}^{-1}&-\mathbf{R}_{[n-1],[n-1]}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{[n-1],n}\\
\mathbf{0}_{n-1}^{\top}&1\end{bmatrix}.

By the induction hypothesis, it holds that
|\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{ij}|\leq\max\{1,2^{j-i-1}\}
,
i,j\in[n-1]
,
i\leq j
. Note that
\mathbf{R}^{-1}
is upper triangular as claimed. We now prove the
coefficient bound for
\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{i,n}
, for
i\leq n
. If
i=n
,
\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{i,i}=1
, as
needed. For
i<n
, using that
\mathbf{R}
has entries at most
1
we get that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{in}|

\displaystyle=\big|(\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{[n-1],[n-1]}\mathbf{R}_{[n-1],n})_{i}\big|=\big|\sum_{j=i}^{n-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{i,j}\mathbf{R}_{j,n}\big|\leq\sum_{j=i}^{n-1}|\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{i,j}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=i}^{n-1}\max\{1,2^{j-i-1}\}=1+\sum_{l=0}^{n-i-2}2^{l}=\max\{1,2^{n-i-1}\},

as needed. For the last statement, by a direct calculation

\left\lVert\mathbf{R}^{-1}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{R}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\rm F}^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=i}^{n}\max\{1,4^{j-i-1}\}=\frac{2}{3}n-\frac{1}{9}+\frac{4^{n}}{9}\leq 4^{n},

as needed.
∎

We now show how
3Greedy-SVD
produces a

(n2^{n})
-approximation of the singular values and corresponding subspaces of
\mathbf{M}
.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a matrix and
\mathbf{P}
,
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{Q}
be the matrices constructed in

3Greedy-SVD
applied to
\mathbf{M}
. Let
V_{\geq i}\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\geq i})
and
similarly
V_{\leq i}\coloneqq\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\leq i})
Then, we have that

2^{-n}\max_{v\in V_{\geq i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}v\right\rVert}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert}\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\leq n\min_{v\in V_{\leq i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}v\right\rVert}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert}\,.

Furthermore,
\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{n}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})
.

Proof.

For the lower bound on
\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert
, we have that

\displaystyle\max_{v\in V_{\geq i}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}v\right\rVert}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-i+1}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\geq i}x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\geq i}x\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-i+1}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,\geq i}x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\geq i}x\right\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n-i+1}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\geq i}x\right\rVert}\quad\left(\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:approx-svd-properties} \ref{it:Q-orthogonal}, \ref{it:Q-norm-monotonicity}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\left\lVert\mathbf{R}^{-1}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\,,\quad\left(\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:well-conditioned}}\right)

proving the inequality.

For the upper bound on
\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert
, note that

\displaystyle\min_{v\in V_{\leq i}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}v\right\rVert}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\leq i}x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\leq i}x\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,\leq i}x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\leq i}x\right\rVert}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\frac{\left\lVert x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{R}_{\bullet,\leq i}x\right\rVert}\quad\left(\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:approx-svd-properties} \ref{it:Q-orthogonal}, \ref{it:Q-norm-monotonicity}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\left\lVert\mathbf{R}\right\rVert_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert}{n}\,.\quad\left(\text{$\left\lVert\mathbf{R}\right\rVert\leq n$ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:approx-svd-properties} \ref{it:R-properties}}\right)

It remains to prove the furthermore statement. By
Proposition

2.20
, there exists a subspace
U_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\dim(U_{i})=n-i+1
such that
\max_{x\in U_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert}=\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P})=\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})
. By dimension counting, there exists
\bar{x}\in U_{i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
such that
\operatorname{supp}(\bar{x})\subseteq[i]
. Therefore,

\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\geq\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\bar{x}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\mathbf{P}\bar{x}\right\rVert}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}\bar{x}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\bar{x}\right\rVert_{\infty}}\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\min_{j\in[i]}\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{i},x_{j}=1}\left\lVert\mathbf{M}\mathbf{P}_{\bullet,[i]}x\right\rVert=\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert}{\sqrt{n}},

where the last equality follows form
Lemma

9.1

(vi)
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem 9.4
.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a matrix. The output of

3Greedy-SVD
on
\mathbf{M}
is a
(n2^{n})
-
SVD
approximation of

\mathbf{M}
in the sense of
Definition

6.13
. Furthermore, the algorithm runs in strongly
polynomial time
O(n^{2}\max(m,n)^{3})
.

Proof.

First, note that
\mathbf{B}
is an orthogonal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Furthermore,
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq i})=V_{\geq i}
. Using
Lemma

9.3
, we therefore have that

\max_{v\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq i})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}v\right\rVert}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert}=\max_{v\in V_{\geq i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}v\right\rVert}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert}\leq 2^{n}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\leq 2^{n}\sqrt{n}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\,.

(119)

Using the variational characterization of singular
values (
15
) we obtain that

\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\leq\max_{v\in V_{\geq i}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}v\right\rVert}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert}\leq 2^{n}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\\

(120)

Together with
Lemma

9.3
, this proves that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\leq\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\leq 2^{n}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert\,.

(121)

In particular, by choice of
s_{i}=2^{n}\left\lVert\mathbf{Q}_{\bullet,i}\right\rVert_{1}
for
i\in[n]
, we get that

\frac{1}{2^{n}n}s\leq\sigma(\mathbf{M})\leq s\,,

(122)

as needed for
Definition

6.13
. The choice of
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1}
instead of
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}
in definition of
s
is necessary to ensure that the output of the algorithm has polynomial bit complexity.

For the strongly polynomial guarantees, it remains to check that all the intermediate iterates have polynomial bit complexity. To see this, note first that the columns of
\mathbf{R}
correspond to solutions of a well-described linear system in the original matrix
\mathbf{M}
, and thus has bit-complexity polynomially related to that of
\mathbf{M}
. From here,
\mathbf{B}
corresponds to a “reverse order” GSO applied to
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}
(or again, the columns are solutions to well-described linear systems in
\mathbf{P}\mathbf{R}
), and hence
\mathbf{B}
also has bit-complexity polynomially related to that of
\mathbf{M}
(see, e.g.,
[
30
, Section 1.4]
for a thorough overview of the bit-complexity of GSO).

Let us now prove the statment about the running time. Using the characterization in
Proposition

2.15
and further noting that the required rank factorization can be computed in
O(\max(m,n)^{3})
, we are able to compute the pseudoinverse of an
m\times n
matrix as well as the projection onto the image of an
m\times n
matrix in time
O(\max(m,n)^{3})
. Therefore, a single execution of both Lines
3
and
3
of Algorithm
3
takes
O(\max(m,n)^{3})
. The repeated execution of these lines dominates the overall running time of the algorithm, hence the overall running time of the algorithm can be bounded by
O(n\cdot n\cdot\max(m,n)^{3})=O(n^{2}\max(m,n)^{3})
.

This completes the proof of the theorem. ∎

9.1
Boosting via the Power Method

In this section, we demonstrate a method to enhance the approximation ratio of any algorithm for the

\varrho
-
SVD
problem (as in
Definition

6.13
) to a
(1+\varepsilon)
-
SVD

approximation. This is possible provided
\varrho=\varrho(\dim(\mathbf{M}))
, i.e., the approximation ratio
\varrho
is a function of the dimension of the matrix only and does not depend on the
matrix’s conditioning. The strategy involves using a low accuracy

\varrho
-
SVD
approximation to calculate an approximate SVD of the matrix
\mathbf{S}\coloneqq(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})^{p}
. Here, a large power
p=O(n/\varepsilon\cdot\log(\varrho/\varepsilon))
is used to achieve the
desired error
\varepsilon
. We will then show that the approximate SVD of
\mathbf{S}
is in fact a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximation of the SVD of
\mathbf{M}
.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
and
\varrho\geq\varepsilon
. Then, there is a reduction
from
(1+\varepsilon)
-
SVD
on an
m\times n
matrix to

\varrho
-
SVD
on one
n\times n
and one
m\times n
matrix that
runs in time
O(mn^{2}+n^{3}\log(\log(2+\varrho)/\varepsilon))
, and
requires space polynomial in
m,n,\log(2+\varrho)/\varepsilon
and the bit-encoding length of

\varepsilon,\varrho
and the input matrix.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
be a
m\times n
matrix. The reduction proceeds as follows. Compute
\mathbf{S}\coloneqq(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})^{p}
for
p\coloneqq\lceil\log_{1+\varepsilon/3}(\varrho)/2\rceil
. Let
(\mathbf{B},s)
be
the output of
\varrho
-
SVD
on
\mathbf{S}
and
(\cdot,s^{\prime})
be the output of

\varrho
-
SVD
on
\mathbf{M}
. For each
i\in[n]
, apply binary search to
compute
\hat{s}_{i}=s^{\prime}_{i}(1+\varepsilon/3)^{-2k_{i}}
,
-1\leq k_{i}\leq 2p
, satisfying
s_{i}^{1/(2p)}\leq\hat{s}_{i}\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)s_{i}^{1/(2p)}
. Finally, return
(\mathbf{B},\hat{s})
.

We first proof correctness of the reduction. Consider a singular value
decomposition
\mathbf{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})

u_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
of
\mathbf{M}
,
where
r=\mathrm{rank}(\mathbf{M})
. Fix
i\in[n]
. By definition of
\mathbf{B}
, we have for all
x\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq i})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
that
\left\lVert\mathbf{S}x\right\rVert_{2}/\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2}\leq s_{i}\leq\varrho\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{S})
, which implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{M})^{4p}\langle v_{j},x\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{M})^{2p}v_{j}\langle v_{j},x\rangle\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert\mathbf{S}x\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq s_{i}^{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}\leq\varrho^{2}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{S})^{2}\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}=\varrho^{2}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})^{4p}\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}.

(123)

Therefore,
\forall x\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq i})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{M}x\right\rVert^{4p}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{M})^{2}\langle v_{j},x\rangle^{2}\right)^{2p}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{M})^{2}\frac{\langle v_{j},x\rangle^{2}}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}\right)^{2p}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{4p}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sigma_{j}(\mathbf{M})^{4p}\frac{\langle v_{j},x\rangle^{2}}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{2}}\quad\left(\text{Jensen's inequality}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{4p}s_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert^{4p}\cdot\varrho^{2}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})^{4p}\left(\text{by \eqref{eq:boosting}}\right)\,,

(124)

and so

\max_{x\in\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{B}_{\geq i})\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{M}x\right\rVert}{\left\lVert x\right\rVert}\leq s_{i}^{1/(2p)}\leq\varrho^{1/(2p)}\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)\sigma_{i}(\mathbf{M})\,.

(125)

From the above, note that
(\mathbf{B},s^{1/(2p)})
is already a solution to

(1+\varepsilon)
-
SVD
on
\mathbf{M}
. Unfortunately, we cannot compute the vector

s^{1/(2p)}
in strongly polynomial time, which motivates the binary search
used to construct the approximation
\hat{s}
. By the guarantees of
s
and

s^{\prime}
, we have that

s^{\prime}(1+\varepsilon/3)^{-2p}\leq\frac{s^{\prime}}{\varrho}\leq\sigma(\mathbf{M})\leq s^{1/(2p)}\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)\sigma(\mathbf{M})\leq s^{\prime}(1+\varepsilon/3),

and thus the claimed range for
k_{i}
,
i\in[n]
, in the binary search is
correct. From here, by the guarantees on
\hat{s}
, since
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2]
,
we have that

s^{1/(2p)}\leq\hat{s}\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)s^{1/(2p)}\leq(1+\varepsilon/3)^{2}\sigma(\mathbf{M})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\sigma(\mathbf{M}).

Therefore,
(\mathbf{B},\hat{s})
is a valid solution to
(1+\varepsilon)
-
SVD
.

We now justify the running time of the reduction. Since
p=\Theta(\log(2+\varrho)/\varepsilon)
, we can compute
\mathbf{S}=(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})^{p}
in time

O(mn^{2}+n^{3}\log(p))=O(mn^{2}+n^{3}\log(\log(2+\varrho)/\varepsilon))
by repeated
squaring of
\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M}
. For each
i\in[n]
, the corresponding binary
search requires
O(\log p)
comparisons, where each comparison are of the
form
s_{i}\leq(s^{\prime})^{2p}(1+\varepsilon/3)^{-2k}
for
-1\leq k\leq 2p
.
Lastly, the reduction makes two
\varrho
-
SVD
calls, one to
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and one to
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
.

It remains to show the claim about the space needed by the algorithm. To this
end, it suffices to show that the matrix
\mathbf{S}
has a bit-encoding length
polynomial in
\log(2+\varrho)/\varepsilon^{-1}
and the bit-encoding of
\mathbf{M}
. To this end, let the
input matrix
\mathbf{M}
be given as
\mathbf{M}_{i,j}=p_{ij}/q_{ij}
for
i\in[m],j\in[n]
with
p_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. We
can write
\mathbf{M}=k^{-1}\bar{\mathbf{M}}
, where
k=\operatorname{lcm}_{i,j}{q_{ij}}
and
\bar{\mathbf{M}}_{ij}=|p_{ij}/q_{ij}|\cdot\operatorname{lcm}_{i,j}q_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\operatorname{lcm}

denotes the least common multiple. In particular,
\bar{\mathbf{M}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
and
