\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{M}}\right\rVert_{\rm F}\leq\sum_{ij}|p_{ij}|\cdot\prod_{ij}q_{ij}
. Therefore, we can write
\mathbf{S}=(\mathbf{M}^{\top}\mathbf{M})^{p}=k^{-{2p}}(\bar{\mathbf{M}}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{M}})^{p}
, where
\left\lVert(\bar{\mathbf{M}}^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{M}})^{p}\right\rVert_{\rm F}\leq\left\lVert\bar{\mathbf{M}}\right\rVert^{2p}_{\rm F}\leq(\sum_{ij}|p_{ij}|\prod_{ij}|q_{ij}|)^{2p}
. In
particular, the entries of
\mathbf{S}
can be written in a number of bits that is
polynomial in
p
and the number of bits of
\mathbf{M}
. Recalling that
p=O(\log(2+\varrho)/\varepsilon)
finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem

6.16
is a now a simple consequence of
Lemma

9.5
and
Theorem

9.4
.
See
6.16

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon=\min\{\varrho-1,1/2\}
, and apply the reduction in

Lemma

9.5
with parameters
(\varepsilon,n2^{n})
, where we use

Theorem

9.4
to implement the
(n2^{n})
-
SVD
algorithm. The
space guarantees now follow from the guarantees of

Theorem

9.4
and
Lemma

9.5
. For the running time, observe that
Theorem

9.4
requires
O(n^{2}\max(m,n)^{3})
operations, while the application of
Lemma

9.5
requires
O(mn^{2}+n^{3}\log(\log(2+n2^{n})/\min(\varrho-1,\frac{1}{2})))=O(mn^{2}+n^{3}\log(n+\frac{1}{\varrho-1}))
operations. The sum of these running times is
O(n^{2}\max(m,n)^{3}\log(n+\frac{1}{\varrho-1}))
, which completes the proof.
∎

10
Self-Concordant Barrier Central Paths

The theory of self-concordant barriers, introduced by Nesterov and Nemirovski
[
49
]
, is used to describe interior point methods in a general setting. In this section, we show that the central paths obtained from self-concordant barriers relate to the max central path, as in the case of the log-barrier. From this, we deduce a proof of
Theorem

1.7
.

We follow the presentation of self-concordant barriers made by Renegar
[
52
]
. We consider a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
function
f\colon D\to\mathbb{R}
with open convex domain
D\subseteq E
where
E=x^{0}+L
is an affine subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We recall that, for
x\in D
, the gradient
\nabla f(x)
of
f
at
x
is the element of
L
such that for all
h\in L
,
\left\langle\nabla f(x),h\right\rangle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{f(x+th)-f(x)}{t}
. Moreover, the Hessian
\nabla^{2}f(x)
of
f
at
x
is the self-adjoint linear operator from
L
to itself such that for all
h,k\in L
,
\left\langle\nabla^{2}f(x)h,k\right\rangle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{\left\langle\nabla f(x+tk)-\nabla f(x),h\right\rangle}{t}
. We assume that
\nabla^{2}f(x)
is positive definite, i.e.,
\left\langle\nabla^{2}f(x)h,h\right\rangle>0
for all
h\in L
,
h\neq 0
. In particular,
f
is a strictly convex function on
D
. Given
x\in D
, we introduce
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{x}
, the norm over
L
associated with the Hessian
\nabla^{2}f(x)
, i.e.,
\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{x}\coloneqq\big(\left\langle\nabla^{2}f(x)z,z\right\rangle\big)^{1/2}
for all
z\in L
. We define
\mathcal{B}_{x}(y;r)\coloneqq\{z\in D:\left\lVert y-z\right\rVert_{x}<r\}
, the open ball with center
y\in D
and radius
r\geq 0
with respect to the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{x}
.

The function
f
is
(strongly nondegenerate) self-concordant
if, for all
x\in D
, we have
\mathcal{B}_{x}(x;1)\subseteq D
, and for all
y\in\mathcal{B}_{x}(x;1)
and nonzero vector
v\in L
,

1-\left\lVert y-x\right\rVert_{x}\leq\frac{\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{x}}{\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{y}}\leq\frac{1}{1-\left\lVert y-x\right\rVert_{x}}\,.

We define the
complexity value
as

\vartheta_{f}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in D}\sup_{\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{x}\leq 1}\left\langle\nabla f(x),h\right\rangle^{2}\,.

If
\vartheta_{f}<\infty
, then
f
is called a
self-concordant barrier
.

Let
f
be self-concordant barrier over the (relative) interior of the polyhedron
\mathcal{P}
. The
central path
associated with
f
is the function that maps
\mu>0
to the unique minimizer of the function
x\mapsto\left\langle c,x\right\rangle+\mu f(x)
. When
f
is (the restriction to
E
of) the log-barrier
x\mapsto-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(x_{i})
, the scalar
\mu
is used as the parameter of the central path. In the case of general barriers, it is more convenient to parametrize the central path by the optimality gap
g
. This is made possible by the fact that, if any two points of the central path have the same optimality gap
g
, they both minimize the (strictly convex) function
f
over
\{x\in\mathcal{P}\colon\left\langle c,x\right\rangle=g\}
, thus they are equal. Following this, we denote by
x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)
the unique point of the central path induced by the barrier
f
with optimality gap
g>0
.

In the next statement, we generalize
1.2
to the case of the central path associated with the self-concordant barrier
f
.

Proposition 10.1
.

For all
g>0
, we have
\frac{1}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)}x^{\mathfrak{m}}(g)<x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}(g)
.

Proof.

The right hand side inequality follows from
x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)\in\mathcal{P}_{g}
.
Using
[
52
, Theorem 2.3.4]
and the fact that
\mu\nabla f(x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g))+c\in L^{\perp}
for some
\mu>0
, we know that
\mathcal{P}_{g}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)}(x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g);4\vartheta_{f}+1)
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{g}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\geq 0}
,
[
3
, Lemma 4]
shows that
y<2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)
for all
y\in\mathcal{B}_{x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)}(x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g);4\vartheta_{f}+1)
. We deduce that
x^{\mathfrak{m}}(g)<2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)
.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

1.7
.

Proof (
Theorem

1.7
).

Let
x^{(0)},\dots,x^{(T)}
be the successive iterates of the IPM. We denote by
g^{(0)},\dots,g^{(T)}
their respective optimality gap, where
g^{(0)}\geq g_{0}
and
g^{(T)}\leq g_{1}
.

Let
i\in[n]
. We claim that,
\forall k\in[T]
, the segment
[(g^{(k-1)},x^{(k-1)}_{i}),(g^{(k)},x^{(k)}_{i})]
is included in the neighborhood
{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{mp}}_{i}(\theta^{\prime})
for
\theta^{\prime}=1-\frac{1-\theta}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)}
. Indeed, for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
, the point
x=(1-\lambda)x^{(k-1)}+\lambda x^{(k)}
has optimality gap
g=(1-\lambda)g^{(k-1)}+\lambda g^{(k)}
. As
x\in\mathcal{P}
, we have
x_{i}\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
. Moreover, since
x\geq(1-\theta)x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)
,
Proposition

10.1
ensures that
x_{i}>\frac{1-\theta}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)}x^{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(g)
. Hence,
(g,x)\in{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathfrak{mp}}_{i}(\theta^{\prime})
. We deduce by
Lemma

4.4
that
T\geq\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta^{\prime},i}(g_{1},g_{0})
.

Consequently, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta^{\prime},i}(g_{1},g_{0})\leq nT
. By
Theorem

1.4
, the number
T^{\prime}
of iterations performed by the algorithm
2SLLS-IPM
is in

O\left(\sqrt{n}\log\left(\frac{n}{1-\theta}\right)\min\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{p}}_{\theta,i}(g_{1},g_{0}),\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{SLC}^{\mathrm{d}}_{\theta,i}(g_{1},g_{0})\right\}\right)\,.

(126)

This implies that

T^{\prime}=O\left(n^{1.5}\log\left(\frac{n\vartheta_{f}}{1-\theta}\right)T\right)\,.

∎

Theorem

1.7
relates the iteration complexity of general
barrier IPMs traversing the wide neighborhood
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\theta)
as
defined in (
6
) to the iteration
complexity of our IPM. Our wide neighborhood definition is however not
entirely standard, and in particular, it does not map directly to the
more standard neighbhorhoods such as those based on the Newton decrement. Furthermore, it does not obviously capture IPMs using primal-dual neighborhoods.

In
[
3
, Section 4.1]
however, it has been shown that all the neighborhoods of self-concordant central paths used in the literature (including that of the log-barrier central path like the projections of
\mathcal{N}^{2}(\beta)
,
0<\beta<1/4
, and
\mathcal{N}^{-\infty}(\theta)
,
0<\theta<1
, to primal variables) are actually contained in
multiplicative neighborhoods
, i.e., sets of form

\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\underline{m},\overline{m})=\big\{x\in\mathcal{P}:\exists g\,\kern 5.0pt\underline{m}x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)\leq x\leq\overline{m}x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)\big\}\qquad(0<\underline{m}\leq 1\leq\overline{m})\,.

The latter turn out to be essentially equivalent to our definition of wide
neighborhoods in (
6
). This is justified
by the following lemma:

Lemma 10.2
.

We have
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\theta)\subseteq\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}\big(1-\theta,2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)\big)
for all
\theta\in(0,1)
, and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\underline{m},\overline{m})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}\big(\frac{\underline{m}}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)\overline{m}}\big)
for all
0<\underline{m}\leq 1\leq\overline{m}
.

Proof.

For the first statement,
let
\theta\in(0,1)
, and
x\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\theta)
, and let
g=\left\langle c,x\right\rangle-v^{*}
denote the optimality gap of
x
. The inequality
x\geq(1-\theta)x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)
follows by the definition of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\theta)
. We have
x\leq x^{\mathfrak{m}}(g)
since
x\in\mathcal{P}
, and so
x\leq 2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g)
by
Proposition

10.1
.

For the second statement,
let
0<\underline{m}\leq 1\leq\overline{m}
, and let
(\tilde{g},x)
be such that
\underline{m}x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\tilde{g})

\leq x\leq\overline{m}x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\tilde{g})
. Let
s^{\star}\geq 0
denote a dual optimal solution to (
LP
). The optimality gap
g=\left\langle c,x\right\rangle-v^{*}=\left\langle s^{\star},x\right\rangle
of
x
satisfies
g\leq\overline{m}\left\langle s^{\star},x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\tilde{g})\right\rangle=\overline{m}\tilde{g}
(since
s^{\star}\geq 0
). Then, we have

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\underline{m}}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)}x^{\mathfrak{m}}(\tilde{g})

\displaystyle\left(\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:mcp-sccp}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\underline{m}}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)\overline{m}}x^{\mathfrak{m}}(\overline{m}\tilde{g})

\displaystyle\left(\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lemma:mcp_subhomogeneous}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\underline{m}}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)\overline{m}}x^{\mathfrak{m}}(g)

\displaystyle\left(\text{as $g\leq\overline{m}\tilde{g}$}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\underline{m}}{2(2\vartheta_{f}+1)\overline{m}}x^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(g).

\displaystyle\left(\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:mcp-sccp}}\right)

∎

As a consequence, the neighborhoods
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{cp}(f)}(\theta)
are flexible enough
to capture all known
neighborhoods of central paths. Therefore,
Theorem

1.7

indeed shows that our IPM is approximately optimal when compared against
essentially any straight-line following IPM.

Appendix A
Missing Proofs in Section
2.3

Proof of
Proposition

2.7
.

We show the first part. In particular, we show that
\ker(\operatorname{ad}(T))=V\cap\operatorname{im}(T)^{\perp}
:

\displaystyle\ker(\operatorname{ad}(T))

\displaystyle=\{y\in V:\left\langle\operatorname{ad}(T)y,\operatorname{ad}(T)y\right\rangle=0\}=\{y\in V:\left\langle\operatorname{ad}(T)y,u\right\rangle=0,\forall u\in U\}

\displaystyle=\{y\in V:\left\langle y,Tu\right\rangle=0,\forall u\in U\}=V\cap\operatorname{im}(T)^{\perp}.

The second part now follows from the first part, applying the first part with

T
replaced by
\operatorname{ad}(T)
and using that
\operatorname{ad}(\operatorname{ad}(T))=T
.

For the third part, we must show
\operatorname{im}(T)=T(\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T)))
and
\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T))=\operatorname{ad}(T)(\operatorname{im}(T))
. Since
\operatorname{ad}(\operatorname{ad}(T))=T
, it suffices to prove the first
equality. For this purpose, by the first part, we have that

\operatorname{im}(T)=T(U)=T(\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T))+\ker(T))=T(\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T))),

as needed. For the in particular, note that the above equality directly
implies that
\mathrm{rank}(T)=\dim(\operatorname{im}(T))

=\dim(T(\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T))))\leq\dim(\operatorname{ad}(T))
. Using again that
\operatorname{ad}(\operatorname{ad}(T))=T
, we also have

\mathrm{rank}(\operatorname{ad}(T))\leq\mathrm{rank}(T)
, which proves the desired equality
\mathrm{rank}(T)=\mathrm{rank}(\operatorname{ad}(T))
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.11
.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{ij}=(e^{i})^{\top}T(\Pi_{U}e^{j})
,
\forall i\in[m],j\in[n]
. With
this definition, clearly
\mathbf{M}x=T(\Pi_{U}x)
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and thus

\mathbf{M}u=T(\Pi_{U}u)=T(u)
,
\forall u\in U
. We now check that

\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\top})\subseteq U
. Since
U^{\perp}=\ker(\Pi_{U})\subseteq\ker(\mathbf{M})

by construction, we see that
\operatorname{im}(\mathbf{M}^{\top})=\ker(\mathbf{M})^{\perp}\subseteq(U^{\perp})^{\perp}=U
as needed.

We now show uniqueness: if
\bar{\mathbf{M}}u=T(u)
,
\forall u\in U

and
\operatorname{im}(\bar{\mathbf{M}}^{\top})\subseteq U
, then
\mathbf{M}=\bar{\mathbf{M}}
. Let
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{M}-\bar{\mathbf{M}}
and examine
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i}
for
i\in[m]
. Since
\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i}=\mathbf{M}^{\top}e^{i}-\bar{\mathbf{M}}^{\top}e^{i}\in U
, we have
\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i}=\mathbf{M}\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i}-\bar{\mathbf{M}}\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i}=T(\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i})-T(\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i})=\mathbf{0}_{m}
. In
particular,
0=(e^{i})^{\top}\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i}=\left\lVert\mathbf{D}^{\top}e_{i}\right\rVert^{2}
, and thus

\mathbf{D}^{\top}e^{i}=\mathbf{0}_{n}
,
\forall i\in[m]
. In particular,
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{0}_{m\times n}
and uniqueness follows. Therefore,
\mathbf{M}=\mathcal{M}(T)
, as needed.

The equality
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{M}(T))={\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|^{\mathbb{R}^{m}}_{U}}\circ{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{U}\vphantom{\big|}\right|^{U}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}
follows directly from the above construction, recalling that
\mathcal{M}(T)(x)=T(\Pi_{U}(x))
,
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We now prove the furthermore. Since
\operatorname{im}(\mathcal{M}(T)^{\top})\subseteq U
by
definition, we have
U^{\perp}\subseteq\ker(\mathcal{M}(T))
, and hence
\operatorname{im}(\mathcal{M}(T))=\mathcal{M}(T)(U+U^{\perp})=\mathcal{M}(T)(U)=T(U)=\operatorname{im}(T)
, where the
second to last equality follows by definition of
\mathcal{M}(T)
. By

Proposition

2.7
, we have
\mathbb{R}^{m}=\ker(\mathcal{M}(T)^{\top})+\operatorname{im}(\mathcal{M}(T))=\ker(\mathcal{M}(T)^{\top})+\operatorname{im}(T)
. In particular,
\operatorname{im}(\mathcal{M}(T)^{\top})=\mathcal{M}(T)^{\top}(\ker(\mathcal{M}(T)^{\top})+\operatorname{im}(T))=\mathcal{M}(T)^{\top}(\operatorname{im}(T))=\operatorname{ad}(T)(\operatorname{im}(T))
, where the last equality follows by
Remark

2.6
and

\operatorname{im}(T)\subseteq V
. Since
\ker(\operatorname{ad}(T))+\operatorname{im}(T)=V
by

Proposition

2.7
, we similarly get that
\operatorname{ad}(T)(\operatorname{im}(T))=\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{ad}(T))
,
as needed.
∎

A.1
Approximate Singular Subspaces

We now give the proof of
Lemma

2.26
, which relates the approximate
singular subspaces of an operator to those of its associated matrix.

Proof of
Lemma

2.26
.

By
Proposition

2.11
, we have that
\bar{T}(x)=T(\Pi_{X}(x))
,

\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Therefore, for all
x\in X^{\perp}
we have
\bar{T}(x)=T(\Pi_{X}(x))=T(\mathbf{0}_{n})=\mathbf{0}_{m}
, and hence
X^{\perp}\subseteq\ker(\bar{T})
.

We begin by proving (
1
). Firstly,
\ker(T)=\ker(\bar{T})\cap X\subseteq S\cap X
by our assumption that
\ker(\bar{T})\subseteq S
, which proves the first
inclusion. Using that
X^{\perp}\subseteq\ker(\bar{T})\subseteq S
and
orthogonal decomposition, we get the desired first equality

\Pi_{X}(S)=X\cap(S+X^{\perp})=X\cap S.

We now claim that
(S\cap X)+X^{\perp}=S
. Using orthogonal decomposition
again, this follows by

S\subseteq\Pi_{X}(S)+X^{\perp}=(S\cap X)+X^{\perp}\subseteq S+S=S.

(127)

Using the above and
X^{\perp}\subseteq\ker(\bar{T})
, we get
that
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\cap X}})=\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\bar{T}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{(S\cap X)+X^{\perp}}})
via the same argument as in
Remark

2.23
.
This yields
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\cap X}})=\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\bar{T}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})

by (
127
).

We now prove (
2
). Firstly, using that
\mathbb{R}^{n}=X+X^{\perp}
and
S=(S\cap X)+X^{\perp}
, we have
n=\dim(X)+\dim(X^{\perp})
and
\dim(S)=\dim(S\cap X)+\dim(X^{\perp})
. In
particular,

n-\dim(S)+1=\dim(X)-\dim(S\cap X)+1.

(128)

Therefore, using
\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\cap X}})=\sigma_{1}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\bar{T}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})
from part (
1
), we get
that
S
is a
\varrho
-approximate singular subspace for
\bar{T}
of
dimension
d\geq 0
if and only if
S\cap X
is a
\varrho
-approximate
singular subspace for
T
of dimension
d-\dim(X^{\perp})
.

We now prove the moreover. By
Proposition

2.19
, recall that

\sigma^{+}(T)=\sigma^{+}(\mathcal{M}(T))

=\sigma^{+}(\bar{T})
. Thus,

\sigma_{[\dim(X)]}(T)=\sigma_{[\dim(X)]}(\bar{T})
and
\sigma_{[n]\setminus[\dim(X)]}(\bar{T})=\mathbf{0}_{[n]\setminus[\dim(X)]}
. Therefore, for
\tau\geq 0
,

{\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(\bar{T},\tau\right)={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(T,\tau\right)+(n-\dim(X))={\mathcal{C}}_{\sigma}\left(T,\tau\right)+\dim(X^{\perp}).

∎

We now move on to the proof of
Lemma

2.27
, which shows that
the orthogonal complement of an approximate singular subspace is an
approximate maximizer of (
16
) whenever there is a large
enough gap in the singular values. The proof will require the following two
helper lemmas.

Lemma A.1

(Subspace Projection Adjoints)
.

Let
S,U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be subspaces. Then,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{U}\vphantom{\big|}\right|^{U}_{S}}=\operatorname{ad}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{S}\vphantom{\big|}\right|^{S}_{U}})
. In particular,
\sigma^{+}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{U}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})=\sigma^{+}({\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{S}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U}})
and if
\dim(U)=\dim(S)
, then
\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{U}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}})=\sigma({\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{S}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U}})
.

Proof.

Let
s\in S
and
u\in U
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\langle s,{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{S}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U}}(u)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle s,\Pi_{S}(u)\right\rangle=\left\langle\Pi_{S}(s),u\right\rangle=\left\langle s,u\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle s,\Pi_{U}(u)\right\rangle=\left\langle\Pi_{U}(s),u\right\rangle=\left\langle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{U}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S}}(s),u\right\rangle\,.

This proves that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{U}\vphantom{\big|}\right|^{U}_{S}}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Pi_{S}\vphantom{\big|}\right|^{S}_{U}}
are adjoints of one another. The other statements follow from this and
Proposition

2.18
.
∎

Lemma A.2

(Singular Subspace Proximity)
.

Let
T\colon X\rightarrow Y
,
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n},Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a linear operator with singular value decomposition
\mathcal{M}(T)=\sum_{i=1}^{\mathrm{rank}(T)}\sigma_{i}(T)y_{i}x_{i}^{\top}
as in
Definition

2.16
. Let
S\subseteq X
be a

\varrho
-approximate singular subspace for
T
with
\dim(\ker(T))\leq\dim(S)<\dim(X)
and let
U=\operatorname{span}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)})^{\perp}\cap X
. Then, for all
s\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
we have that

1\geq\frac{\|\Pi_{U}(s)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|s\|_{2}^{2}}\geq 1-\varrho^{2}\frac{\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)+1}(T)^{2}}{\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}}\,.

Proof.

The first inequality follows from properties of projections. We now prove the
second inequality. Recalling that
\mathrm{rank}(T)+\dim(\ker(T))=\dim(X)
and
using that
\dim(\ker(T))\leq\dim(S)<\dim(X)
, we have
1\leq\dim(X)-\dim(S)\leq\mathrm{rank}(T)
. Let
\bar{U}=\operatorname{span}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)})=U^{\perp}\cap X
. Note that
T(\bar{U})=\operatorname{span}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)})\perp\operatorname{span}(y_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)+1},\ldots,y_{\mathrm{rank}(T)})=T(U)
. Therefore, for any
s\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}_{n}\}
we have that

\displaystyle\varrho^{2}\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)+1}(T)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\|T(s)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|s\|_{2}^{2}}=\frac{\|T(\Pi_{U}(s))\|_{2}^{2}+\|T(\Pi_{\bar{U}}(s))\|_{2}^{2}}{\|s\|_{2}^{2}}

(129)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\bar{U}}})^{2}\frac{\|\Pi_{\bar{U}}(s)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|s\|_{2}^{2}}=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}\frac{\|\Pi_{\bar{U}}(s)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|s\|_{2}^{2}}\,.

Noting that
\|s\|_{2}^{2}=\|\Pi_{U}(s)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\Pi_{\bar{U}}(s)\|_{2}^{2}
and
reordering the terms gives the result, where the condition
\dim(X)>\dim(S)\geq\dim(\ker(T))
ensures that
\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)

\geq\sigma_{\mathrm{rank}(T)}(T)>0
.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

2.27
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.27
.

If
\dim(S)<\dim(\ker(T))
, then
\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)\leq

\sigma_{\mathrm{rank}(T)+1}(T)=0
and there is nothing to prove. So assume
\dim(S)\geq\dim(\ker(T))
. Let
U
be as in
Lemma

A.2
. Recall that
\bar{U}=U^{\perp}\cap X
satisfies
T(U)\perp T(\bar{U})
and

\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\bar{U}}})=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)
by construction. Furthermore,
\dim(S)=\dim(U)
and
\dim(\bar{S})=\dim(\bar{U})=\dim(X)-\dim(S)
. From here, we get that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\bar{S}}})^{2}

\displaystyle=\min_{\bar{s}\in\bar{S}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|T(\bar{s})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\bar{s}\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\bar{s}\in\bar{S}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|T(\Pi_{\bar{U}}(\bar{s}))\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\bar{s}\|_{2}^{2}}\hskip 130.0002pt\left(\text{by $T(U)\perp T(\bar{U})$}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}\min_{\bar{s}\in\bar{S}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\Pi_{\bar{U}}(\bar{s})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\bar{s}\|_{2}^{2}}\quad\left(\text{as $\sigma_{\min}({\left.\kern-1.2ptT\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\bar{U}}})=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)$}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}\left(1-\max_{\bar{s}\in\bar{S}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\Pi_{U}(\bar{s})\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\bar{s}\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\quad\left(\text{as $S\subseteq U+\bar{U}$ and $U\perp\bar{U}$}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}\left(1-\max_{u\in U\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\Pi_{\bar{S}}(u)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|u\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\hskip 35.00005pt\left(\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subspace-projection-adjoint}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}\min_{u\in U\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\Pi_{S}(u)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|u\|_{2}^{2}}\hskip 26.00009pt\left(\text{as $U\subseteq S+\bar{S}$ and $S\perp\bar{S}$}\right)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}\min_{s\in S\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\frac{\|\Pi_{U}(s)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|s\|_{2}^{2}}\hskip 70.0001pt\left(\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subspace-projection-adjoint}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}\left(1-\varrho^{2}\frac{\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)+1}(T)^{2}}{\sigma_{\dim(X)-\dim(S)}(T)^{2}}\right)\,.\,\left(\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:subspace-proximity}}\right)

This proves the lemma.
∎
